#10371. 7 E-commerce UX Tips for Selling Car Accessories and Home & Kitchen Products Online

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Here is a question a product page should answer in about five seconds: “Will this magnetic phone mount hold my phone with the case still on?” When the answer is hard to find, shoppers tend to open another tab instead of contacting support.

Stores that sell both car accessories and home & kitchen products face an extra challenge: two very different catalogs share one site. A car accessories buyer cares about fit, mounting and compatibility. A home & kitchen buyer cares about size, capacity and how easy the product is to clean. A single generic product page frustrates both.

This guide covers seven practical e-commerce UX tips that designers and developers can apply right away, with code snippets and a checklist at the end.

A car mount and a vegetable peeler should not share the same spec table. Keep a single reusable product page component and change only the fields based on category. The layout stays consistent, while each shopper sees the details they need.

In code, this can be a config object that your template reads from:

Showing “Not specified” instead of hiding a missing field is a small habit with a real benefit: it makes gaps in your product data visible to your own team.

Shoppers look for deal-breaker information near the price and the purchase button: fit, size and compatibility. Do not bury it in an “Additional information” tab halfway down the page.

A short comparison table also works better than three paragraphs of marketing copy, because shoppers scan rather than read. Baymard Institute’s product page research consistently finds that unclear or hard-to-find specifications are a common reason shoppers hesitate.

A responsive layout is the starting point, not the finish line. People compare car accessories in a parking lot and browse kitchen gadgets while standing in the kitchen, often with one thumb.

A phone mount on a white background says nothing about how it attaches to a dashboard. Show it in a real car, from the driver’s point of view. For home & kitchen products, place the item next to something familiar, like a coffee mug, so people can judge its size.

Good alt text matters for accessibility and for image search. Compare “phone mount” with “Magnetic phone mount fixed to a car dashboard, holding a phone in landscape mode”. The W3C alt text decision tree is a useful guide for deciding what to describe.

Keep images fast, too. Responsive sources with srcset and sizes, explicit dimensions and lazy loading help prevent layout shift and slow pages:

One caveat: do not lazy-load the main product image at the top of the page. It is usually the Largest Contentful Paint element, so web.dev advises loading it eagerly.

Shoppers often search for a problem rather than a product. Few people type “seat gap filler” the first time; they type “stuff falling between car seats”. Your search, synonyms and category names should handle both.

Mixed catalogs show why this matters. Stores that cover both vehicle and household products, such as VNSPORT.org with its separate product collections, keep the two audiences apart so that someone looking for a dashboard mount never has to scroll past kitchen tools. The principle applies to any store with more than one audience: group by the shopper’s intent, not by your supplier list.

Decorative “100% secure” badges do little. What builds trust is information shoppers can check for themselves:

At checkout, show the full cost, including shipping and taxes, before payment, let people fix mistakes easily and offer guest checkout. In Baymard’s checkout usability research, unexpected extra costs and forced account creation are among the top reasons shoppers abandon a cart.

If your support inbox gets the same question every week, the product page has a gap. Answer it on the page, close to where the decision happens, instead of on a generic help page three clicks away.

You can mark up those answers with FAQPage structured data:

A reality check: since 2023, Google shows FAQ rich results only for well-known government and health websites. Do not add FAQs just to chase a snippet. Add them because they answer real questions and help shoppers decide.

What are the most important e-commerce UX tips for a car accessories store? Show compatibility and mounting details next to the price, use in-car photos, and make sure the mobile layout works with one hand.

How should product pages differ for car accessories and home & kitchen products? Use the same layout but different spec fields. Car accessories need fit, mounting and power details. Home & kitchen products need dimensions, capacity, materials and cleaning instructions.

How big should buttons be on a mobile product page? WCAG 2.2 sets a minimum target size of 24 × 24 CSS pixels. For primary actions such as “Add to cart”, around 44 pixels is more comfortable.

Do FAQ sections still help SEO? Mostly indirectly. FAQ rich results are now limited, but clear on-page answers can match long-tail searches, reduce returns and keep shoppers on the page.

How can I reduce cart abandonment? Show total costs early, offer guest checkout, display delivery times and return terms before payment, and keep the checkout form short.

Good e-commerce UX is not about clever animations. It is about answering the shopper’s question before they have to ask it. Clear specs, honest photos, thumb-friendly controls and real FAQs turn an ordinary product page into one that helps people buy, whether they are shopping for car accessories or home & kitchen essentials.

#10370. Orbiting ESA satellite plays Doom after receiving voice commands from amateur radio operators on Earth

An Oslo rooftop, about 80 watts, and a hand-aimed antenna were enough to reach orbit.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

An Oslo radio amateur group, working with Tanagra Space, got a European Space Agency satellite to recognize and launch the 1993 game Doom after catching the word in a voice command sent over the 23 cm band. It took seven attempts, shared with a group in Poland, and the command finally got through on the last two, on the same day in late July. The satellite “understood its assignment,” with the help of a matcher after the 27 MB onboard speech model transcribed the audio, returning a postcard with details of the launch.

The satellite, named OPS-SAT PRETTY and flying under the ESA’s OPS-SAT Space Lab program for outside experiments, ran the game in an experiment punningly called PRETTY DOOMed. Doom ran on a different satellite, the OPS-SAT-1, in 2023, but this time is different, as the game was launched with a voice command.

The radio club sending the command in question is LA4O, of the Norwegian Radio Relay League, hams transmitting as part of the experiment. The club’s signal went over 1296 MHz narrowband FM at about 80 W, indicated, using an Icom IC-9700 radio and a Wimo helix antenna at 15.1 dBi, directed by hand. As for the voice, Run 6, the morning pass, used a looped synthesized voice from a keyer, with three of six captures detecting the command. Run 7, the evening pass, used a live operator at a microphone, with one of five usable captures detecting it.

The live operator’s command was “LIMA ALFA FOUR OSCAR, PRETTY PLAY DOOM DOOM DOOM, PRETTY PLAY DOOM DOOM DOOM.” The call sign is for legal identification.

On board the satellite, the voice is transcribed by a 27 MB quantized English speech-recognition model, picked because it could fit the satellite’s limited computer. The voice was handled by software-defined radio (SDR), which was able to narrow in on the voice and demodulate the source for the speech model. Tuning and demodulating a signal is usually handled by fixed circuits, but using software has the advantage of being mutable with an uploaded update. The voice used this time “was only the test signal,” project lead Georges Labrèche said, and the same setup could handle other things with a new model.

The live-voice transcript caught “AND FAR BRIEFLY PLAYING DOOM STEAM DOOM PRECIPATED” with two exact DOOM matches, although one DOOM is enough. Morning detections passed only on fuzzy tolerance with transcripts such as “PLAY DOON.” The achievement is less on the understood side and more about differentiating a voice and a specific command out of the noise in orbit.

This can be an iffy business, which is one reason it took seven runs, and being able to update proved useful. The spacecraft heard LA4O, but the output was mostly static, because about 170 kHz reached the FM discriminator for a signal of about ±5 kHz. Offline analysis brought a fix: find the peak within ±100 kHz, shift to baseband, low-pass, and decimate before demodulation. It’s effectively like tuning a car radio to a specific station.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Tanagra then reproduced the fix and was able to remove a redundant audio filter. Delivery of the fix took about a week, although the Oslo group’s effort took 59 days and about 7,200 NOK (about $770), pizza included.

Tanagra proposes repeat runs that keep the original data and send it back down to Earth, so the researchers can test the live-voice result properly. Better detection may be possible with larger speech models, too, which the team could test on the ground first. Labrèche says the team has demonstrated “a satellite that can listen to any type of radio signal.” Hopefully, the setup can one day detect “mayday” calls and even radio bursts from space.

#10367. Android tablets are back, but are they for you?

Android tablets have always been a bit of a touchy subject, but it’s abundantly clear that the form factor is making a comeback. But, as someone who’s a fan of the smaller sizes, are you actually using the gargantuan Android tablets that every company seems obsessed with? In other words – are you the customer for the Android tablets these brands are selling?

9to5Google Weekender by Ben Schoon is a part of our free newsletter that also includes ‘Inbox,’ a twice-weekly roundup of key stories from around the web by Daniel Bader. Sign up here to get Weekender delivered to your inbox every Sunday along with ‘Inbox’ on Tuesday and Thursday. You can find past issues here.

For a while there, Android tablets had hit what felt like a dead end. Bigger smartphones and cooling of the tablet market as a whole, along with the general sentiment that Apple was doing a way better job with the iPad anyway, left the category in a place that felt stagnant – and that’s the nice way of putting it.

But, over the past few years, Android tablets have had a renaissance.

New form factors, big releases from a variety of brands, and flagship-tier devices left and right. Despite Android’s push into foldables and still-huge displays on nearly every smartphone, tablets are very much back. Prior to RAMageddon’s worst impacts, tablet sales were growing for nearly everyone, and Android brands were seeing a larger slice of the pie.

Anyway, this is on my mind right now because I’ve had a lot of Android tablets arrive in my office in the past few weeks, capping off a lot of tablet announcements in the past few months. I just finished up with Redmagic Astra 2, a tablet I think fits the description of “Android’s iPad Mini OLED,” and I’m in the process of moving into tablets from Lenovo and Amazon. Our Jordan Floyd has also been testing out the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra behind the scenes. And if there’s one trend amongst recent tablet releases, it’s that big tablets seem to be the primary focus among a lot of brands.

Take Amazon’s new Google Play-equipped Alexa Tablet lineup, for example.

The Alexa Tablet 12 is the crown jewel of the lineup, packing the most powerful specs and a premium anti-glare display. It’s the only one that gets a “Pro” moniker, while the 11-inch and 8-inch models are noticeable steps down. Unboxing the Alexa Tablet 8 this week, for example, I was immediately reminded this is not a premium device – the screen makes that clear without any hesitation whatsoever. It was a case of whiplash after setting up the Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 with its 4K display.

It’s a weird one for me. I’ve never been a big fan of these super-sized Android tablets (or iPads, for that matter). I can get behind a 10-inch or 11-inch model, but I find myself more at home on the smaller slates. They’re fantastic for travel as an alternative to draining my phone’s battery, while I find myself just reaching for a laptop instead if I want a bigger screen. A big tablet, regardless of specs, can rarely replace a proper laptop for me, so lugging both around just sounds like more trouble than it’s worth.

But, clearly, people are buying these bigger slates. Samsung appears to be seeing enough success with its mammoth Galaxy Tab S Ultra lineup to keep the 14.6-inch tablet around each year while the smaller sizes are now on an every-other-year cycle. Lenovo’s lineup is virtually all underpowered when the screen size goes down, aside from the Legion Tab that’s now $900.

Are you buying these bigger tablets? What are you using them for?

I imagine that most folks opting for a bigger tablet are using these in the place of a laptop. Thinner and lighter, longer battery life, etc. And in cases like the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro and its $550~ price tag, I get the appeal. Lenovo Yoga Tab Plus Gen 2 takes that down a notch for me at $799 – you’re getting into laptop territory there – but it still is a compelling-enough alternative if it fits your needs. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra at $1,400 plus a $200 keyboard, yeah I don’t get that one at all.

Let’s discuss! I want to know if you’re using an Android tablet, what you’re using it for, and what size you actually prefer.

Ladies and gents, we’ve finally got a new flagship in the US. Motorola Signature 27 is launching on November 19 and looks excellent, though its flashy spec sheet won’t come cheap at $1,599.

Finally, something good came out of Googlebook

Googlebook is a disaster, but there’s finally one good thing coming out of it in Henry Cavill’s new “Mustache Protocol” game which, supposedly, was built on Googlebook. I’ll bite my tongue there, but the accompanying video brought a needed bit of joy into my week.

I use an 8" Samsung. Not the best in specs, but fine for browsing and reading books. I had a Nexus 10 years ago and gave it to my sister-in-law as I rarely needed something that big. My wife also uses an 8" Samsung, mostly for reading.

#10366. All net-connected vehicles from two major automakers allowed hackers to remotely unlock doors and start the engine

Backend infrastructure failures included hardcoded, user-visible administrator credentials.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Before proceeding further, we'd like to advise our dear reader to sit down and perhaps grab a helmet, for this post will elicit quite a few face-palms and forehead-slaps. Two security researchers joined forces and, during early 2026, managed to hack the vehicle connectivity backend infrastructure of two automakers, managing to remotely open the doors on any of their net-connected cars and start their engines across multiple world regions.

The vulnerabilities were quickly fixed, and they pertained to a handful of belief-begging security issues with multiple server APIs that their respective mobile applications use. Furthermore, although remotely opening the cars and starting them were the most concerning bits, the vulnerabilities also exposed the vehicles' location, mileage, fuel, range, door states, and the customers' personal information.

The white-hat hacking duo isn't disclosing the name of the impacted automakers, but they managed to get access to Automaker #1's U.S. vehicles, while vehicles from Automaker #2 were susceptible to unplanned lock disengagement in Australia, the U.S., and Canada. As far as we can tell by visual inspection alone, the second firm may be Toyota, as the hack demonstration video appears to depict a RAV4 SUV.

Both affected automakers use ForgeRock's authentication and authorization services, though with the one small exception, the vulnerabilities found by Shubham Shah and Sam Curry pertained to poor implementations and security practices. One of their writeups goes over the ForgeRock-related affairs in detail for both companies, while Shah's article discusses the specifics on automaker #2 and their Australian connected-car infrastructure.

Shah first decompiled the company's Android application and found network calls to a telematics subdomain, quickly finding an "admintool" location containing a "TEST HARNESS" page with a login prompt. If you haven't been keeping track by now, the first problem is that this endpoint is reachable from the internet at large. But what really piqued Shah's interest was problem #2: a bundle of JavaScript code that should have been stripped of any unnecessary cruft actually contained the code for the backend's entire functionality, providing a map for further shenanigans.

This alone was enough to grant Shah the ability to pull up customer information with API keys that were hard-coded into said JavaScript. After some careful chaining of requests and connecting a license plate to a VIN with publicly available info, he connected that to a customer and found their email. At this point, the team noticed that the automaker was using ForgeRock's authentication mechanisms, and spent "a couple weeks" finding how they were used.

And this is also where they assess that the easiest path to victory is managing to mint an access token for any user, a task made way easier by the "cursed" JavaScript bundle they found at an endpoint containing hard-coded administrator credentials. These were likely meant for the staging environment, but the duo estimated, correctly, that they could be leveraged nevertheless.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

At this point, they ran into the WAF in front of the service. A Web Application Firewall is similar to a network firewall, but instead of working with IPs and ports, it considers endpoints (URLs) and the requirements to access them. The researchers hypothesize that although it's a good and standard practice, the WAF was put in place because of how bad things were underneath.

And they were right. The app decompilation had revealed a "pinService", and Shah found that it was accessible through the WAF. From thereon, they found "getUser", and correctly guessed that they could retrieve users' information with their email, phone, and ID. Remember those admin credentials? They came in handy, revealing the users' password hashes, but as these were SHA-512, cracking them was unfeasible.

At this point, they let GPT-5.6 Sol Ultra loose to brute-force path names, and found an unprotected one that ultimately let them overwrite any user's password, partially enabling taking over their accounts. However, there was still MFA to contend with.

As it turns out, even that too was misconfigured, as the Australian environment would let users skip MFA entirely. With some judicious token shuffling across endpoints and the mobile app, they eventually could mint access tokens, letting them fully impersonate any user account... and unlock their cars. Then, it was as simple as sending out an API request for the "door-unlock" command, and sure enough, the test car promptly opened.

He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10364. Cyber Vulnerabilities Besiege Italy, Absent Clear Cause

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

Researchers can't quite explain why cyber threats look so different in Italy compared with everywhere else.

To read this article in Italian, click here!

Italy is suffering from a disproportionately large number of cyberattacks, new data suggests. Not only that: The kinds of attacks hitting organizations in the country are vastly different from those hitting other places. Researchers have a triad of theories as to why.

It's true that certain regions and countries face greater cyber-risk thanks to economic, military, and political factors. Israel is a magnet for hacktivists and regional, state-aligned advanced persistent threats (APTs). Brazil's unique history makes it a hive of banking Trojans. The United States economy will always draw the most attention from scammers. It isn't so obvious, though, why Italy is such a popular mark for cybercriminals.

"One of the main reasons why we decided to look into the Italian threat landscape is because Italy kept appearing at the top of our stats in our monthly internal reports," says Gianluca Tiepolo, cybercrime analyst at Intel471. In a report shared exclusively with Dark Reading ahead of its release, Intel471 found that Italy is a total outlier in the global cyber threat landscape. Organizations there are being compromised at a rate beyond what one would reasonably expect, based on the country's size, economy, and politics. And exploits against vulnerabilities and Web infrastructure are far more common in Italy than elsewhere.

Related:L’Italia è afflitta da vulnerabilità informatiche, senza una causa chiara

"We wanted to understand what was driving those stats," Tiepolo says. In the end, though, the data paints a nearly inexplicable picture of what's going on in the land of pasta and Chianti.

Italy is the 25th largest country in the world but, according to this new data, its organizations make up about 7% of all initial access data sold on cybercriminal forums. That's second only to the United States (30%) globally. Italy also ranks fourth among countries most targeted by ransomware.

Far stranger still are the types of cyber intrusions affecting organizations in Italy. According to the new data, initial access brokers (IABs) obtained their initial foothold through vulnerabilities in 57% of cases. Credentials only explained 23% of cases. Meanwhile, IABs have been selling access to victim-owned Web infrastructure in 49% of cases, with remote access and remote desktop tools making up 26% of cases.

So what? Maybe those figures don't pop off the page. Now, though, let's compare them with Intel471's own data from its 2026 Cyber Threat Trends & Outlook report:

Globally in 2025, IABs targeted remote access and remote desktop tools 67% of the time — 41% more often, relatively, than in Italy. Meanwhile, Web infrastructure — by far the most at-risk asset in Italy — represented such a small fraction of Intel471's global data that it didn't even bother to cite it in their report.

Related:Aussies Face Reduced Cybercrime Risk, as Pressure Shifts to SMBs

In the same vein, according to the company's global data, credential-based attacks are eight times more common in general than any other type of attack today. It found that 49% of IAB listings advertised credentials as the attack type, compared to 23% in the Italian-specific data. And guffaw-ingly, whereas in Italy they represent around 57% of all attacks, exploited vulnerabilities showed up in only 6.5% of cases globally.

Though cybersecurity data reporting can be interpreted in different ways depending on the circumstance, Italy does seem like a kind of cyber-risk unicorn, Tiepolo says, adding that Italy has been falling behind on cyber readiness for a long time now.

"I'm Italian," he says. "I've been working with a lot of different Italian clients. What I can say is that Italy is probably slightly behind other European countries, simply based on security technology [adoption]. For example, endpoint detection and response (EDR) technologies became popular in Italy around 2020–2021. Before 2020, most Italian companies didn't use EDR technology. So maybe historically, Italy is a bit behind in actually investing in cybersecurity products and technologies."

Related:Europe Evolves Into Ransomware's Favorite Region

Also, opportunistic cybercriminals are exploiting known, widespread, and often quite old vulnerabilities en masse to scoop up more victims in Italy than in other countries. "The data we saw last week from PHP2Shell, I think, validates this assessment. Italy is the country in Europe with the largest number of PHP 4, 5, and 7 versions, which are end-of-life," Tiepolo notes.

"A full 90% of initial access offers for Italy are what we call 'wholesale' offers," he posits. "They're not offers that are targeting Italy specifically, but simply exploiting vulnerabilities. Our assessment is that Italy is probably slower than other countries in patching their systems."

Another, related possibility is that Italy finds itself victim to a kind of cyber vulnerability network effect. The logic goes something like this: If certain technologies are especially common in a country like Italy, and organizations are slow to address vulnerabilities in those technologies, then that country becomes an attractive economy of scale.

As Intel471 wrote in its report, "Once an actor develops an effective exploitation method, the actor can apply that method to additional exposed systems without conducting a separate, extensive reconnaissance effort against each organization. This reduces the effort required to compromise businesses whose size or profile might otherwise offer limited incentive for a dedicated intrusion."

Researchers couldn't say exactly which technologies, vulnerabilities, or exploitations this theory might refer to, but noted that it does fold into the finding that 90% of initial access offerings to Italian organizations online are sold in bulk.

Without co-signing Intel471's data or conclusions specifically, representatives from the Milan-based cybersecurity firm Cleafy tell Dark Reading that they've observed broadly similar patterns.

"Our threat research does point to a related pattern: attackers reusing tools, infrastructure and techniques to scale campaigns across Italian banking targets," the company wrote via email. It pointed to cases such as the drIBAN Web injection kit, which attackers used to exploit the Certified Electronic Mail (PEC) system used widely across Italy. Cases like drIBAN "don't establish that Italy has a disproportionately large initial-access market, but they do illustrate how reusable attack techniques and infrastructure can help cybercriminals scale operations across multiple targets."

There is one other, potentially significant contributing factor to why Italy looks so different from other countries in cyberattack datasets: its prevalence of small businesses.

Most businesses everywhere are small, but, at least in comparison to other advanced economies, it's especially the case in Italy. A May 2024 "data snapshot for Italy" by McKinsey found that "MSMEs" — micro-, small-, and medium-sized enterprises — contributed roughly 10% more to Italy's economy and employment compared to their relative share in other advanced economies. And last March, the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) published a report that cited that 99.9% of Italian firms in 2023 were SMEs, and 94.8% were micro-enterprises.

"Italy has a lot of small and medium-sized companies," Tiepolo says. "One of the things that came out from the data — especially the extortion data — is that these groups are [disproportionately] targeting small- and medium-sized businesses. So this could be one of the reasons [Italy stands out] in comparison to other countries that have more large companies."

He began his career as a freelancer, ghostwriting Forbes and CNBC op-eds for executives in tech and finance. Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. Throughout those years he co-created a cybersecurity podcast, Malicious Life, which in its day climbed into the Top 20 technology podcasts charts on Apple Podcasts and Spotify.

#10363. Welcome EmbeddingGemma, Google's new efficient embedding model

#10362. Android Auto cutting off older devices, now requires Android 11 or newer

Google has officially dropped support for some older Android versions in Android Auto, now requiring Android 11 or newer to function.

On a support page, Google says that Android 11.0 or newer is now required to use Android Auto:

An Android phone with Android 11.0 (Red Velvet Cake) or higher and an active data plan.

Previously, Google supported Android 9 and Android 10 for the in-car experience, a minimum requirement that itself was raised back in 2024 (but not enforced until 2025).

Some drivers started seeing a “Phone will no longer be supported” message in October 2026, but Google only updated its documentation this week. Android Auto 18.1, the latest app update, still supports Android 10.

#10361. Top 6 AI YouTube Transcript Generators: Features, Accuracy, and Limits

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A one-hour YouTube talk contains roughly nine thousand words, and finding the one point you need can take longer than watching the whole thing. A transcript solves that. With text in hand you can search for a phrase, quote an expert accurately, turn a webinar into a blog post, create subtitles or feed a summary tool.

Plenty of tools now promise instant YouTube transcripts, but they work in different ways. Some read the captions YouTube already has. Others listen to the audio themselves and produce a fresh transcript. That difference affects accuracy, speed and cost more than any feature list does. This guide compares six options, explains how each works and points out where each one falls short. Plans and limits change often, so confirm current details on each vendor's site.

Caption extraction. The tool pulls the captions attached to a video, either written by the creator or generated automatically by YouTube. It is fast and usually free, but it is only as good as those captions. YouTube notes that its automatic captions come from machine learning, so quality may vary, and accents, background noise and overlapping speakers can cause errors. Captions may also be missing entirely for some videos.

Fresh speech recognition. The tool takes the audio and runs its own speech-to-text model. This can be more accurate on difficult recordings and works when no captions exist, but it takes longer and often costs money.

Lynote is built for exactly this job. Paste a YouTube link, search by keyword or add a playlist, and it returns a timestamped transcript with an AI summary. Batch mode accepts up to 10 videos at once. The tool lists transcription in more than 100 languages, translation into 25, auto-chapters, one-click copy and TXT download, with SRT export also mentioned. The page says transcripts are free, with no sign-up required.

Best for: Students, researchers and marketers who want a quick, readable transcript or summary from a public video without installing anything.

Limitations: It extracts YouTube captions, so videos without captions cannot be processed. If the original captions are poor, the transcript will be too. For critical accuracy, review the text or use a service with human verification.

Before you install anything, check what YouTube already gives you. Many videos show a transcript option in the description area, and creators can review and edit automatic captions in YouTube Studio under Subtitles. YouTube offers automatic captions in dozens of languages for long-form videos and Shorts.

Best for: Quick lookups on videos that already have decent captions.

Limitations: Captions may not generate for videos with poor sound, long silences, overlapping speakers, mixed languages or unsupported languages. Export options are minimal, and there is no summary or cleanup.

Notta transcribes uploaded audio and video files and also accepts YouTube links. It lists support for 58 languages, real-time and bilingual transcription, and AI Notes that produce summaries and action items. A free plan includes 120 transcription minutes per month at the time of writing.

Best for: Professionals who handle a mix of recordings, meetings and YouTube videos in one place.

Limitations: The free minutes disappear quickly if you process long videos, and the strongest features sit behind paid plans.

Descript is an audio and video editor built around text. It transcribes recordings automatically, and deleting a word in the transcript removes the matching footage. You can import video, audio or a Zoom recording, and there is a free tier that does not require a credit card.

Best for: Creators who want to clean up their own videos and podcasts, not just read a transcript.

Limitations: It is an editor first, so it is more than you need for a quick YouTube lookup. It works with files you own, so you need the source media rather than a public link.

Rev offers both AI and human transcription. It advertises AI transcripts at 96 percent or better accuracy in minutes, and human transcripts at 99 percent or better, guaranteed within 12 hours. AI transcription covers 37 or more languages, while human transcription is English only. Pay-as-you-go rates start at $0.25 per minute for AI, and human transcription is listed at $1.99 per minute.

Best for: Legal, research and media work where mistakes are costly and a human check is worth paying for.

Limitations: The cost climbs for long videos, and the human option is slower than instant AI tools. Those accuracy figures are the vendor's own claims, so test your own audio.

Whisper is OpenAI's open-source speech recognition model, released under the MIT license. It handles multilingual transcription, translation into English and language identification. You install it with pip and need ffmpeg, and it comes in several model sizes from tiny to large, so you can trade speed against accuracy and hardware demands.

Best for: Developers building their own pipeline and teams with sensitive audio they prefer to keep on their own machines.

Limitations: You supply the audio, which means downloading it yourself and respecting the video owner's rights and YouTube's terms. Larger models need a capable GPU, and there is no built-in interface for non-technical users.

Start with the source. If the video already has good captions, a link-based tool is the fastest route. If the audio is difficult or the stakes are high, pick a tool that runs its own recognition or offers human review. If you are processing hundreds of videos or handling private recordings, look at Whisper or a paid platform with bulk features.

Whatever you choose, treat the transcript as a first draft. Check names, numbers and technical terms against the video before you publish or quote anything, and credit the original creator.

There is no single best transcript generator. Caption-based tools are quick and free, recognition-based tools are better when captions fall short, and human-verified services make sense when accuracy matters most. Try two or three on a video you know well, compare the results and keep a human in the loop for anything you publish.

Can I get a transcript if a video has no captions? Caption-extraction tools cannot. You need a tool that runs its own speech recognition on the audio, such as the options above that transcribe files.

Are AI transcripts accurate enough to publish? Often they are close, but not perfect. Accents, noise and technical terms cause mistakes, so review before publishing.

Is it legal to transcribe someone else's YouTube video? Transcribing for personal research or accessibility is common, but republishing the text may infringe copyright. Check the video's license and YouTube's terms, and get permission when in doubt.

What is the difference between a transcript and subtitles? A transcript is the full text, while subtitles are timed text shown on screen. Many tools can export both, often as SRT files.

#10360. Seagate resurrects hybrid storage: Hands-on with the FireCuda Forge – 8 or 20TB external hard drive requires you to bring your own NVMe SSD

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Seagate wants you to side-load an SSD in its latest external hard drive.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

What's old is new again. Seagate started adding faster flash-based storage to its hard drives in 2010, when solid-state storage was prohibitively expensive. And now that SSDs are stupid-expensive again (thanks, AI hyperscaler demand) the hard drive maker is offering up another hybrid drive, of sorts, with the Firecuda Forge.

Available in 8TB and 20TB capacities, the Forge is an external hard drive that requires that you bring and install your own SSD. That's right, the drive won't work unless you partially disassemble and install your own M.2 2280 SSD, which mounts in the side of the enclosure. 1TB drives with DRAM are recommended. Performance maxes out at PCIe Gen3 drives; newer, faster, and roomier SSDs are supported, but won't improve performance. The Firecuda Forge only has a mounting point for 2280-length drives, and while drives that are less than 1TB are supported, that means less room for frequently used files, so "performance may be reduced."

Interestingly, Seagate sent us a 2TB FireCuda 530R SSD to test with the Forge hard drive, which is both faster (PCIe 4.0) and more spacious than what the company says you should pair with its hard drive. The list of SSD that Seagate has officially qualified to work with the Firecuda Forge is also pretty sparse, including just three other drives: Samsung's 990 Pro, WD/Sandisk SN850X/GX Pro 850X, and the WD/SanDisk SN710/GX7100.

We only received a review sample a few days before launch, and haven't yet had a chance to put the FireCuda Forge through its paces for a full review. But I was impressed that, after I installed the SSD and plugged the drive in, it just showed up in Windows 11 as an 8TB drive. (The company says it also works with macOS 26 and later). There was no software setup involved, and Seagate says you can even remove and upgrade the hard drive to something roomier down the line. Seagate calls the "product architecture" that handles unifying and data handling between the spinning platter drive and the SSD "Alkemi," and says it combines "onboard processing" with the hard drive and the user-supplied SSD to "prioritize active data without requiring users to manually manage separate drives."

Seagate says Alkemi observes workloads and gives priority to currently relevant or often-used data, that it adapts over time to user behavior, and describes it as an "on-device device dynamic caching and workload-aware data-prioritization technology." That sounds pretty similar to the SSD caching used on hybrid drives of the early 2010s, although with up to 1TB of fast SSD storage, you're getting a much bigger fast data pool than those drives of the past, which often made do with 4-16GB.

Interestingly, Seagate says that machine learning is a part of what Alkemi does, but repeatedly points out in its review materials that it is not using generative AI. It's likely the company is worried about customers connecting the many reported instances of LLMs and agents deleting data with this new product. I can understand why Seagate would want to distance its hybrid storage tech from reports of catastrophic data deletion.

Getting past the somewhat odd-feeling aspect of having to crack open an external drive right out of the box and install another drive into it in order for it to work, the process was pretty painless. You have to remove four screws from the bottom (with a tiny included hex-bit driver) to get the outer shell off the FireCuda Forge. From there, the M.2 drive bay is clearly visible on the side. If you want to remove six more hex screws, you can also pop out the hard drive for future upgrades. But for now, we're just going to install an M.2 SSD.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

SSD installation is pretty much the same as putting one of these drives in any laptop or motherboard. Though you'll need a tool; the drive requires a a tiny screw and Seagate does not include a suitable Phillips head screwdriver. The drive bay is somewhat recessed, and there are two thick thermal pads included in the box that should ensure the drive makes contact with both the inner edge of the bay and the external shroud.

This should provide some heat dissipation, but these surfaces are all's plastic, with only a foil layer on the outer shroud to draw some of the drive's heat away from the SSD itself. This is probably fine for most bursty game and creative workloads. But this design does not feel ideal for workloads that hit the drive consistently for hours on end. During our testing, the edge of the drive where the SSD is installed got noticeably warmer than the rest of the enclosure, but it didn't get hot.

In terms of design, the FireCuda Forge looks very similar to the FireCuda X Vault HDD we looked at earlier this year, complete with some gamer-friendly RGB lighting. The primary physical difference is that you'll need the supplied USB-C cable and external power adapter to use the Forge, while the X Vault only has a USB-C port. Presumably, powering both a 3.5-inch hard drive and an M.2 SSD draws too much power for most (or at least some) USB-C ports. I still would love for drives like this to be able to run off of one single cable, though, which was my favorite feature of the X Vault hard drive.

We only received the FireCuda Forge a couple of working days before its announcement. But we were able to run some benchmarks with the Forge drive and a 530R SSD that Seagate provided, and saw some interesting results. The Forge's USB-C connection is USB4, and Seagate's performance claims are up to 2,500 MB/s read and 2,000 MB/s write. That's obviously much faster than a hard drive, but you're also going to be sacrificing the speed of the SSD you're dropping into this drive — especially if it's a PCIe 4.0 SSD.

PCMark 10 is a trace-based benchmark that uses a wide-ranging set of real-world traces from popular applications and everyday tasks to measure the performance of storage devices.

On this synthetic test, Seagate's Forge beats recent traditional hard drives (and the slowest external SSD we've tested, Samsung's T5). But Kingston's fast flash drive does better, as does Sandisk's Desk Drive, which nearly doubles the score of the Forge, which isn't surprising given it's a full-fledged external SSD.

We use the DiskBench storage benchmarking tool to test file transfer performance with a custom 50GB dataset. We copy 4,617 files (images, videos, and software ISO files) to a folder on the test drive (write). Then, after leaving the system idle for five minutes, we run the same test in reverse, moving the test folder to a different location on our PCIe 4.0 testing drive.

Here , we see the Forge drive easily beating the spinning-platter-only drives and Samsung's T5 Evo on writes, only falling to the SanDisk Desk Drive desktop SSD. But on reads, the Forge leads by the pack.

CrystalDiskMark (CDM) is a free and easy-to-run storage benchmarking tool that SSD companies commonly use to assign performance specifications to their products. It gives us insight into how each device handles different file sizes. We run this test at its default settings.

Last up (for now), CrystalDiskMark proves the Force does benefit significantly from its USB4 interface, with its performance on sequential reads and writes jumping well ahead of everything else on our chart — because all other drives here top out at 10 Gbps. There are, of course, faster external SSDs (like Samsung's recent P9). But at 8TB, solid-state options are generally more expensive, and faster external SSDs at 8TB are much more expensive. And of course, 20TB external SSDs aren't really an option, at least in the consumer drive space.

The whole reason a product like the Seagate Forge exists is because SSDs are expensive. The problem is, hard drives have faced similar price jumps due to demand. And Seagate says the 8TB Force will launch at $699 for the 8TB drive, and $899 for the 20TB option. That's a lot of money for a drive you need to also put your own M.2 SSD into for it to function.

We'll also need to do more testing with things like game load times before passing judgment on the FireCuda Forge. But it likely makes much more sense in the 20TB capacity, where no single-drive consumer SSD-based option exists. At 8TB, there are options like SanDisk's Desk drive, which has a slower 10GBps interface, but is a full-fledged SSD, rather than a hard drive with some fast SSD cache. Surprisingly, that Sandisk drive is still on sale for $999. And unless you happen to have an old M.2 SSD lying around to install into the Forge, its price, including the SSD it needs to function, isn't going to be far off traditional solid-state options.

#10358. Meta’s Horizon+ looks to become Meta VR+ and include 3D movies

Meta Horizon+ is rumored to be changing its name to Meta VR+ and may start including movies in addition to games with the subscription price.

Meta’s quest (ahem) to distance itself from the Horizon era is in full swing, and the latest rumor says it’s time for the company’s Game Pass-like subscription to change its brand name. VR leaker Nori posted a screenshot on X showing a handful of 3D movies coming to the Meta VR Plus service. Considering there is no such Meta VR Plus service currently, the obvious conclusion is that Meta Horizon+ is being renamed once again.

Meta Horizon+ currently includes over 100 games with its $7.99 per month subscription and is similar to Microsoft’s Xbox Game Pass service in many ways. Unironically, Meta Horizon+ also includes the basic Xbox Game Pass tier with over 50 Xbox games playable via the Game Pass cloud. Meta offers a 3-month trial with every new Meta Quest purchase.

The new Meta VR+ service is purported to include 3D movies as well, although we don’t yet know if this will be included in the regular $7.99 per month rate, or if Meta will be introducing additional content tiers, as services like Netflix, Disney+, Xbox Game Pass, and others do. Based on the screenshot, these will be big-name movies and are likely positioned to enhance the value of the upcoming $1,300 Meta VR Glasses, although all the same content will work on existing Meta Quest 3 units.

Meta has been slowly distancing itself from the “Horizon” name ever since the beginning of this year, when its VR division got a big reset. In a February blog post, Samantha Ryan, the VP of Content at Meta’s Reality Labs, spoke about the changes to the company’s strategy, including the loss of most in-house VR gaming studios. Since then, several prominent VR developers from Meta have gone on to create independent studios, including the super-popular Supernatural exercise app.

Meta then decoupled the unpopular Horizon Metaverse from its headsets, moving it to mobile-only, and has been removing the “Horizon” name from just about everything since then. Meta Horizon+ looks to be the latest big change, as the company moves toward “Meta VR” branding, which is clearer and separates its VR division from mobile and smart glasses efforts.

#10352. Best Static Website Hosting Providers in 2027: Free and Paid Options Compared

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You've finished building your website. The HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files are ready, everything works locally, and now you need somewhere to publish it.

Static website hosting is one of the simplest ways to get a website online. Many providers offer free plans, and you don't need to maintain a traditional web server.

The harder part is choosing the right provider. Some let you upload a single HTML file and share it immediately. Others connect to GitHub, automatically deploy code changes, and support entire development teams.

We've compared eight static hosting providers, including TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, Vercel, and GitHub Pages. Below, you'll find their free hosting limits, pricing, advantages, and drawbacks, along with recommendations for different types of projects.

Static website hosting is a service that stores and delivers pre-built website files, including HTML, CSS, JavaScript, images, and fonts.

Unlike traditional server-rendered websites, static sites don't need a server to generate each page when someone visits. The HTML is already prepared and can be delivered directly to the visitor's browser.

That doesn't mean static websites can't be interactive. JavaScript can still handle navigation, forms, filtering, and other features. Static sites can also communicate with external APIs or databases.

The main distinction is how pages are generated.

You can write a static website using plain HTML or generate one with frameworks and tools such as Astro, Hugo, Eleventy, or Next.js static export.

Static hosting is also commonly associated with Jamstack, an approach that separates the front end from back-end services.

Lower hosting costs: Many providers let you publish smaller websites for free.

Simpler maintenance: There's no application server or server-side runtime to maintain for the static pages themselves.

Efficient content delivery: Static files can be cached across a content delivery network (CDN), reducing the distance between visitors and website assets.

There are limitations, too. If your website requires user authentication, database operations, or server-side functionality, you'll need additional services or a hosting platform that supports them.

We compared eight providers based on their deployment methods, free plan allowances, pricing, and suitability for different projects.

Free hosting limits: Bandwidth, storage, build allowances, and restrictions.

Deployment process: Whether you can upload files directly or need a Git repository.

Custom domains and HTTPS: Support for publishing under your own domain.

Development features: Automatic deployments, preview environments, and integrations.

Pricing: What happens when a project exceeds its free allowance.

Usage restrictions: Whether free hosting is suitable for commercial projects.

The following table summarizes the main differences between all eight services.

Free plans aren't directly comparable. For example, Vercel restricts its Hobby plan to personal, non-commercial use, while GitHub Pages prohibits using the service as free hosting for an online business or commercial SaaS.

Bandwidth limits also aren't the same as visitor limits. A website serving large images or downloadable files can consume its monthly allowance with relatively few visitors.

Cloudflare Pages is a strong choice for developers who want to host static websites without worrying about a conventional monthly bandwidth cap.

You can connect a GitHub or GitLab repository, and Cloudflare automatically builds and deploys the website when you push changes. Direct uploads are also supported.

A maximum individual file size of 25 MiB.

The combination is useful for blogs, documentation websites, and other projects that primarily serve HTML, CSS, JavaScript, and images.

Cloudflare also provides preview deployments, custom domains, and HTTPS support.

No fixed bandwidth cap for static asset delivery.

Generous free plan for typical static websites.

Monthly build limits can matter for frequently updated projects.

Cloudflare's product range can make the initial setup less straightforward for beginners.

Cloudflare is increasingly emphasizing Workers Static Assets for new projects. Pages remains available, but developers starting a new application should compare both deployment approaches.

For a conventional static website, however, Pages is still a practical option, particularly when traffic could grow beyond the limits of other free plans.

See the official Cloudflare Pages limits for the current allowances.

Netlify combines static website hosting with tools designed to make development and deployment easier.

Connect a Git repository, configure your build settings, and Netlify can publish a new version whenever you push changes.

It also creates deploy previews, allowing you to inspect changes before merging them into your production branch.

This is useful when multiple people work on the same website or when you want to review design changes before publishing.

Netlify supports custom domains, HTTPS, serverless functions, and form handling.

Netlify's free plan uses a credit-based model with 300 credits per month.

Production deployments consume 15 credits each.

Web requests consume 2 credits per 10,000 requests.

Deploy previews and branch deployments consume no deployment credits.

The paid Personal plan starts at $9 per month and includes 1,000 monthly credits.

Convenient Git integration and deploy previews.

Built-in features such as forms and serverless functions.

Suitable for both simple websites and more advanced front-end projects.

Credit-based pricing requires monitoring several types of usage.

A busy site with frequent deployments can consume its free allowance faster than expected.

When comparing Netlify alternatives, Cloudflare Pages is particularly worth considering if your main concern is bandwidth.

For developers choosing between Vercel vs Netlify, the deciding factor is often the framework and workflow. Netlify offers a flexible approach to static site deployment, while Vercel has particularly close integration with Next.js.

Vercel is particularly well suited to Next.js applications, although it also supports React, Vue, Svelte, Astro, and other frameworks.

Like Netlify, Vercel connects to your Git repository and deploys updates automatically.

Every deployment receives its own URL, which helps developers review changes before publishing them to the main website.

Vercel also supports server-side rendering, functions, and other features beyond traditional static site hosting.

There is one important restriction: Vercel Hobby is intended for personal, non-commercial use only.

If you're deploying a business website or another commercial project, you'll need an appropriate paid plan.

Convenient deployment workflow for Next.js.

Supports both static and server-rendered applications.

Paid usage can involve multiple resource-based charges.

If you're building a personal Next.js application, Vercel is an obvious candidate. For a basic HTML website, however, its additional framework features may be unnecessary.

GitHub Pages is a straightforward way to publish a website directly from a GitHub repository.

It works particularly well for developer portfolios, project documentation, open-source websites, and small informational sites.

You can upload HTML, CSS, and JavaScript files to a repository, configure GitHub Pages, and publish them under a github.io subdomain.

GitHub Pages can serve existing static files or build websites using supported workflows, including Jekyll and GitHub Actions.

Published websites have a maximum size of 1 GB.

Bandwidth has a soft limit of 100 GB per month.

Free accounts can publish Pages sites from public repositories.

The bandwidth and build restrictions are soft limits, meaning they aren't necessarily enforced as strict cutoffs.

Simple integration with GitHub repositories.

Well suited to documentation and open-source projects.

Free accounts require a public repository for Pages.

GitHub Pages is not intended for running online businesses, e-commerce websites, or commercial SaaS products.

GitHub Pages is particularly convenient if your code already lives on GitHub and you only need to publish documentation or a portfolio.

For commercial websites, Cloudflare Pages or Netlify may be more appropriate.

The GitHub Pages documentation explains its hosting limits and usage restrictions.

Not every website needs a repository, deployment pipeline, or command-line interface.

If you've created a simple HTML page and want to share it, TiinyHost lets you upload an HTML file or ZIP archive and publish it under a shareable URL.

It can also host PDFs, making it useful for sharing documents and presentations alongside website projects.

The main advantage is the upload process. You don't need to configure a repository or install development tools before publishing.

Sharing an HTML file with someone who doesn't use Git.

The free plan includes one active site, a 3 MB project upload limit, and an allowance of 5,000 monthly visits.

The Tiny plan costs €5 per month when billed monthly. It supports uploads up to 25 MB and includes built-in analytics.

The Solo plan starts at €13 per month when billed monthly and adds features such as custom domains, password protection, and additional projects.

Annual billing is available at lower effective monthly prices.

No Git repository or command line required.

Suitable for previews and smaller projects.

The free plan's 3 MB limit is restrictive for websites with many images or other assets.

Custom domains require the Solo plan or higher.

Upload-based publishing is less convenient than Git automation for frequently updated websites.

For someone who simply needs free HTML hosting to share a small project, TiinyHost is worth considering.

For a larger production website that receives regular updates, Git-based hosting is likely to be more manageable.

Firebase Hosting is Google's hosting service for static websites and web application assets.

It integrates with other Firebase products, including Authentication, Cloud Firestore, and Cloud Functions.

That makes it useful when a website needs static hosting but also relies on Google's back-end services.

Deployment is handled through the Firebase command-line interface.

After configuring your project, you can deploy using:

Firebase distributes website assets through its CDN and supports custom domains with SSL certificates.

The Blaze plan uses pay-as-you-go billing while retaining applicable no-cost allowances.

One detail worth checking is the distinction between Firebase Hosting and Firebase App Hosting. They are separate products with different features and pricing models.

Integrates with Firebase Authentication and Firestore.

Useful for applications already using Google's developer services.

Initial setup is more involved than uploading an HTML file.

The daily transfer allowance may become restrictive for larger websites.

Firebase Hosting makes the most sense when Firebase is already part of your application.

For a standalone static blog or portfolio that doesn't use Firebase services, Cloudflare Pages or GitHub Pages may be simpler.

Amazon S3 and CloudFront offer a different approach to static hosting.

Instead of using a platform that handles the entire deployment process, you combine AWS services to store and deliver your website.

S3 stores the website files, while CloudFront distributes them through a CDN.

This setup provides control over caching, permissions, security, and integration with other AWS services.

It can be suitable for organizations already using AWS or developers who need specific infrastructure configurations.

AWS uses usage-based pricing for S3 storage, requests, and applicable CloudFront usage.

Free allowances may apply depending on your account and the current AWS pricing program, but you should calculate costs based on your expected traffic, storage, and requests.

Unlike simpler hosting services, there isn't one fixed monthly price for every website.

Several services can contribute to the final bill.

Incorrect permissions or origin settings can cause access errors.

One common issue when setting up S3 static website hosting is receiving a 403 Forbidden error because of bucket permissions or CloudFront origin configuration.

AWS recommends using CloudFront Origin Access Control with a regular S3 bucket origin so that the bucket can remain private. This differs from exposing the S3 website endpoint directly, which does not support HTTPS on its own.

AWS S3 and CloudFront are worth considering if you need that level of control. For a small static website, a managed hosting platform usually requires less setup.

Render is a cloud platform that supports static websites alongside web services, databases, background workers, and other application components.

Its static hosting works through a Git repository.

Connect your repository, select the build command and publish directory, and Render automatically deploys your website.

The service includes CDN delivery, custom domains, and managed TLS certificates.

Render is particularly useful when your project includes both a static front end and back-end services that you want to manage through the same platform.

Render's Hobby workspace is free and includes:

500 standard build pipeline minutes per month.

Additional bandwidth is billed at $0.15 per GB under the current pricing structure, so the Hobby workspace should not be treated as unlimited free hosting.

The Pro workspace costs $25 per month, excluding any applicable usage-based compute charges.

Static websites and back-end services on one platform.

The 5 GB included bandwidth allowance is relatively small.

Render makes sense when you're already using it for other parts of an application.

If you only need to host a static HTML website, Cloudflare Pages offers a more generous bandwidth allowance without the additional platform complexity.

Free static website hosting can be sufficient for a personal portfolio, documentation site, prototype, or small informational website.

However, the restrictions vary considerably between providers.

Some hosts measure data transfer, while others use visitor counts or a credit system. Check how each provider calculates usage rather than comparing headline numbers alone.

If your website rebuilds whenever you publish content, deployment limits matter. A blog updated a few times a month has different requirements from an application receiving dozens of deployments every day.

Free hosting doesn't always mean you can run a business website. Vercel's Hobby plan and GitHub Pages have restrictions that may rule out certain commercial projects.

Some services include custom domains on free plans. Others require an upgrade before you can connect one.

A free plan might stop serving additional usage, while another provider may bill for overages.

Before publishing a production website, check the provider's billing behavior and configure spending controls where available.

The right service depends primarily on how you build your website and what you intend to do with it.

For most developers publishing a conventional static website, Cloudflare Pages is a reasonable starting point.

If you're working with Git and want convenient previews and deployment tools, Netlify is another good choice.

For small files, temporary demos, and client previews, a simpler upload-based service may save time.

You should also consider how often the website will change.

Uploading files manually may be fine for a page that rarely changes. For a website with regular content updates, automated deployments from Git will generally be easier to maintain.

Publishing your website is only the first step. If you want search engines to discover and understand your pages, check the technical SEO basics before sharing the URL.

Every important page should have a descriptive HTML title and a relevant meta description.

You can use the Meta Tags Generator to create the necessary markup.

Open Graph tags help control how a page appears when shared on platforms that support them.

Set the title, description, canonical page URL where appropriate, and preview image.

A robots.txt file tells compliant crawlers which paths they may access. It doesn't guarantee indexing or prevent a public page from appearing in search results.

Use the Robots.txt Generator to create your crawler rules.

Include a sitemap with the canonical URLs you want search engines to discover.

Structured data helps search engines interpret eligible content.

Depending on the website, you may want Organization, Article, BreadcrumbList, or other relevant schema types.

The JSON-LD Schema Generator can help generate markup.

Confirm the correct domain loads over HTTPS.

Check canonical tags and redirect behavior.

Make sure important pages don't contain unintended noindex directives.

Test page performance and Core Web Vitals.

Verify the website in Google Search Console.

Static hosting makes serving website files relatively straightforward, but technical SEO still requires configuration and testing.

Cloudflare Pages is a good general-purpose choice because of its free static asset bandwidth and Git integration. Netlify is well suited to teams that need deploy previews, while GitHub Pages works well for portfolios and documentation. The best option depends on your deployment workflow, traffic requirements, and whether the website is commercial.

Yes. TiinyHost, Cloudflare Pages, Netlify, GitHub Pages, Firebase Hosting, Render, and several other services offer free hosting options. Most free plans have restrictions involving bandwidth, deployments, storage, or usage. Check the terms before using a free plan for a business website.

A small static website can cost nothing to host if it stays within a provider's free allowances. Paid plans vary from fixed monthly subscriptions to usage-based pricing. Domain registration, paid back-end services, and additional storage or bandwidth may increase the total cost.

For a standalone HTML file, upload-based hosting is generally the simplest approach. Services such as TiinyHost let you publish a file without configuring Git or a build process. If the project already lives in a GitHub repository, GitHub Pages or Cloudflare Pages may be more convenient.

GitHub Pages is a sensible option for documentation, open-source projects, and personal portfolios. Netlify offers more deployment features, including preview environments, forms, and functions. It also supports commercial projects, making it more appropriate for some business websites that would fall outside GitHub Pages' permitted uses.

Static website hosting doesn't need to be expensive or complicated. The important part is choosing a service that matches your website and deployment process.

Cloudflare Pages is a good starting point for static websites that need generous free bandwidth. Netlify is useful for Git-based development, while GitHub Pages suits personal projects and documentation.

If you're publishing a small HTML file or sharing a prototype, upload-based hosting may be all you need.

For under $40, you're getting Hall Effect sticks and triggers, along with three different connectivity methods and a charging dock. The GameSir Super Nova wireless controller is compatible for use with PC, Nintendo Switch, iOS, and Android devices. The GameSir Super Nova is fitted with Hall Effect switches for the controller's trigger buttons, and also uses Hall Effect thumbsticks. These magnetic switches reduce physical wear and tear, avoiding the stick-drift issues that have plagued plenty of older controllers. You've also got trigger stops you can engage for a hair-trigger feel, giving you faster response times in FPS games. There's also 6-axis gyroscopic movement that you can use in Nintendo Switch games, along with vibration motors in both handgrips for haptic feedback.

It's possible to remove the magnetic faceplate and swap the ABXY button layout for your own preference, while vibration settings, button mapping, and dead zone adjustment can be made using GameSir's own app.

The $39.99 sale price for this GameSir Super Nova game controller is a good one, and it's the lowest we've seen on Amazon before. Expect this deal to sell out fast, as there's no guarantee that this limited-time deal will stick around for very long.

#10347. Shadowy AI Relay Disables OpenAI Codex, Claude Code Sandboxes

The enigmatic service is providing popular AI coding tools to mainland China and other regions where OpenAI and Anthropic products are not officially for sale — but with key security features turned off.

A mysterious relay service is installing authentic but unauthorized OpenAI Codex and Anthropic Claude Code tools for enterprise users overseas, and making dangerous configuration changes that put those organizations at risk.

The AI relay, first spotted by the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, appears to be designed to bypass regional restrictions that prevent certain countries and regions from using AI tools and platforms of US companies. According to Symantec's report published Thursday, the relay service installs genuine versions of the command-line interfaces (CLIs) for Codex and Claude Code on enterprise systems, but reconfigures them to route requests through the relay's infrastructure rather than OpenAI's or Anthropic's networks.

On the surface, this appears to be the case of a gray market provider or unauthorized reseller providing the AI tools to markets where they are not officially sold. Symantec researchers noted that the service, which has been active since early 2026, has installed the tools on systems at a dozen organizations, primarily in mainland China but also in South Korea, Taiwan, and Malaysia.

Related:Researchers Infiltrate Microsoft Tech Support Scam Operation

But the relay service also disables security safeguards for the tools, and the research team said it's unclear why it's being done (no malware or malicious activity has been observed thus far). One thing is for certain, according to Symantec: the relay service is creating enormous cyber-risk for the organizations using it.

Brigid O Gorman, senior intelligence analyst at the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, tells Dark Reading that researchers found a suspicious PowerShell downloader that they later determined to be "an AI-agent hijack script." After investigating further, the team found similar activity on other networks and traced it to the unauthorized relay service.

According to Symantec's report, when a user of the AI relay installs a legitimate Codex or Claude Code CLI through npm, the fileless PowerShell or bash script is fetched from "a lookalike domain by a one-line command that the user appears to run themselves. While this evokes the specter of attack techniques like ClickFix and InstallFix, the research team noted that the CLI clients aren't modified or Trojanized in any way.

And yet, the tools perform suspicious actions that heighten risks for enterprises. For example, the reconfigurations turn off the CLIs' execution sandbox, which is a key security feature because it limits what the AI coding tools can do.

Related:F-Secure Launches Victim-First Scam Encyclopedia Covering 50+ Types of Consumer Fraud

Additionally, the relay service enables network access for the write sandbox, turns off telemetry and error reporting features, and replaces the user's API credentials with relay-issued tokens. In one case observed by the Symantec Threat Hunter Team, the script logged a user out of their Anthropic session and deleted their authentication variables before reissuing credentials controlled by the relay service.

"Bypassing the regional restrictions that keep Western AI vendors from selling directly into certain markets is the simplest explanation, but it does not require disabling a CLI's sandbox or establishing persistent credentials that survive a reboot," the report stated.

The relay service activity is something of an enigma. The Symantec Threat Hunter Team said it's unclear why the operators of the client-side relay would need this level of control over users' systems, though the researchers did not observe any evidence of the operators abusing the access. The report noted that the architecture of the service would support malicious exploitation of the access if the operator's intentions were to change.

Furthermore, the Claude Code keys issued by the relay service are formatted with the prefix that Anthropic reserves for subscription-based OAuth tokens, "which is consistent with, though not proof of, a relay pooling flat-rate subscriptions and splitting the capacity across many customers," the team wrote.

Related:AI Slop Isn't Just for Social Media; It's Ruining Cyberattacks, Too

On the other hand, disabling security features hints at a potential fraud operation involving stolen credentials or payment cards, or even the malicious resale of prompts and files that run through the relay infrastructure. Gorman says it's "certainly notable" that the relay disables execution sandboxes and establishes its own persistent credentials.

"We did not see any malicious activity being carried out using these relay services, so any commentary on the potential intended goals of those using it are speculative," Gorman says. "However, a relay in this position can see every prompt, file, and secret an employee sends to the agent, and can alter or fabricate every response it returns. With the sandbox disabled and network access enabled, a malicious or compromised response could execute with the full privileges of the user's account."

Symantec provided indicators of compromise (IoCs) in the report, even though technically speaking there have been no compromises at this stage. "The organizations affected here look less like chosen targets and more like workplaces where individual employees found and used such a service to get access to tools they wanted," the report stated.

Nevertheless, the research team warned that using the service presents serious risk to corporate networks. And while the relay appears to be aimed at regions where Codex and Claude Code tools aren't officially offered, organizations looking for cheaper alternatives and users engaging in shadow AI activity could potentially find their way to such a service.

Prior to becoming a technology journalist in 2000, he worked as a weekly and daily newspaper reporter in Virginia, where he won three Virginia Press Association awards in 1998 and 1999. Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The current feature is a beta and only available for Steam Deck, but it's coming to all users eventually.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Valve is adding framerate telemetry data to games so you can see what your hardware will manage in a given game before you buy it. The new initiative will start with the Steam Deck, but Valve has promised to bring the feature to more hardware in the future, presumably not only its Steam Machine but desktop gaming PCs more generally. The new feature shows players' average FPS on Steam Deck while playing the title, so customers can decide whether to invest without risking a purchase.

Valve already added the option to insert your hardware specifications when you write a review for a game, but an extremely small fraction of players actually write reviews, and from looking at reviews on recent Steam games, it seems like an even smaller fraction actually select to include their hardware specifications, for whatever reason.

The clever among you may have realized "but wait, PC games have settings you can adjust, so even the same hardware might deliver really different performance levels." Correct! And it's also exactly why Valve is doing this. Its Steam Deck Verified program requires a certain level of performance, and games can get marked as "Unsupported" if they don't reach that level, but some people may find them playable regardless. In other words, there's no one perfect standard for "playable performance," and thus Valve wants to let users decide for themselves.

Valve says that, at least for this early trial with Steam Deck data, that data is being filtered to only show data from Decks that are set to the default frame limit. The firm doesn't make any attempt to account for custom in-game settings, yet it says that "despite this, [Valve has] found that the framerate data consolidates around relatively narrow ranges," after accounting for the unusual effects of vertical sync.

So what if you don't have a Steam Deck? Well, Valve says it sees this update (adding the Steam Deck framerate data) as "a stepping stone toward providing framerate data for more devices than just Steam Deck." Obviously, that includes Valve's own Steam Machine mini-desktop and Steam Frame VR headset, but presumably it will extend to folks on standard gaming PCs (whether desktop or laptop)

It's not clear whether desktop performance data will be matched by graphics card, some combination of GPU and CPU, or multiple factors, but either way, there's no question that this is yet another feature that sets Steam as a storefront above its competitors. If you'd like to opt in, you'll have to join the Steam Client Beta for now; Valve says the feature is coming to all users "in the future," though.
