#10342. A Practical Introduction to the Twitch Helix API

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Behind almost every Twitch tool you have ever seen, the stat trackers, the moderation bots, the stream overlays, the alert systems, sits one interface: the Twitch Helix API. Twitch is unusually generous with public data, and Helix is the door to it. If you have ever wondered how third-party sites know who is live, what a channel's viewership looks like, or details a Twitch profile page does not even display, this is the machinery.

This article is a practical overview of Helix for developers who have not used it yet: what it offers, how its authentication works, what people build with it, and where the sharp edges are.

Helix is Twitch's current REST API, served from api.twitch.tv/helix. It replaced the old "Kraken" API, which was fully retired in 2022, so any tutorial still showing Kraken endpoints is a sign to keep scrolling. Helix covers users, streams, channels, games and categories, clips, chat, moderation actions, subscriptions, and events, with JSON in and JSON out.

Two things distinguish it from many platform APIs. First, a large share of it works without any user's permission, because the data is public by Twitch's own definition. Second, it is paired with EventSub, a push system that notifies your application when things happen (a stream goes live, a user follows, a message arrives) instead of forcing you to poll. Most serious Twitch tools use both: Helix for lookups, EventSub for live events.

Authentication is where most newcomers stall, mainly because Twitch offers several OAuth flows and tutorials rarely say which one you actually need. The short version:

Every request needs a Client ID, which you get by registering an application in the Twitch developer console (two-factor authentication on your account is required first). Requests carry the Client ID and a bearer token together; forgetting the Client ID header is the classic mystery 401.

App access tokens come from the client credentials flow, a single server-to-server request with your ID and secret. They have no user attached and that is fine, because public reads, user profiles, live streams, top games, clips, need no user context. For most read-only tools, this is the only flow you ever touch.

Tokens expire, so real applications cache them with their reported lifetime and refresh on a 401 rather than assuming a token lives forever.

A tour of the endpoints that power most tools:

Users. Give the users endpoint a username and it returns the account's public record: display name, profile image, description, Affiliate or Partner status, and the account's exact creation timestamp. That last field is interesting because Twitch does not display it anywhere on a profile, yet it is one of the platform's most useful trust signals, moderators use account age to spot throwaway accounts, and streamers use it to filter giveaway entries. The creation date alone powers a small subcategory of tools: free account age checkers, such as Twitch account age checker, are essentially this one endpoint with a clean page around it, taking any public username and returning the creation date, age, avatar, and broadcaster status. It is a good illustration of how far a single well-chosen Helix field can go.

Streams. The stream's endpoint reports who is live right now, with titles, categories, thumbnail URLs, and viewer counts. This is the backbone of every "is my favorite streamer live" notifier and every directory-style site, and polling it over time is how stat trackers chart viewership history.

Games and categories. Top games, category search, and metadata. Combined with streams data, this is how analytics sites rank categories and how tools answer "how crowded is this category right now."

Clips and videos. Clip metadata, view counts, and creation tools. Highlight compilers and clip-sharing bots live here.

Helix enforces a points-based rate limit per Client ID, and every response tells you where you stand through Ratelimit-Limit, Ratelimit-Remaining, and Ratelimit-Reset headers. Well-behaved tools read those headers and back off before hitting zero; badly behaved ones retry 429s in a loop and wonder why things get worse. For lookup-style tools the practical fix is caching, data like account creation dates never changes, so there is no reason to ask twice.

A few more edges worth knowing before they bite:

Usernames change, IDs do not. Store user IDs, not logins, or your saved data will eventually point at the wrong person.

Missing users are not errors. Looking up a nonexistent username returns a successful response with an empty data array, so handle the empty case explicitly.

Batching exists. The users endpoint takes up to 100 logins in one call, which matters the moment your tool checks lists instead of individuals.

Secrets stay server-side. The client secret identifies your application; calling the token endpoint from browser code hands your app's identity to anyone who opens devtools. Public-facing tools put a small server between the browser and Twitch.

The reason Twitch's third-party ecosystem is so large is that the barrier is so low: one public API, generous free access, and a platform where small questions genuinely matter to people. That is why the space is full of narrow tools that each answer one thing well, when was this account created, is this channel live, how fast is this category growing, which clips popped this week. Most of them are a weekend of work on top of one or two endpoints, and some of them grow into real products.

If you want to start, the path is short: register an application at the developer console, request an app access token, and call the users or streams endpoint for something you actually care about. The official documentation at dev.twitch.tv is current and well maintained, which is not something every platform can claim. Pick one piece of data Twitch collects but does not display, put a clean interface on it, and you are following the same recipe as most of the Twitch tools you already use.

● Check out these PS5 SSD deals at Newegg

One thing worth mentioning, too, is that the PS5's M.2 expansion slot only supports PCIe 4.0 speeds. That doesn't stop you buying a Gen 5 SSD (in fact, in some cases, it might be better value to do so). If you do use a Gen 5 SSD, however, you'll only be able to use it at Gen 4 speeds. These drives also require a heatsink, but you can pick up third-party ones to work with each drive to save costs.

Limitations aside, here are some of the top PS5 SSDs you'll want to consider to get your console ready for the GTA 6 release next week.

This 4TB Samsung 990 Pro SSD will be a massive upgrade to your PS5's storage, giving you plenty of space for GTA 6 and any other game. It's a Gen 4 SSD with read and write speeds of 7,450 and 6,900 MB/s apiece.

This drive doesn't come with a heatsink, so you'll need to pick up a third-party one to ensure the PS5 doesn't throttle your drive's speeds. The alternative is to buy the 990 Pro 4TB with a heatsink, but for $944, you're better off buying a third-party heatsink instead.

The same drive as above, but with a smaller 1TB capacity. Still a healthy upgrade that'll likely double your PS5's storage, and at a cheaper price.

The SN850X has sequential reads and writes of up to 7,300 MB/s and 6,300 MB/s each. This model comes with its own heatsink, so you don't need to supply your own. The non-heatsink model is available for $259.99, however.

The same model as above, but with 2TB of storage. It's around $100 less than the model with the heatsink, however. If you do want the heatsink built-in, you can grab it for $439.99 instead.

Boost to 4TB with this SN850X instead. You'll pay a premium for the heatsink version at $765, so it's worth grabbing this one and getting a third-party model to keep it cool.

The Biwin NV7400 doesn’t have DRAM, but it does have good performance, good power efficiency, and a good warranty with high TBW.

Sequential reads and writes are 7,450 / 6,500 MB/s apiece, but you'll need to add your own heatsink.

The SN7100 is a good, cheap option for a PS5 storage upgrade. It delivers sequential read speeds of up to 7,250 MB/s and sequential write speeds of up to 6,900 MB/s.

No heatsink here, so a third-party one is recommended.

The Samsung 9100 Pro is a high-end PCIe 5.0 SSD featuring Samsung's own Presto controller and 236-layer NAND. The 1 TB model delivers sequential read and write speeds up to 14,700 MB/s and 13,300 MB/s, respectively.

Remember, however, that the PS5's M.2 expansion slot is only Gen 4. This drive is backwards compatible, so it'll work, but you won't be able to hit its top-rated speeds. You'll also need a third-party heatsink with this drive, otherwise the PS5 might throttle your speeds.

As above, but 2TB of storage instead. This will be a sizable increase for your PS5's storage, but as above, you'll need to remember that you won't be able to utilize the 9100 Pro's fastest speeds, even if it does remain fully backwards compatible.

Sony recommends that you install an SSD with a heatsink when you're upgrading your PS5's storage to avoid overheating and throttling.

#10336. Software engineer Rickrolls his Prusa 3D printer via remote code execution exploit

Prusa has patched all the vulnerabilities discovered ahead of a coordinated news release.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Software engineer Yannick Dixken spent a week attacking his Prusa MK4S 3D printer in the name of science. At the end of the relatively short but intense period, he produced a video demonstrating the ability to Rickroll the printer via remote code execution (RCE). Dixken shared the video and findings – charting “roughly 23 individual defects” with Prusa and published his amusing but detailed Never gonna print you up! blog in coordination with the 3D printer maker. Yes, all the vulnerabilities are now patched in the Buddy firmware versions >7.0.0.

Dixken is an experienced Freelance Cloud Architect, DevOps and Platform Engineer. Security research is more of a hobby, he notes in the blog post, but it is clearly a passion strong enough to spend a whole week probing the MK4S for vulnerabilities. The result is far more than the funny old Rickroll. The week of debugging, fuzzing and reverse engineering “armed with Opus 4.8 and a fuzzer” resulted in “14 findings, roughly 23 individual defects, and a chain that ends with the printer's main controller running code I sent it over the network,” noted Dixken. “Yup, fully fledged RCE.”

The headlining RCE scored 8.1 and is officially described as High Severity. This serious bug was in the cloud (or self-hosted) management platform called Prusa Connect. You can see proof-of-concept code Rickrolling the user in Dixken’s embedded video. He sent this video to Prusa.

The methods used to uncover these vulnerabilities are outlined in the source blog, if you’d like to know more. Meanwhile, there were many other vulnerabilities uncovered and cataloged. These include a USB filename attack, heap overflows triggered by malformed encrypted print files, and network denial-of-service flaws. Many of the bugs were probably symptoms of the original developers’ use of assert statements to enforce assumptions.

Happily for Prusa 3D printer users, Dixken has responsibly disclosed this multitude of vulnerabilities. The engineer says that within 24 hours of the Rickroll video being shared on social media, the 3D printing firm’s team coordinated professionally, efficiently, and kindly. Companies don’t always react so positively to those helping them find vulnerabilities in their wares.

LangSmith is closed-source and restricts self-hosting to enterprise licenses, teams that run their own LLMOps stack and want to keep traces on their own servers need an alternative. To find the best fit, we set a baseline by tracing a small TypeScript agent (equipped with two tools) in LangSmith Cloud. Then, we ran the exact same agent through Langfuse, Arize Phoenix, Opik, and AcruxCore using each platform's recommended TypeScript setup. All tests were run on November 5, 2026, and we pulled every trace back through each tool's API to verify the data.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree for the test prompt: a single agent span containing three LLM calls and two tool calls. The real differences between them come down to how much boilerplate code you have to write, how spans are typed, whether costs are tracked, and the overall server footprint.

Why Teams Look for a LangSmith Alternative

LangSmith remains the best choice if you already use LangChain and allow cloud hosting—it required just 25 lines of code in our test. The alternatives below are for teams that don't fit that profile.

To test these platforms, we built a simple customer support agent equipped with two tools:

Both tools use static fixture data to ensure every test run is identical. The agent runs a standard loop: it prompts the model, executes any requested tools, and stops once the model provides a final answer.

We tested the agent with three specific prompts to see how the tracing handled different levels of complexity:

For our comparison, we focused entirely on the third question because it generates a robust, multi-step trace.

Under the hood, the agent uses the standard openai package to call the qwen3.8-flash model. We originally picked this model based on its reliable tool-calling behavior during early probing thought it once skipped a tool call later.

We used LangSmith as our baseline. By tracing the agent there first, we created a checklist of every field it captured, and we judged all the alternatives against that exact list.

For setup, we followed the recommended TypeScript approach from each tool's documentation. Phoenix didn't have specific instructions for using its register() helper with OpenAI in TypeScript, so we adapted the pattern from their Anthropic guide.

Here is the agent with no tracing. Each tool section adds one tracing file that imports these files and changes nothing in them.

LangSmith traces the plain openai package with two helpers. wrapOpenAI records each model call, and traceable records your own functions. Without traceable on the run and the tools, wrapOpenAI alone gave three separate root runs and no tool runs.

This file adds 25 lines and removes 3, and installs one package.

LangSmith showed one root run with five children in call order: LLM, get_order_status, LLM, get_refund_policy, LLM. Every field it recorded became a row of the checklist table under "Results". Cost appeared only after a custom price entry. In our error run the failing tool was marked as an error, but the root stayed success: traceable turned the throw into a rejected promise that our agent did not await, so the agent carried on and answered.

Langfuse self-hosts as six containers, including ClickHouse, a column store built for large scans. In our measurement the stack held 2.60–2.64 GiB of memory at idle. Its Docker Compose guide recommends at least 4 CPU cores and 16 GiB RAM, with about 100 GiB of disk.

The TypeScript path is built on OpenTelemetry. You start the OTel Node SDK with LangfuseSpanProcessor, wrap the client with observeOpenAI, and wrap your own functions with observe:

This file adds 31 lines and removes 5, and installs four packages. We pinned the OTel Node SDK to 0.223.0, because @langfuse/otel 6.1.1 does not accept 0.300.0.

Langfuse successfully recreated the same trace tree, neatly tagging the spans as AGENT, GENERATION, and TOOL. It captured all the essentials: tool calls, tool definitions, and the complete message history. However, for the LLM output, it only saved the assistant's message—dropping the finish_reason, response ID, and the provider name.

We ran our deployment in Langfuse v4's events_only mode, which turns off the v1 trace endpoint. Because of this, the API didn't return a centralized trace object or root-level sums for tokens and costs, even though the UI calculated and displayed them perfectly (we didn't test other modes).

Costs showed up exactly as expected once we added a custom price entry. Since Langfuse calculates prices based on the specific usage types you define, and we only set rates for input and output, the separate reasoning tokens went unpriced. Finally, during our error test, the observe wrapper let the error bubble up to the agent, correctly flagging both the tool and the root span as an ERROR.

Phoenix self-hosts as two containers, one Python server and Postgres. In our measurement it held 0.47 GiB at idle. Phoenix is source-available under the Elastic License 2.0, so it is not open source. Authentication is off until you turn it on.

The TypeScript path uses Phoenix's register() helper and the OpenInference instrumentation for the OpenAI package. OpenInference is a set of OpenTelemetry attribute names for LLM spans. traceAgent and traceTool wrap the run and the tools:

This file adds 32 lines and removes 3, and installs three packages.

Phoenix rebuilt the same tree, typed as AGENT, LLM and TOOL. Each LLM span keeps the full OpenAI response object, plus the messages as flattened attributes. The root span stores no token count, but Phoenix computes a sum for the root and the trace when you read it. Cost and those sums are only in the GraphQL API and the UI, and the REST API has no per-trace endpoint.

Cost appeared after a custom price entry under Settings > Models, and Phoenix priced the reasoning tokens by falling back to the output price. In the error run, the tool span and the root were marked ERROR, and the tool span carried an exception event with the stack trace.

Opik self-hosts as eight containers, including ClickHouse, MySQL and ZooKeeper. In our measurement it held 3.69 GiB at idle, the most of the four. The self-hosted UI opened without a login, so every trace was readable from any machine that could reach the port.

The TypeScript path wraps the client with trackOpenAI and your own functions with track. By default trackOpenAI starts a new trace for every model call, so the file passes the run's span through the documented parent option:

This file adds 33 lines and removes 5. It installs opik-openai 2.2.142, and npm adds opik 1.11.14 as its peer, because opik-openai asks for opik ^1.8.61.

Opik successfully recreated the trace tree, and its trace object neatly tallies token counts across all LLM spans. In the logs, tool arguments are bundled into an array, and the LLM output only retains the assistant's message.

We did notice a quirk with the documented OpenAI wrapper (tested on the npm package opik 1.11.14): it tags model calls as general rather than llm, which incorrectly leaves the llm_span_count at zero. (We didn't test newer Opik core versions to see if this has changed). Additionally, because our synchronous tools execute in under a millisecond, they log the exact same start time as the subsequent LLM call, causing the UI to list the LLM call first out of order.

Cost tracking is entirely manual. Opik showed no costs by default, and the documentation doesn't mention a custom price table. We checked the configuration tabs on our 2.2.52 instance (Feedback definitions, AI Providers, Workspace preferences) and found no model pricing settings. To see costs, you have to manually assign a price to each individual span yourself—only the spans we hardcoded this way displayed a cost.

Finally, error reporting is highly detailed. If something fails, Opik attaches an error_info block—complete with the error type, message, and full traceback—directly to the tool span, the root span, and the overall trace.

AcruxCore self-hosts as five containers: Postgres, Redis, a Node API and worker, and nginx. In our measurement it held 0.39 GiB at idle, the least of the four. Its quickstart leaves sign-up open and ships a committed default GATEWAY_ENCRYPTION_KEY, which encrypts stored provider keys; replace it. We tested a local build from 2026-09-17.

The TypeScript path AcruxCore's docs put first is its gateway. The official openai client keeps working: you point its baseURL at ACRUXCORE_BASE_URL + "/gateway" and call a model registered in AcruxCore, which forwards each call to an OpenAI-compatible provider. The gateway records every model call as an llm span. The SDK's step helpers add the rest: traces.step opens the agent span, traces.wrap wraps each tool, and two headers file the gateway's spans under the agent span:

This file adds 30 lines and removes 5, and installs one package. It builds its own client, so it does not use provider.ts. The gateway refused our team API key, so the client uses a gateway virtual key created in the dashboard.

On this path every model call passes through the AcruxCore server, which none of the other four tools need. If you do not want a proxy in the request path, the SDK's register() helper sends OpenTelemetry spans from your process instead. On that path the step helpers do not join the openai package's spans, so you write the agent and tool spans by hand.

AcruxCore rebuilt the same tree, typed as agent, llm and tool, in call order. Each LLM span keeps the full response, the provider and the cached-token count, and the trace carries a token total. The LLM spans do not keep the tool definitions sent to the model, and no reasoning-token count was recorded. The trace took its name from the question, not from the agent span, and each LLM span is named by its start time.

Cost appeared on every LLM span and as a sum on the trace, from the custom price we set on the model in AcruxCore's model list. In the error run, the step helpers marked the tool span and the agent root as error with the error message, with no status code of ours. The API returned no stack trace.

All five tools accept traces over OTLP, the OpenTelemetry wire format. trace-otel.ts sends the same spans with no vendor package: the OTel Node SDK, the OTLP/HTTP protobuf exporter and the OpenInference OpenAI instrumentation. The exporter reads its endpoint and headers from environment variables:

We ran the two-tool question once per backend and changed only environment variables: the endpoint, the headers, and for Phoenix a resource attribute that sets the project name (without it, spans go to a default project). "Accepted" means the exporter reported success, which it does only for an HTTP 2xx, and we read the spans back through each API.

Opik stored the root and tool spans as general although AGENT and TOOL kinds were sent; the cause is Not tested. LangSmith mapped AGENT to its chain type. If you instrument with plain OTel and OpenInference attributes, your raw spans are not tied to one vendor, but span types, cost and trace views still differ by backend.

The first table covers setup: how much server each tool needs, how much code we changed in the agent, and how cost and the plain OTel exporter worked. Phoenix and AcruxCore had the smallest servers, and AcruxCore needed the fewest lines among the self-hosted tools. Opik was the only one without a price table.

The second table checks each trace against what LangSmith recorded for the same run, one field at a time. All five rebuilt the same tree with tokens and durations. They differ in span types, how much of the LLM output they keep, where token sums live, and how much detail they store for the failing tool.

Token totals for the two-tool question ranged from 1,910 to 2,111. Each tool traced a separate run and reasoning length varies, so these numbers do not compare the tools.

All five tools successfully traced our test agent, so the right choice really comes down to your team's specific constraints, infrastructure preferences, and how tracing fits into the rest of your MLOps tooling.

All four alternatives successfully recreated LangSmith’s trace tree. The real deciding factors are boilerplate code, span typing, cost tracking, and server bloat.

LangSmith remains the simplest option for LangChain users who can utilize the cloud. Otherwise, your choice depends on your priorities: Langfuse for community size, Phoenix for a lightweight Postgres footprint, Opik for strict Apache-2.0 compliance with deep error logging, or AcruxCore for combined tracing and tool governance on minimal hardware. Best of all, if you stick to standard OpenTelemetry spans, you aren't locked in—you can try them all and easily pivot later.

#10332. Threat Modeling With 4 Simple Questions on a White Board

A four-question exercise that CISOs can use to create a threat model that promises results, without a big spend.

ISC2 Security Congress 2026 — Denver — A threat model should be a starting organizing principle for any security program. But rather than investing in a 200-page high-dollar threat model report, one longtime chief information security officer (CISO) argues that "four questions on a white board," can provide any team with an actionable plan forward.

Lora Vaughn, cybersecurity expert, former CISO, and now founder of her own consulting firm, The Vaughn Cyber Group, said that over her many years leading cybersecurity programs, she has developed a wickedly simple four-question threat modeling exercise that can be used in a wide range of organizations, be it the "National Security Agency (NSA) or your friendly neighborhood small-to-medium business (SMB)."

The answers, she said, can then offer a road map for investment decisions. That requires further exploration, she acknowledges, given there's always a new technology right around the corner that promises to drive down the cost of cybersecurity investment in enterprises. And reality must be balanced with industry hype.

Related:AI Gave Your Incident Archive a Second Job

For instance, around 2015, the move to the cloud was supposed to put the onus on providers like AWS to protect enterprises. What came next was a flood of misconfigurations and misunderstandings about who owned which part of security. By 2018, the buzzword was "automation." But teams quickly learned that automating anything required defining buttoned-downed processes first — often a prohibitively time-consuming exercise that pushed many of these project timelines out. By 2021, zero-trust networks were on everyone's mind. But that turned out to be less of a prescription and more of a customizable, guiding principle; and, architecting a true zero-trust environment requires a big investment in systems that would prove to be a much longer journey than many in the sector had hoped.

And regarding the current zeitgeist, handing over enterprise security to an agentic AI model is a "fantasy," according to Vaughn, speaking from the stage while presenting her four-question threat model at ISC2 Strategic Congress in Denver this week.

"AI is a force multiplier," Vaughn said. "It just multiplies what you already have. Zero times anything is still zero. Math is undefeated."

Bottom line: Once the threat modeling is done, choose your path wisely, and with your eyes wide open. And recognize that it's an ongoing cycle. "Refresh the threat model quarterly," Vaughn added. "Or after any big change."

Ask yourself, if you walked into work at 8 a.m. Monday morning, what would be the worst-case scenario(s)? For example, is it a ransomware attack that could result in the leak of sensitive information? What about one that locks up production systems? Mass pwning of firewalls and edge devices? What about a business email compromise (BEC) that results in a $100,000 bank transfer to attackers? Would falling for the North Korean fake worker scheme cause big problems?

Related:What We Missed: Knicks Phishing Test Goes Out of Bounds

Vaughn stressed that once identified, these should be ranked by pain to the business. She added, five to eight "painful" items are a reasonable number to determine.

Vaughn ranks risk in three categories: existential, painful, and annoying. Existential risk means that a breach would close the doors of the business. Painful risk equates to business disruption with associated costs and bad press. Annoying risk is something that can be fixed semi-easily.

A key piece of determining your risk profile is understanding who sees you as a target. Do state-sponsored advanced persistent threat (APT) groups have your organization in their sights? Or is your business most likely to be targeted by BEC scams? Are ransomware affiliates trying to find a way into your systems? Do you have a large inventory of software or appliances that are high on attackers' hit lists? How is your patching hygiene, and do you have bugs that are known to be exploited?

Related:9 Cyber Awareness Tips That Actually Match Today's Maze of Threats

This type of intel is free and available from sources like the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA), but can also come from private threat intelligence services. Vaughn recommended that CISOs and security leaders lean on their vertical Information and Analysis Centers (ISACs) as well.

"That’s your most valuable resource," Vaughn said. "And it's free."

Items that fall between both the "worst-case scenario" and "who's targeting you" lists are where teams need to start first. "Every overlap is a scenario," Vaughn explained. "Write one sentence for each: who, how, against what, and costing what?"

In terms of a BEC attack, Vaughn pointed out a simple phone call verification could be the most effective prevention, and it costs nothing. "Find where the attack stops for the least money," Vaughn advised. "Spend in that order."

Additional free controls that actually work, according to Vaughn, are multifactor authentication (MFA) everywhere, which is something she adds is "non-negotiable" and urges CISOs to "fight for." An asset inventory is also invaluable, even if it's just a spreadsheet, and of course it "beats doing nothing." Patching bugs that have already been exploited, resisting the urge to give everyone admin credentials, and testing backups also make the list. And for ransomware, having backups isn't enough; network segmentation has to go along with it.

"If you can't restore from backups, or you don't have backups, you have hope," Vaughn said. "Hope is not a control."

She pointed out that nothing on this list of controls can be bought from a vendor. "There's no SKU for 'turn on MFA' and 'keep a spreadsheet,'" she added.

It might be tempting for cybersecurity teams to try and tackle these four questions on their own, but Vaughn said that including management in these conversations will help better align priorities to the broader business and will help management understand the potential fallout of not taking care of cybersecurity.

There are some best practices for doing this; for instance, Vaughn recommends linking spend to a business outcome: "If nobody funds a CVE, we stop shipping for a week," is one example she offered.

She also recommends bringing three spend options, with prices attached, and then making a recommendation for one. Additionally, Vaughn said CISOs and cyber teams should never promise "security," only reduced risk, and discuss investment decisions from that lens.

Offer, say, options that cost "$12K, $4K, or zero," she explained. "Zero means we accept the risk, so I recommend the $4K."

And once the decision is made, Vaughn suggested an email follow-up like, "Confirming we’re deferring X and accepting the risk of Y. Tell me if I got that wrong." That paper trail can prove invaluable following a compromise, she said.

#10328. $10 programmer saves BIOS-locked Lenovo ThinkPads from the dump

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Go beyond the headlines with expert reporting on the hardware industry.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

In-depth features, interviews and insider stories from the world of hardware.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Rebooted by Hileeq has shared how others can get cheap Lenovo ThinkPads and save them from becoming e-waste by using a simple BIOS unlock hack that only requires a $10 programmer and another laptop or PC. The technique is niche, known mostly by technicians, but Hileeq is the first to give this bypass mass attention with an easy-to-follow YouTube tutorial.

Rebooted by Hileeq's channel, dedicated to fixing cheap eBay finds, documents the process and what he learned while fixing the laptops. Three weeks ago, he posted a video explaining how to bypass locked Lenovo ThinkPads with Intel 4th- to 8th-generation CPUs using a cheap CH431A programmer to read, write, erase, and save EEPROM and SPI flash memory.

Companies often add supervisor passwords to the fleet of ThinkPads they hand out to their employees as an IT security measure. When these ThinkPads are disposed of, often with the storage removed and destroyed, it’s rare that the supervisor passwords are included and documented. These locked ThinkPads are then incredibly cheap because of the lack of access, ending up as e-waste most of the time. However, Victor Voinea shared a post in a Romanian forum containing his ThinkPad supervisor removal exploits.

Unfortunately, Voinea’s method generated a random password every time the exploit was run, requiring users to contact Voinea for the key specific to the generated hardware ID — and getting the password wasn’t free. However, in 2020, Knucklegrumble posted a free way to bypass the password in the Badcaps electronics repair forum. Mark Juvan, an Ethical Hacker, reverse-engineered Knucklegrumble’s exploit as his cybersecurity master’s thesis at Radboud University Nijmegen, Netherlands, which goes into deeper detail.

The exploit begins by extracting the BIOS from the EEPROM chip on the motherboard using the CH341A programmer and plugging it into a USB port of another PC or laptop. Hileeq then uses NeoProgrammer, a utility for CH341A programmers, and dumps the BIOS, saving a copy of it on the other laptop. NeoProgrammer then applies the same auto-patching workflow Knucklegrumble created.

When the patched BIOS is flashed onto the EEPROM, the laptop can then be turned on, and Hileeq goes through some steps requiring a few keystrokes which remove the password saved in the Embedded Controller (EC), a separate IC that stores security data, among other system tasks. The original BIOS can then be flashed back into the EEPROM, and with the supervisor password removed, the laptop is now usable.

Juvan states in his paper that the exploit takes advantage of a weakness in Lenovo’s firmware verification and driver architecture. Lenovo actually provides the EmulatedEepromDxe driver needed by the exploit, and the driver allows the EEPROM to be read and written to — and the EC’s supervisor password happens to be stored where it’s treated as EEPROM memory, allowing the supervisor password to be overwritten or removed.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The hard, complicated work has already been done by the electronics repair community, and now the process to bypass the supervisor password requires just a few steps and an inexpensive programmer tool. While the method may only be for ThinkPads with Intel 4th- to 8th-generation CPUs, this will still save plenty of usable ThinkPads from going to waste, as Hileeq’s popular post potentially encourages more people to attempt this themselves — who knows, this might cause a domino effect to unlock newer generations of ThinkPads.

#10325. Hunter is killing Google Home, Alexa controls for its smart ceiling fans

Hunter, a major brand for ceiling fans in the US, is pulling the plug on its Google Home and Amazon Alexa bridge for controlling “HunterSMART” models in just a few weeks.

Effective January 1, 2027, Hunter will pull support for the cloud-reliant “HunterSMART” connection used for Google Home and Amazon Alexa controls. Hunter’s app and the included remote will continue to work, but connections to smart home ecosystems will not.

Beginning January 1, 2027, the legacy cloud-to-cloud connections used for app linking between the HunterSMART™ mobile apps and Google Home and/or Amazon Alexa will no longer be supported.

After this date, Google home and Alexa functionality that relies on this legacy cloud connection will no longer work for HunterSMART fans, including voice commands, scenes, and automations.

This bridge is going away in favor of a Matter connection, with Hunter having been upgrading select models to be Matter-compatible over the past year. But this is only available to “eligible” models, not everyone who was already using Hunter’s existing bridge. A list of compatible models is available on Hunter’s website, with firmware updates rolling out via the HunterSMART app.

Some models, such as the one I reviewed a few years ago, require updated hardware. Besides that, the one I have can’t even be re-connected to Wi-Fi to check for an update as Hunter’s Android app consistently crashes during the setup process.

Once the fan has been updated, it won’t be available via the existing link and will instead require re-pairing via your smart home app of choice, whether that’s Google Home or another.

…Hunter is transitioning eligible HunterSMART™ products to Matter, an industry standard designed to support broader smart-home compatibility.

If your fan is eligible for Matter-compatibility, you will be able to continue using all smart functionality within the Google Home and/or Alexa ecosystems after completing the applicable Matter migration & setup.

Once your HunterSMART fan is Matter-enabled, connecting your fan to Google and Alexa will work differently than it does today. Instead of linking your HunterSMART account to Google Home or Alexa, you will need to setup your Matter-enabled fan directly into these compatible ecosystems using the Matter setup available via the Google Home and/or Alexa apps. Matter-enabled devices do not use the legacy cloud linking service for smart controls that is being discontinued.

How can I migrate my HunterSMART fan to Matter?

Depending on your fan model and hardware configuration, a firmware update or updated hardware components may be required for Matter compatibility. Available Matter upgrade options and instructions will vary by product. You can visit our HunterSMART™ Matter resource page for the latest information about Matter compatibility, available upgrade options, and next steps.

#10324. How to Build an Accessible Customer Reviews Section with HTML and CSS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Customer reviews are often displayed on business websites, but presenting them effectively requires more than adding a few star icons and customer quotes. Developers need to consider semantic HTML, responsive layouts, accessibility, and how review content fits into the overall user experience.

A well-built review section makes customer feedback easy to read without distracting visitors from the rest of the page. It should also work across different screen sizes and remain understandable to people using assistive technologies.

Let's look at how to build one using standard HTML and CSS.

The first step is to structure review content properly. Instead of placing everything inside generic

elements, use meaningful HTML elements to describe the content.

For example, a review can use

as its container and
for the customer's actual feedback.

This structure separates the review from its attribution and gives the section a clear heading. The rating also includes an accessible label so screen-reader users can understand what the stars represent.

For real websites, developers should display genuine customer feedback and avoid presenting invented testimonials as actual customer experiences.

A review section might look attractive on a desktop but become difficult to read on smaller screens. CSS Grid provides a straightforward way to create a flexible layout without relying on JavaScript.

The auto-fit and minmax() combination allows cards to adjust to the available space. Instead of defining a fixed number of columns for every device, the browser fits as many cards as the container can comfortably accommodate.

Developers working with more complex interfaces can explore SitePoint's guide to responsive web design for additional layout techniques.

A responsive layout should remain readable when viewed on a phone. Avoid fixed card widths, excessive padding, and text sizes that force users to zoom.

You can also adjust spacing at smaller viewport widths:

Test the section at several viewport sizes rather than relying on one desktop preview. Check that long customer names wrap correctly, cards do not overflow, and text remains comfortable to read.

Some review components use animated sliders, automatic scrolling, or hover effects. These features can create problems for visitors who prefer reduced motion or navigate with a keyboard.

A simple approach is to avoid unnecessary animation. If transitions are included, respect the visitor's system preference:

The MDN guide to using media queries for accessibility explains how developers can adapt interfaces to user preferences.

Also make sure interactive controls have visible keyboard focus styles. A carousel should provide usable previous and next controls, clear labels, and a way to pause automatic movement if it is used.

On a production website, reviews may come from a database, a content management system, or an external review service. Keeping the content separate from the presentation makes the component easier to maintain.

Businesses can also use an online reputation management platform to monitor customer feedback and understand how their brand is perceived across online channels.

For example, a developer could store each review with fields such as:

The frontend can then render the same component for every review. This avoids manually creating separate HTML for each customer and makes future design changes easier.

Developers should also validate incoming data, handle missing fields, and avoid displaying private customer information without permission.

Before publishing, check the component in different browsers and screen sizes. Test keyboard navigation, screen-reader labels, text wrapping, and color contrast. If reviews are loaded dynamically, check how the section behaves when there are no reviews or when the data request fails.

A useful review component does not need complicated animations or a large JavaScript library. Semantic HTML, flexible CSS Grid layouts, accessible labels, and reliable content handling provide a strong foundation.

Customer feedback can only be useful on a website when visitors can understand and navigate it easily. By treating reviews as a proper interface component rather than decorative content, developers can create sections that are maintainable, accessible, and consistent with the rest of the website.

#10322. Googlebook apps built by Googlers add macOS-like status bar shortcuts, keyboard commands

Googlebooks are a bit starved of desktop-class features at launch, but one of Google’s pitches around this platform is how apps can be built for it, and the company’s own employees are showing off what that looks like with some clever apps that just so happen to add features that feel a lot like they’re replicating macOS staples.

In a Reddit thread, three Googlebook apps built by Googlers through Antigravity are shown off, and they’re pretty cool, regardless of how you might feel about the platform itself.

“BentoBox” is a utility that gives you control over the status bar on Googlebook, enabling you to fill the status bar with shortcuts, mini-apps, and more. It’s full of glancable information such as weather conditions, battery charging speeds, and more, but also includes useful tools such as being able to access a clipboard history, set a timer, and more. The app requires permissions including accessibility overlays, but it works like a charm.

I had no idea the status bar on Googlebook could even be used like this, and it really makes that space a lot more useful. Of course, you have to draw a comparison here to macOS, which also uses this space for app shortcuts and tools, though BentoBox offers a different type of customization.

BentoBox is available for download via Google Play for free.

The other Mac-like app here is “Booklight,” a launcher that’s basically Apple’s Spotlight, just for Googlebook. It has a transparent design and lets you search “apps, settings, and the web.”

Technically, Googlebook already can do this in its built-in search bar (in the app drawer), but Booklight has more customization and functionality, including being able to write out shorthand commands all in one sentence with the app then completing the command in the appropriate apps, as well as having a clearer UI for experiences such as typing in quick math equations. It’s also good at finding symbols (so you don’t have to remember the U+ codes that carried over from ChromeOS). Not one I found myself wanting to use, but the potential is there.

Both BentoBox and Booklight were developed by Alexander Kuscher, VP over Laptops & Tablets at Google.

The other app highlighted in Google’s thread was Wayfinder, a file manager app described as a “desktop-grade file manager.” The big bonus here is network file access for NAS boxes and other uses, but the app also just has a more advanced UX compared to Googlebook’s built-in Files app.

Googlebook’s app gap is a real problem at launch, but these apps do show there’s a lot of potential in the platform, it’s just a matter of getting developers on board – which itself will require Google and its partners to actually sell some of these laptops… classic chicken-and-egg situation.

#10320. Claude Model Builds Exploits Across Major Browsers & OSes

An unreleased version of the AI model has found and chained multiple vulnerabilities in Chrome, Safari, Android, Windows, and Linux, Anthropic says.

An unreleased version of Claude identified critical vulnerabilities in some of the world's most widely used browsers and operating systems and chained them into working exploits requiring little or no human interaction, Anthropic said this week.

The exploits included one that enabled data theft from a locked Android smartphone, another zero-click exploit allowed remote code execution (RCE) with root privileges on Linux servers, and a third that could have allowed attackers to remotely crash Windows systems. In total, Anthropic said, the model generated five working exploits targeting Chrome, Safari, Android, Windows, and Linux. All affected vendors have addressed the vulnerabilities after Anthropic reported them.

The exploits are the latest evidence that frontier AI models are moving beyond simply identifying individual flaws to combining multiple into sophisticated working exploits. "These attack chains demonstrate the model's ability to create sophisticated, multi-step exploits in both open-source and proprietary software," Anthropic's Frontier Red Team said in a blog post this week.

Related:AI Makes Reverse Engineering Mainstream Software & Malware Far Easier

Historically, creating such chained exploits would have required special teams with domain expertise, the researchers wrote. "Now, it's something models can do with little human involvement, and then prove by producing a working exploit that anyone can run and verify."

Anthropic's report described a one-click RCE exploit chain involving Chrome on macOS. The exploit combined four vulnerabilities in Chrome's JavaScript engine, memory protections, browser process isolation, and federated sign-in functionality.

Each of the vulnerabilities that Claude discovered helped overcome a different security control and allowed the chained exploit to escape the browser's sandbox. As a proof of concept, Anthropic's exploit launched macOS Calculator from Chrome when a user loaded a malicious webpage.

Anthropic said it reported 62 vulnerabilities to Google, all of which had been assigned CVE identifiers, rated high or critical, and patched by Chrome 156.

"For example, if the user is signed in to their email account, the attacker can now read their email; if they’re logged into their bank account, the attacker can view account balances or attempt transfers," Anthropic said. The exploit chain combined four weaknesses Claude identified in Safari — a use-after-free flaw in Safari's JavaScriptCore engine, a way to bypass WebKit's Gigacage memory protection, a vulnerability in the WebContent sandbox, and a JavaScriptCore flaw.

Related:Microsoft Fixes RCE Bug in Windows Paint After Weeks of Confusion

Apple has since fixed the issue, Anthropic said.

The Android exploit, meanwhile, targeted a locked Google Pixel 9. Claude chained three vulnerabilities in Android's device-transfer and USB-handling mechanisms, bypassing four layers of protection to extract photos and other personal information from the phone without unlocking it. Anthropic said Google has addressed the issue.

Claude's Windows exploit, which Microsoft has patched, involved a remotely exploitable denial-of-service (DoS) vulnerability. The flaw was an out-of-bounds read in the Windows kernel's implementation of the transport protocol used by HTTP/3. An attacker could send a small number of specially crafted network packets to a vulnerable server to crash it and repeatedly trigger crashes, disrupting access for users, Anthropic said.

For Linux, Claude developed a zero-click exploit against the kernel's networking stack that would have let an unauthenticated remote attacker execute commands with root-level privileges on affected servers, without requiring any user interaction. "This vulnerability (CVE-2026-98432) was patched upstream and backported to all maintained stable kernel branches," Anthropic said.

Related:Critical Rails Bug Threatens to Derail Vulnerable Servers

With AI getting better at discovering and exploiting vulnerabilities, defenders need powerful models for patching them before attackers can exploit the flaws, Anthropic said. "Enabling defenders with powerful models is critical to giving them a headstart on patching systems before attackers do," the company said.

To that end, the company is expanding access to its most capable AI models for vetted cybersecurity professionals through its Cyber Verification Program. The program now has three access tiers that give security teams a way to apply for the level of access appropriate for their work.

"It includes access to our most capable models, including Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Sonnet 5.5, Claude Mythos 5.1, and other new models moving forward," Anthropic said. "Our advice to defenders remains the same: scanning your own code is more important than ever."

Andrew Bolster, senior R&D manager at Black Duck, says Anthropic's results are credible where they can be verified. The Chrome, WebKit, Windows, and Linux fixes are all public, he notes, but any claims that Claude discovered them autonomously are Anthropic's own. "The report gives no success rates, cost, or human-hours per chain, which is critical context for security leaders," he says.

Some of the conditions for the exploits to work were also noteworthy, he says. For instance, the Android chain involved a stolen phone and a USB cable, and the Linux exploit required an exposed MPTCP listener. "Exploit benchmarks show model success dropping sharply once ASLR, stack canaries, and sandboxing are enabled, and VulnCheck found only 1.3% of AI-discovered vulnerabilities confirmed exploited in the wild," Bolster says. "Delivery, reliability, and target preconditions remain the attacker’s problem for now, though I'd expect that gap to narrow."

Claude's chained exploits are another indication that AI has lowered the barrier to discovering vulnerabilities and building working attacks, says Jacob Krell, senior director, secure AI solutions and cybersecurity at Suzu Labs. But there are still substantial barriers between a proof of concept and a reliable attack in the wild. "A shell on one honeypot tells you little about the next server's worker count, build, or kernel," Krell notes. "AI has made finding the bug cheap. Getting it to work on a server it has never seen is still the expensive part."

At the same time, the gap between a proof of concept and a reliable, broadly deployable attack is increasingly becoming about time and target-specific adaptation rather than specialized expertise, he cautions.

Defenders are making a mistake if they assume this kind of vulnerability discovery always requires the most powerful AI models, adds John Strand, owner of Black Hills Information Security. "Local systems, including a Mac Studio with 128GB of memory or a DGX Spark, can support AI-assisted reverse engineering and vulnerability research. The target, model, tools, and operator's skill matter."

It may take attackers longer, with less capable systems, and attacks may be more practical only against smaller, less-scrutinized code bases. But defenders need to account for that capability, Strand says. "If AI can find vulnerabilities in Windows, Android, Linux, and Chrome, what does that say about code that receives a fraction of that attention? Think about your IP cameras. Think about the firmware on the router running a remote office. All of that is in play."

Illinois-based Jai Vijayan is a veteran, award-winning technology journalist with more than 25 years of experience covering cybersecurity. His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. Jai holds a Master’s degree in statistics from Bangalore University, and studied broadcasting and electronic communication at Marquette University in Milwaukee.

#10319. Fitbit Sense & Versa losing 3rd-party watch faces this month, existing downloads remain

Google announced today that Fitbit Sense and Versa smartwatches will soon lose the ability to “install or update third-party apps and clocks.”

This change to the Fitbit App Gallery applies to the Versa, Versa 2, Versa 3, Versa 4, Sense, and Sense 2.

Existing “third-party apps or clock faces installed on your Fitbit device” will remain available, but you can no longer download new ones (or update them) starting November 28. As such, “if you choose to delete an app or clock face, or factory reset your device, you will no longer be able to re-install it.”

Instead, the Fitbit App Gallery will “exclusively feature apps and clock faces created by Fitbit and Google.”

The November 28 deadline also applies to third-party developers. Google will “no longer support uploading new apps and clock faces or updating existing ones.”

Google winding down Fitbit OS for smartwatches is not particularly surprising as the company has been all-in on Wear OS and the Pixel Watch. That said, the Sense 2 and Versa 4 should probably no longer be on sale.

It would certainly be a welcome development for open-source projects everywhere.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Most open-source projects have meager resources, and the inrush of AI-provided bug reports and "fixes" has been a bane on their respective maintainers. However, the Linux kernel is well-manned, and code for it generally has a higher barrier to entry than in other places. Linus Torvalds thinks that the flood of AI bug reports may be settling, as the kernel's Berkeley Packet Filter (BPF) portion saw more AI fixes than reports.

Torvalds splits off the changes among three areas: graphics, networking, and sound drivers; networking and the BPF; and miscellaneous changes. He proceeds to state that "much of this was driven by various AI fixes," an apparent underscoring of his stance that AI-written contributions should be embraced rather than shunned — so long as they're subject to the same quality control.

The Linux maestro also relayed a note from Alexei Starovoitov, the BPF maintainer, who claims that this subsection actually saw a higher rate of AI-provided fixes versus reports. This might signal a tidal shift, at least where the Linux kernel is concerned, and would be exceedingly welcome news for other open-source maintainers everywhere tired of dealing with junk reports at the same time they have to handle a higher influx of legitimate bugs, thanks to AI-driven bug discovery.

In Torvalds' own words, "I think most other areas haven't reached that point yet, but it's at least a sign that we may be starting to see the tide starting to turn."

Anyone can make a pointless bug report, but it's hard to deny that the existence of powerful AI bots has prompted a renewed focus on cybersecurity most everywhere. Firefox fixed 423 security bugs in an April release, Chrome found and fixed 1,072 bugs in June — making for more fixes in 2026 than the project's previous eight years — and my personal "favorite," NodeJS, sadly fixed 50 vulnerabilities instead of announcing its closure.

Many open-source projects have complained about high-volume, low-quality AI-driven bug reports, with some even shutting down their bug bounty programs altogether, or placing additional verifications for issue reports. We'd venture that the Linux kernel has a much larger bug-triaging team than your average project, though, meaning it has more resources to sift through the junk.

This tentative conclusion popped up in a message sent yesterday to the kernel mailing list, wherein Torvalds briefly describes the overall shape of the kernel's 7.4-rc2 update. He says that despite the update looking large from a pure file-count perspective, it's actually mostly composed of one- and two-line fixes and is "very straightforward" to implement.

#10316. Build a Hardware Diagnostic Log with Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A device disconnects halfway through a test. The application reports a timeout, the firmware restarts, and the next attempt succeeds. Where should you start?

For developers working with connected devices, browser interfaces, or hardware prototypes, an application error is often only the final symptom. Replacing code without recording the conditions around a failure can leave you chasing the wrong layer.

This tutorial builds a small browser-based diagnostic log. It records measurements, preserves the test conditions, and exports evidence for another developer to review. You need basic HTML and JavaScript knowledge; no framework or connected instrument is required.

1. Define the question before collecting data Consider a hypothetical development board that disconnects when a peripheral starts. Several explanations remain possible: a firmware exception, a transport problem, or a power supply issue. Start with a testable question: “Does the measured supply voltage change when this peripheral starts?” That question tells you what to record and what to keep constant.

Use a known-good board of the same revision where available. Match the firmware, cable, supply, peripheral, and workload. A comparison loses value when several conditions change together.

Record the observation separately from the explanation:

The hypothesis remains provisional. A reading taken after the device restarts does not establish what happened during the failure.

2. Choose evidence that answers the question Application logs, firmware traces, and bench measurements answer different questions. A timeout establishes that an operation did not finish in time; it does not identify a failed component.

For a view of specialist equipment used in phone-board troubleshooting, DIYFIXTOOL’s overview of Chip-Level Diagnostic Tools discusses thermal imaging, BGA X-ray inspection, and inductance testing. Treat it as a vendor-authored introduction to equipment categories; use instrument documentation and board-specific references when interpreting measurements.

The log below deliberately leaves interpretation to the operator. Enter a numeric value, its unit, and the conditions under which it was measured. Do not turn an isolated reading into an automatic “faulty chip” verdict.

For physical measurements, follow the board and instrument documentation and work within your training. This tutorial covers the software record rather than a procedure for probing or repairing a powered device. 3. Build a small, accessible form

Save the following as index.html and serve it through your usual local development server. For example, if Python is installed, run python -m http. server 8000 in its directory, then open http://localhost:8000. 4. Preserve data without hiding failures

The application stores an array of records as JSON. On reload, it parses that JSON and checks the expected field types before rendering it. Parsing alone does not validate the structure of stored data.

SitePoint’s guide to looping through JSON responses provides useful background on working with objects and arrays after parsing JSON. Each table cell uses textContent, so a note containing HTML is displayed as text rather than inserted as markup. The MDN documentation for textContent explains this behavior.

Storage operations sit inside try...catch blocks because browser persistence can fail. The interface keeps new measurements in memory and offers an export when saving fails. It does not claim those measurements will survive a reload. For another practical vanilla JavaScript project, SitePoint’s **Markdown previewer tutorial **explores browser UI construction and persistence.

As **MDN’s localStorage documentation **explains, storage belongs to the document’s origin. Opening the application on a different port may therefore show a different log. This prototype is a single-browser tool, so export important sessions and avoid recording customer identifiers or private device contents.

5. Compare measurements under matching conditions Suppose two hypothetical readings are 3.29 V and 3.08 V. Their difference is −0.21 V. That arithmetic is straightforward; its meaning depends on the rail specification, measurement uncertainty, workload, and timing.

Do not add a universal voltage threshold to the application. A device-specific limit should come from its specification or an established test procedure.

If the failure is brief, a manually entered steady-state measurement may miss it entirely. Record that limitation. The next investigation may require a time-correlated capture rather than another static reading.

When sharing a session, include the firmware revision, board revision, instrument settings, and reproduction steps. A colleague should be able to repeat the comparison without guessing how the reading was obtained. 6. Verify the workflow before relying on it Try adding a decimal reading and a negative reading, then reload the page. Confirm that both values and their units remain intact. Enter a note containing test and check that it appears literally in the table.

Export the JSON and inspect the record count. Then simulate blocked storage and confirm that the interface reports the failure while allowing an export of the current session.

For a larger application, add editable entries, instrument metadata, and explicit baseline records. A shared service would also need access controls, server-side validation, and a policy for retention. These additions should follow the needs of the diagnostic workflow.

Turn a symptom into a reproducible investigation

The finished prototype captures a useful minimum: what was measured, where, when, and under which conditions. Its value comes from preserving evidence that another person can inspect.

When an application error crosses into firmware or hardware, that record helps you choose the next experiment. Repeat the failure, change one variable, and document what the new evidence supports before deciding what to fix.

#10313. Elon Musk’s Terafab targets 1 million wafers a month after 2032

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Terafab’s procurement team reportedly has already been to Japan, asking some toolmakers to deliver within months.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Elon Musk’s Terafab, a joint venture of Tesla and SpaceX, has informally told equipment makers it is seeking to produce 1 million wafers per month after 2032, according to Financial News. About 70% of this output, or 700,000 wafers, is planned to go to memory, with more than 300,000 going to logic chips. Terafab’s procurement team has already visited Japan to ask some local toolmakers for delivery within months. The memory allotment would rival Samsung’s estimated DRAM production capacity if it were all of that type.

Samsung’s DRAM capacity was estimated last year at 650,000–700,000 wafers a month, while SK hynix is reportedly at about 550,000, with about 1 million a goal by 2030. The comparison has to be taken in context, as Terafab’s 700,000 measures all of its expected memory output, which could be NAND or DRAM, while the Samsung and SK hynix figures measure only DRAM. As it is, the memory portion alone would be 3.5 times the reported 200,000 at SK hynix’s Wuxi fab in China.

Many in the industry were skeptical when Musk announced the plans in March, but orders are now leading more companies to see a real business opportunity. The timeline is aggressive, as it usually takes three years or more to get from planning to volume at a leading-edge fab, and Terafab is targeting volume production in the second half of 2028. Procurement officials from the company reportedly visited Japan earlier this month to meet local suppliers and to work on details on bringing in equipment, with some suppliers asked to deliver within a few months.

The Elec also reports that Terafab representatives visited booths of about 10 Korean materials, parts, and equipment firms at SEMICON West, with some NDAs signed.

Logic chips are one thing — Jiji Press reports Terafab is working with Intel to adopt its 2nm and 1.4nm processes — but memory is another, and it’s not clear what type of memory is on the docket. Technical help from a Taiwanese company is under consideration for memory, but no partner has been named, leaving 70% of the planned wafers without a disclosed process technology.

Memory production tends to split between DRAM and NAND flash, the latter for non-volatile storage. A Tesla job listing for a Terafab Memory Process Integration Engineer, reported in August, covers end-to-end DRAM process integration and DRAM node development, and supports emerging memories such as MRAM, RRAM, and 3D DRAM. Terafab hasn’t said how its DRAM would be divided among products like HBM and LPDDR, both useful for AI and both mentioned in its job postings, or whether it expects to make and stack the DRAM itself or package chips it brings in.

Building a cutting-edge logic fab on U.S. soil is its own challenge, and Tesla and SpaceX lack chip-manufacturing experience. Even Musk, on a SpaceX call last month, called Terafab’s dates “very aggressive” and said he put “big error bars” on the timeline. The first SRAM test chip is targeted for around May or June 2027 at the Austin research facility.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

How TSMC might fit in remains open. TSMC says any fab with its involvement has to be run by TSMC, while Musk said his own companies would build and operate Terafab, a scenario which could leave TSMC as a space tenant. At least the spending plans are known: $16.8 billion for the first phase in Grimes County, Texas, with total investment of up to $119 billion if later phases proceed. A pilot plant could be completed as early as July 2027.
