#10309. Going deep on local AI performance benchmarking with Apple’s Mac Studio with M5 Ultra taking on Nvidia DGX Spark

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Is a Mac Studio a better investment than a DGX Spark?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Apple’s updated Mac Studio with the M5 Ultra SoC has already proven itself as one of the most powerful systems around, thanks to its 36-core CPU and 80-core GPU with Neural Accelerators for the matrix math operations that drive AI compute. Beyond that raw power, options for up to 512GB of unified memory have many local AI enthusiasts lining up for the 13 to 15 weeks it’ll take for Apple to ship one right now.

But is our tested Mac Studio config worth a whopping $12,300 or more for those folks? We already tested the maxed-out M5 Ultra chip with the popular Qwen 3.8 27B in our initial review, but that compact dense model doesn’t come anywhere close to exercising 256GB of memory. We wanted to fill that pool to the brim with cutting-edge large models like DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 and GLM 5.3 Flash.

We also have some new benchmarks at our disposal to see how the Mac Studio stacks up in agentic tasks on the GPU side in the wake of our review of the Microsoft Surface Laptop Ultra with Nvidia’s RTX Spark chip. So there's much to dig into.

We started our more detailed local AI testing with Artificial Analysis' AgentPerf-Local benchmark. This test plays back recorded inference traces with certain models across a range of agentic AI workloads to gauge performance. Artificial Analysis provides a full breakdown of this workload, but the bulk of its work takes place in prompt processing rather than token generation. As a result, it should favor systems with lots of raw compute, which both GB10 and the M5 Ultra have at their disposal.

The M5 Ultra's 80-core GPU dwarfs GB10's 48 Blackwell SMs at a high level. Even without detailed info on clocks, it would be extremely difficult for GB10 to outgun the M5 Ultra in this test. And the Mac's 1.2 TB/s of memory bandwidth gives it just over four times as much decode bandwidth as GB10's 273 GB/s. But as our past tests have shown, raw specs aren't as important as delivered performance with AI. Delivered performance can vary greatly from paper specs.

We continued to use the dense Qwen 3.8 27B for this test. We used llama.cpp as our inference backend on both the Mac Studio and a single Dell Pro Precision GB10 box, which is nearly identical to the DGX Spark. We further enabled Qwen 3.8 27B’s native multi-token prediction (MTP) support for a decode speed boost on both platforms.

Versus a single DGX Spark-like system, the M5 Ultra Mac Studio finishes this agentic test in just under half the time, an extremely impressive result. One might think this is a silly comparison given the imbalance in compute resources and memory bandwidth between these two systems, but the cost of a single 4TB Dell Pro Precision GB10, like the one we tested, is now a whopping $10K, so comparing it to the $12K Mac Studio head-to-head isn’t actually that far-fetched.

Because AgentPerf-Local performs a fixed amount of work on every system, we can also directly compare power efficiency between platforms by recording the wall power consumed by a single Spark and the Mac Studio and converting it to task energy in kilojoules.

At least with llama.cpp, the Mac Studio not only finishes the AgentPerf-Local test much faster than the DGX Spark, but it also consumes less task energy overall — about 16% less. A different inference engine might produce a more favorable result for the Spark-like system, but apples-to-apples, this test looks fantastic for the Mac Studio.

We can multiply both task time and energy consumed (in kilojoules) together to arrive at the energy-delay product (EDP) for each system. A lower EDP wraps up a fast time to completion and a low task energy into a single comparable figure for efficiency.

The Mac Studio’s EDP is under half that of the DGX Spark in this test, once again indicating its impressive efficiency and performance with this dense model and workload.

Of course, Qwen 3.8 27B doesn’t need 256GB (or even 128GB) of RAM to do its thing. To really stretch the legs of the Mac Studio, we chose two larger open models that are near the top of capability benchmarks right now: GLM-5.3 Flash and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017.

The M5 Ultra Mac Studio can natively fit quantized versions of these models (a four-bit quantization in the case of GLM 5.3 and a two-bit quantization courtesy of Unsloth for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash) in its 256GB of RAM, but there’s no way even those quantizations will fit on a single 128GB DGX Spark.

That’s where the clustering capability of Nvidia’s platform comes in. We’ve already seen how pairing up Sparks can scale up one’s local inference capacity, and that capability is critical when evaluating them against a Mac Studio like this.

But the rapidly rising prices of GB10 systems have frustrated our value analysis during the course of this testing. For just one example, Lenovo set all of its ThinkStation PGX systems to a “coming soon” status during our tests. And as we’ve already mentioned, the Dell Pro Precision GB10s we’re clustering here are an eye-popping $10K each. We have no reason to believe the rest of the GB10 systems on the market will be that much cheaper once this current volatility shakes out.

No matter how you slice it, getting even a pair of DGX Sparks with 4TB SSDs is going to end up costing quite a bit more than this single Mac Studio, even if $20K for a dual-Dell cluster is at the top end of the market. $15K or more is a relatively more reasonable benchmark, but even then, that’s 25% more money than a single M5 Ultra Studio.

We don't yet have end-to-end agentic tests for these larger models, but directionally, we can still consider prompt processing and decode performance to see which of these systems would deliver faster agentic turnaround.

Because of the limited choice of compatible inference engines for Apple Silicon, we have to use llama.cpp for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017. Worse, the Mac Studio can't hold both the DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 model weights and the DSpark speculative decoding model required to boost token output even with a two-bit quantization, so we’re forced to leave performance on the table.

In contrast, the DeepSeek V4.1 Flash serving recipe for our Spark cluster can take advantage of DSpark speculative decoding and the TensorFold inference engine, which can deliver higher performance than llama.cpp.

Even with MLX support, llama.cpp performance with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash on the Mac Studio is pokey next to the DGX Spark cluster. Between its slower prompt processing speed and unaugmented decoding, it would end up at a disadvantage in agentic work with this model.

The community two-Spark serving recipe from Mia’s AI Lab with TensorFold is a major asset for owners of those systems, even accounting for the bit of extra work involved in setting up and managing a Spark cluster.

Where the Mac running llama.cpp needs anywhere from 10 to 25 minutes to digest the longest context lengths we tested for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, the Spark cluster needs under five minutes at most to do the same jobs with this recipe — and the TensorFold inference engine is much faster on the decode side, too. If you want to run this model locally, a pair of Sparks is the clear winner right now.

With DeepSeek out of the way, let’s consider GLM 5.3 Flash, another leading open model for local intelligence. Because MTP with GLM 5.3 Flash doesn't require a separate drafting model, we can give it a decode boost on the Mac Studio without the memory headroom issues we saw with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash. For our Spark cluster, we chose to run GLM 5.3 Flash using a recipe cooked up by Mia's AI Lab.

GLM 5.3 Flash runs much better on llama.cpp than DeepSeek V4.1 Flash does, but even so, the DGX Spark cluster delivers much faster prompt processing times across the board. The M5 Ultra's memory bandwidth advantage means the Mac is delivering higher tokens per second on the decode side, but it’s likely that any agentic turn would still finish faster on the Spark cluster.

Because a TensorFold serving recipe is available for GLM 5.3 Flash on Apple Silicon, however, we can see just how much extra performance is available from an MLX-specific four-bit model quantization and a theoretically higher-performance inference engine.

This serving recipe turbocharges the Mac Studio’s performance compared to llama.cpp. Prompt processing times with TensorFold get much closer to those of the Spark cluster, and decode throughput is a startling three times faster, on average.

We expect (and can only hope) that improved inference engine support and serving recipes for DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 on the M5 Ultra will arrive in time as community members unleash their coding agents on this hardware, because the performance potential is clearly there for Apple Silicon.

A single M5 Ultra Mac Studio can deliver incredible local AI performance and power efficiency versus a single DGX Spark when all the puzzle pieces fall into place. And that's not as absurd a comparison as it might seem.

Prices for unified memory systems are ballooning to the point that a single Dell Pro Precision GB10 system with a 4TB SSD is now over $10K, closing in on the $12K-ish price of the Mac Studio configuration that we tested. We expect those trends will only continue as the RAM and NAND supply crunch becomes more acute.

But our broader experience shows that the M5 Ultra's impressive performance isn't assured from every model and inference engine, especially from the bigger and highly intelligent models you'll want to put in 256GB of RAM.

This Mac's prompt processing speeds can really rip with llama.cpp and smaller models like Qwen 3.8 27B, but you really want to ensure that you have a higher-performance serving recipe at your disposal for bigger models like GLM 5.3 Flash and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017. The TensorFold engine runs GLM 5.3 Flash incredibly well, but it doesn’t support DeepSeek at all yet.

As we emphasized in our review of the M4 Max Mac Studio, memory bandwidth is just one factor in AI inference performance. You need to take the entire hardware-software stack into account in order to wholly judge the performance and model compatibility available from these extremely pricey unified memory setups.

Even as Apple closes the performance shortfalls of its hardware for AI inference, development and support around the MLX stack has a ways to go before it catches up with the maturity and performance, as well as the model and inference engine options, available on Nvidia hardware.

The enduring value of the DGX Spark platform beyond its raw performance and scalability is that the breadth and velocity of community software development and support around the platform has gotten to the point that you're virtually assured of a way to run a hot new model soon after its weights hit Hugging Face (at least assuming you have enough Sparks to cluster to provide the memory capacity necessary).

That assurance of community software support and ongoing development, along with the DRAM and NAND supply crunches, means high demand and ever higher prices for 128GB GB10 boxes. Two such Sparks with 4TB drives will run you an eye-watering $15K to $20K+, depending on the partner system and storage configuration you choose. And those prices are likely to continue rising, making a single Mac Studio a potentially appealing option as long as Apple doesn’t jack up its prices for these systems in turn.

If strong local AI performance is just one thing you need from a personal workstation, and you also want one of the world's fastest CPUs and a powerful GPU for general-purpose work in one well-rounded, efficient, and quiet compact chassis, the Mac Studio is a remarkable system.

If you can wait for software to catch up to this system’s incredible performance potential for local AI and don’t mind the possibility of narrower model and inference engine support compared to a DGX Spark setup, get in line, as lead times for these systems stretch into February 2027 as of this writing.

But if you're thinking about dropping well over $10K specifically for the purpose of building a local AI inference setup right now, perhaps for yourself or a small office, clustering a pair of Sparks gets you both the memory capacity and compute horsepower you need with the robust software support and broad model compatibility you’d expect for that outlay to be worthwhile. Those assurances may be worth the lower raw performance potential of such a cluster compared to the Mac Studio.

As the Senior Analyst, Graphics at Tom's Hardware, Jeff Kampman covers everything that has to do with graphics cards, gaming performance, and more. From integrated graphics processors to discrete graphics cards to the hyperscale installations powering our AI future, if it's got a GPU in it, Jeff is on it.

#10307. Google Workspace vs. Microsoft 365 vs. Proton Workspace: Which Is Best for Developers and Freelancers?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Think about what's sitting in your inbox and Drive right now. Client contracts and invoices. Project specs under NDA. Staging credentials a client emailed you. That API key or password you meant to delete.

For most developers and freelancers, all of it lives in Google Workspace or Microsoft 365. Both are excellent tools, and both encrypt your data in transit and at rest. But in their standard setup, the provider holds the encryption keys, which means your provider (and the integrated apps and AI features connected to it) can access what's inside.

Proton Workspace takes a different approach: the same everyday tools, with encryption designed so that not even Proton can read your data.

Here's how the three compare, what each does best, and which makes the most sense for how you work.

Google and Microsoft are built around ecosystem integration. Proton is built around keeping your data yours, without sacrificing productivity tools.

Google Workspace is the go-to suite for startups, agencies and freelancers, and for good reason. Gmail, Drive, Docs, Sheets, Calendar and Meet work together seamlessly, and several people can edit the same file in real time without emailing versions back and forth.

Google encrypts data at rest and in transit, and offers client-side encryption on eligible editions. But that extra layer requires higher-tier plans and configuration, so in the default experience Google holds the keys. A password manager and VPN aren't part of the suite.

Best for: Teams whose top priorities are real-time collaboration and third-party integrations.

If your work runs on Word, Excel and Outlook, Microsoft 365 needs little introduction. Business Standard includes full desktop apps, and Excel remains the benchmark for heavy spreadsheet work.

That breadth comes with complexity. Licensing, permissions and device management can eat into time a small team or solo developer would rather spend shipping. And like Google, Microsoft's everyday productivity experience isn't built around end-to-end encryption, while Copilot and advanced security features add cost.

Best for: Businesses built on desktop Office apps and established Microsoft workflows.

Everything is managed from one admin console, with one bill.

This is the core difference. Proton uses zero-access encryption: your data is encrypted before it reaches Proton's servers, and Proton never holds the keys. Proton can't read your data. Not won't. Can't.

The honest trade-offs: Proton Docs and Sheets cover core editing and collaboration, not every advanced Excel feature. There's no presentation app yet, and the third-party integration ecosystem is smaller than Google's or Microsoft's.

US prices for comparable plans with annual billing, excluding taxes:

The headline prices are close. The real cost isn't:

Proton Workspace covers business email, 1 TB of encrypted storage, docs, sheets, video calls, a password manager and a VPN. That's one bill instead of three.

Need more? Workspace Premium and adds 3 TB of storage per user, 20 custom domains, meetings for up to 250 participants, Lumo private AI and email data retention policies for only $7 more per month. Both plans include a 14-day free trial, and annual plans are currently up to 20% off.

Choose Google Workspace if your team lives in real-time collaboration and relies heavily on Google's integration ecosystem.

Choose Microsoft 365 if your work depends on desktop Excel, PowerPoint and established Microsoft workflows.

Less than you might expect. Proton's Easy Switch tool imports email, contacts and calendars from Google Workspace and other providers, and you keep your custom domain, so clients won't notice a thing. Smaller teams can migrate on their own, and Proton's business team supports larger moves.

Moving from Microsoft 365? Check the migration options for each data type rather than assuming every workflow transfers automatically.

Is Proton Workspace a good alternative to Google Workspace for developers?

Can Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 read my email and files?

By default, yes. Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 hold the encryption keys, and their default terms give them access to your data.

No. Zero-access encryption means Proton never holds the keys to your stored data. Emails between Proton users are end-to-end encrypted automatically, and you can send password-protected encrypted emails to anyone.

Yes. Proton's apps are open source and independently audited.

Can I use Proton Mail with my existing email client?

Yes. Proton Mail Bridge connects Outlook, Apple Mail and Thunderbird.

Does Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 use my data to train AI?

It can. If your plan allows Google Workspace or Microsoft 365 to hold the encryption keys, they can access your data and use it to train their integrated AI models.

No. Proton can't read your data, so it can't use it for AI training.

Google Workspace and Microsoft 365 are mature, capable platforms, and if your work depends on advanced Excel or a deep web of Google integrations, there's a good case for using them.

But if you're a freelancer or a developer or running a small team, Proton Workspace is the stronger choice. You get the same core tools for less money, a password manager and VPN you'd otherwise pay for separately, and encryption that keeps client data, code and credentials readable only by you.

You don't have to take that on faith, either. The code is open source, and the trial is free.

#10306. Samsung Galaxy S27 series camera specs leak, and they’re disappointing

New leaks reveal the camera specs for Samsung’s upcoming Galaxy S27 series which includes three lenses across the lineup, but not the upgrades you want, nor what Ultra owners might expect.

SammyGuru reports that Samsung is “evaluating” camera setups for the Galaxy S27, S27+, S27 Pro, and S27 Ultra, and it looks like a mixed bag across the board.

The base Galaxy S27 and S27+ are looking very familiar, with identical main camera sensors to last year, and the same ultrawide sensor that’s been in use since the Galaxy S23 series. The telephoto and selfie sensors are looking at new hardware, but without any major spec upgrades.

Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, meanwhile, have a new 200MP main camera on deck, a 50MP ultrawide shooter, and a mere 12MP telephoto. The Ultra might get 5x compared to the Pro’s 3x optical zoom, but with a secondary telephoto lens going missing, existing users of the lineup are sure to be disappointed.

Notably, SammyGuru’s article mentions the Pro as using “the Galaxy S26 Ultra’s 3x sensor,” but shows the 12MP Samsung S5K3LD in that place instead. We’re assuming that’s a typo seeing as the post mentions the Pro as using the Samsung S5KJN3 or the Sony IMX855 earlier on, but regardless, the Pro only gets 3x optical zoom to the Ultra’s 5x.

Some of these specs have been mentioned by other sources such as GalaxyClub and leaker Ice Universe, but this is the most complete picture of the S27’s camera plans we’ve seen to date.

Personally, I’m very “whelmed” on this one. As RAMageddon rages on and Samsung hunts for new ways to balance specs vs price, cameras are a place that’s obviously going to be cut, but competitors are already running laps around Samsung on the hardware front. Cutting lenses and skimping out on upgrades (again) doesn’t help matters.

#10304. Google Antigravity adds ‘Knowledge Work’ interface for non-coding work

Following last week’s big update, Google Antigravity is rolling out a “Knowledge Work” user experience.

Antigravity launched with a coding focus, but Google has seen how people, including employees internally, are using it for “far more than code.”

Alongside software engineering, more and more daily sessions center on deep research, data analysis, drafting documents and presentations, prototyping designs, and automating everyday workflows.

The company shared that “tens of thousands of Googlers outside software engineering (across sales, finance, legal, product, design, research, marketing, and operations) now rely on Antigravity every week alongside engineers to get their work done.”

As such, Google Antigravity now has an optimized Knowledge Work experience that joins the existing Software Engineering interface. The underlying Antigravity agent is unchanged “with full access to frontier models, local files, plugins, skills, rules, custom agents, and MCP servers.”

When Knowledge Work is enabled, you’ll see the following UI changes:

To enable, go to Settings > General > Interface Layout. On first launch, there’s a new onboarding flow.

#10303. New framework grades AI jailbreaks across capability gains, scope, and how easy they are to deploy

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

One ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

In recent months, high-profile headlines about AI models letting loose from their safeguards dominated headlines. Now, the Frontier Model Forum (FMF) has released a new Jailbreak Rating Framework that aims to track how easy it might be for a given AI model to get around safeguards. The framework also estimates the scale of the collateral damage, should an AI model wreak cyber-havoc across the internet.

The project started in 2023 with the support of four founding companies: Anthropic, Google, Microsoft, and OpenAI. The published Jailbreak Rating Framework is a rough analog to the well-known CVSS security rating system, but specifically targeted at classifying the type and severity of LLM jailbreaks across various axes and multiple frontier LLM providers.

Many AI companies and evaluation entities already have their own rating systems, but the FMF intends for its scale to be a cross-vendor standard that teams developing patches can rely on.

The FMF's approach is to have a classification method for jailbreaks that can make a model perform cyber-warfare operations beyond what's already out, across both the regular and black market. Version 1.0 of the proposal is a work-in-progress, and the group is taking comments and suggestions on it until December 21, noting that relying on its system in the meantime is inadvisable.

Right now, the framework has four scoring components, split across two main categories: severity, measuring how serious a jailbreak technique is, and likelihood, or how easy it is to find and weaponize it.

A combined final rating adds the scores obtained from the two categories into a final low-to-critical rating. The scientists note that the capability gain is the most important dimension and acts as both a floor and a ceiling for the remaining scores — a jailbreak that doesn't offer results beyond what can already be obtained via other means is meaningless within the purview of the grading system.

The individual ratings pertain to capability gains, capability scope, ease of deployment, and ease of discovery. They're all graded from zero to three, though the FMF stresses that a rating of zero doesn't mean a jailbreak is harmless or ought to be ignored — rather, it means the flaw should be addressed by standard practices like the platform's policies and standard cybersecurity ratings.

An important feature is that the FMF's baseline implicitly changes as models evolve, meaning that a grade of zero for a jailbreak in 2026 likely isn't going to mean the same thing as it would in 2028, or isn't better than alternative tools. A capability-gain rating of zero means that the jailbreak under analysis doesn't offer any improvement beyond what can already be done with existing models or tooling right off the bat.

For example, jailbreaking a model to perform a DDoS on a site isn't all that helpful to an attacker, since there are a number of black-market sites for that — or even other LLMs with fewer safeguards. This doesn't mean the jailbreak isn't handy, only that it's no better than the alternatives. However, the rating can be higher if the attacker can leverage the jailbreak to get a better DDoS than they could otherwise shop for.

On the other end of the capability-gain scale, three means that a jailbroken model can perform cyber-attacks with capabilities similar to those of a model operating without safeguards.

The capability scope measurement is easier to interpret: zero means that the jailbreak only works for one specific action or target and can't be tweaked for other applications, or in other words, is a one-trick pony. A jailbreak gets a rating of three when it can, without specific redesign, make a model perform work across at least two entirely unrelated categories of cyber-warfare — like getting the model to both autonomously discover vulnerabilities or, separately, write malware.

For ease of deployment, the FMF considers the amount of human interaction, or the degree of automation that the jailbreak allows. A zero means that the jailbreak must be steered by humans with a high degree of knowledge, providing additional instructions to be effective, while a three means that, with little effort, it can be used by a novice and results in an automated cyber-warfare task.

Last but by no means least, ease-of-discovery is how hard it is to find the jailbreak. At a zero, the attacker must spend copious amounts of money on inference tokens, need elevated access (like a privileged account given to a white-hat hacker), or specialized infrastructure like a legion of AI servers. A rating of three simply means that anyone can look up the technique and use it.

If all of this sounds familiar, you may be thinking of Anthropic's safeguard-breakage classifiers the company proposed in July 2026, after an incident that led to Claude Fable 5 being temporarily unavailable. Much like the FMF's effort, Anthropic's system would also use a moving baseline and only consider jailbreaks that improve on cyber-warfare capabilities already available.

That document is strikingly similar to FMF's proposal. Anthropic is an FMF founding member, and as such may have had a hand or two in developing the new standard — though we have no confirmation either way.

Bruno Ferreira is a contributing writer for Tom's Hardware. He has decades of experience with PC hardware and assorted sundries, alongside a career as a developer. He's obsessed with detail and has a tendency to ramble on the topics he loves. When not doing that, he's usually playing games, or at live music shows and festivals.

#10301. Prompt Management in TypeScript: LangSmith vs Git vs 3 Open-Source Registries

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every prompt registry we tested can change a prompt in a running TypeScript app, roll it back, and give production and staging different versions. LangSmith's prompt hub, Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore all passed those three tests. The real differences show up elsewhere: which template syntax each one accepts, how many npm packages it pulls in, how long a change takes to reach the app, and what happens when the registry is down.

We tested all of this with one small app and one prompt, which has a condition and a loop. Git files served as the baseline. Every number below comes from a recorded run.

Most teams start with prompts in code: a template string, or a .txt file next to the code that uses it. That works until a product manager wants to change one sentence, and the change has to wait for a full release.

A prompt registry moves the text out of the deploy. The app fetches the prompt by name and by a label such as production. You change which version the label points to, and the app picks up the new text without a deploy.

LangSmith's prompt hub is the best-known registry, but it is not the only one. Several of the LangSmith alternatives also manage prompts, and three of them are open source and self-hostable: Langfuse, Phoenix and AcruxCore. We covered how those tools handle tracing in Self-Hosted LangSmith Alternatives: 4 Tools Tested on One TypeScript Agent. This article covers prompts only.

Every tool got the same prompt, support-reply. Its template takes three variables: a customer name, a plan, and a list of help articles. It has one condition: a priority line appears only for premium customers. It also has one loop: one line per help article. In Jinja2 syntax, the template looks like this:

One help article contains the text Settings > Export. We included it on purpose, to catch any tool that HTML-escapes > into >.

Every tool then ran the same sequence from a TypeScript script:

Each tool used its own documented TypeScript SDK. The four registries passed all six steps. The rest of this article covers how each one got there, and what it cost.

Keeping prompts in the repository costs almost nothing. The template sits in prompts/support-reply.njk, and nunjucks, the JavaScript port of Jinja2, renders it:

The autoescape: false line matters. Our first version called nunjucks.renderString() directly, and the test failed. The default environment HTML-escapes its output, so Settings > Export reached the model as Settings > Export. Escaping is correct for web pages and wrong for prompts.

Git gives you full Jinja2, code review on every prompt change, and history in the same place as the code. What it can't do is change a prompt in a running app. Every edit, and every rollback, waits for a deploy.

LangSmith stores a prompt as a serialized LangChain object (ChatPromptTemplate), not as plain text. LangSmith's own documentation says you must use the LangChain npm package to pull prompts in TypeScript (Manage prompts programmatically). So a plain TypeScript app that uses the openai package still needs langchain and @langchain/core:

Those three packages installed 23 packages and 70 MB. Most of the size is @langchain/* (27 MB) and js-tiktoken (22 MB).

Templates. In TypeScript, LangChain accepts f-string and mustache templates. Mustache sections ({{#docs}}…{{/docs}}) handled our loop. Mustache has no ==, though, so the premium check became an is_premium boolean computed in code. We also tried templateFormat: "jinja2", which LangChain's Python package supports. In TypeScript it threw DEFAULT_PARSER_MAPPING[templateFormat] is not a function.

Labels and rollback. production and staging are reserved tags, and the documentation moves them in the UI. The langsmith JavaScript client has no public method that moves a tag. Our script called the same REST route the SDK uses internally (POST /api/v1/repos/-//tags). It worked, but it is not a documented API.

Caching. The SDK caches prompts for 300 seconds by default. In our test, a running app saw the new version 300 seconds after we moved the tag.

Langfuse's TypeScript client is one package, @langfuse/client. It installed 5 packages and 8 MB:

Creating a version, moving a label and reading by label are all public SDK calls: prompt.create, prompt.update and prompt.get (Get started with prompt management).

Templates. The documentation shows only {{variable}} substitution. But compile() calls the mustache library, so sections worked for our loop. The type signature accepts only string values, so passing a list needed a cast. The Langfuse UI colours the section tags red, and it lists title and body as the prompt's variables, not docs or is_premium. We would not depend on behaviour the documentation doesn't promise. When we sent the Jinja2 version instead, Langfuse returned the {% if %} and {% for %} tags unchanged, with no error. The model would have received them as text.

Caching. The cache time is 60 seconds by default (Caching). Our app saw the change after 62 seconds. Langfuse is also the only client with a fallback option: a prompt string the SDK uses when it cannot reach the server on a cold start.

Phoenix's TypeScript client fetches a prompt by name and tag, and toSDK() converts it into the message format the openai package expects. Phoenix templates only replace variables, so the condition and the loop have to run in your code before rendering:

That is 16 lines, against 6 to 8 for the other registries. All the extra lines are template logic that now lives in the app.

Templates. Phoenix offers MUSTACHE, F_STRING and NONE formats (Prompts reference). Even in MUSTACHE mode, though, rendering is a plain replacement of {{name}}. When we sent mustache sections, they came back unchanged, with no error.

Errors. getPrompt() catches every error, logs it and returns null. You can see this in the client source, src/utils/getPromptBySelector.ts. A missing prompt and an unreachable server look the same to your code, which is why the snippet checks for null.

Size and caching. The client installed 57 packages and 90 MB. Most of that is OpenTelemetry, which comes in through its @arizeai/phoenix-otel dependency. The client has no prompt cache, so every render is a network call. That is also why a label change showed up immediately: 0 seconds in our test.

AcruxCore stores Jinja2 templates and renders them on the server with nunjucks. The SDK sends the variables and gets back finished messages:

The template from the test section works as it is, condition and loop included, with no is_premium flag computed in code. A label is called an alias here, and promoteAlias() moves it (Manage prompts via the SDK). The SDK installed 3 packages and 12 MB. Most of the 12 MB is one OpenTelemetry package, @opentelemetry/semantic-conventions.

Templates. Jinja2 only. When we committed the mustache version, AcruxCore rejected it at commit time with Template parse error: unexpected token: #. That is a clear error, but it also means that moving mustache prompts from LangSmith or Langfuse requires rewriting them.

We sent the same two templates, a Jinja2 one and a mustache one, to each tool's own renderer:

The rows that matter most are the ones where the tags were left in the output, because those failures produce no error. The model receives raw template syntax, and the only symptom is a strange answer. Whatever tool you pick, test one prompt with logic in it before you move the rest.

Putting logic in the template is not automatically better. It lets someone change the condition without a deploy. It also lets them break the condition without a deploy. Keeping logic in code, as Phoenix forces you to do, puts every branch under code review and in your tests.

A registry is a new runtime dependency, so we tested an outage. The app reached each registry through a local proxy, and we switched the proxy off. A separate process then started while the registry was still unreachable.

LangSmith, Langfuse and AcruxCore keep serving the last good version while the registry is down, even after the cache time has passed. The risk is a cold start: a new container or a serverless function that boots during the outage. Langfuse's fallback option is the built-in answer to that. With the other tools, keep a copy of each prompt in your code as a fallback, which brings back part of the Git setup.

Keep prompts in Git if engineers make every prompt change and your deploys are quick. Set autoescape: false, and you get Jinja2, reviews and history for free.

Use LangSmith's prompt hub if you already use LangChain, or already trace with LangSmith. If your app doesn't use LangChain, budget for the extra dependency and for the tag moves that only the UI supports.

Use Langfuse if you want a small client, a local compile(), and a fallback for cold starts. Keep to {{variable}} substitution unless you are comfortable relying on mustache behaviour that the documentation doesn't promise.

Use Phoenix if you already use it for tracing, or if you prefer all template logic in code. Remember the null check, and that every render is a network call.

Use AcruxCore if you want real conditions and loops in the template and rendering on the server. Expect one network call per new set of variables, and a personal API key for writes.

Before you choose, run one of your own prompts with real logic in it through the tool's SDK, not through its playground. Two of the four registries we tested failed on template syntax without any error, and an SDK run is where you would see that.

#10299. Samsung’s paid Galaxy AI plan has a giant Gemini problem

Good morning. Today I’m trying desperately not to buy Sony’s new nostalgia-bait headphones, reeling from how emotionally invested I was in Spider-Man: Brand New Day (yes, I know I’m late), trying to figure out what a “private, agentic notebook” is, and slowly coming around to the idea of the New York Times owning Letterboxd.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

The relationship between Samsung and Google has, over the years, waxed and waned depending on their respective interdependence. Samsung is Android’s physical emissary, powering a plurality of all its handsets globally. But Google, and in particular its services, is the glue that holds Samsung’s smartphone experience together.

The back and forth is over a decade old; back in early 2014, Samsung shipped a refreshed magazine-style skin with its Galaxy tablets that eschewed many of the design tenets Google had been pushing its partners to adhere to. Not only was Samsung willfully defying its most important partner, but it made a point of barely mentioning Android during its press conferences.

Around the same time, it transitioned its smartwatches from Android Wear to Tizen and began shipping duplicative apps on its devices (many of which persist to this day). The goal was to distance itself from Google as much as possible.

While we don’t know exactly what happened behind closed doors, we saw the outcome quite clearly: Magazine UX lasted one product cycle, and the two companies signed a 10-year cross-license patent deal, which may have helped Samsung prevent any future lawsuits with Apple. Google then sold Motorola to Lenovo, clearing the way for Samsung to continue building its burgeoning hardware business on the back of Google’s mobile ecosystem.

Since then, the two companies have grown even closer. In 2021, they co-announced a new Wear OS along with an overarching wearables partnership that has since grown to include Android XR as well. And Google is still the default search engine for Samsung devices, despite reported inroads made by Microsoft in 2023 to convince the Korean giant to switch to a ChatGPT-powered version of Bing Search. (Samsung’s ongoing relationship with Microsoft, which bundles its Office suite on most Galaxy devices, could be seen as a point of leverage for negotiations with Google, too.)

This brings us to today, and to AI. Samsung was among the first companies to adopt Google’s full suite of Gemini Intelligence products, and its phones often get new AI features before anyone else. But like a decade ago, when Samsung shipped Bixby to kind-of-sort-of compete with Google Assistant, it still wants to offer its own services alongside Google’s.

So a rumor that Samsung may launch a paid Galaxy AI Premium subscription next year for $5/month piqued my interest for the same reason Magazine UX did 12 years ago. Galaxy AI features today are a blend of first-party and third-party capabilities, but none of them are especially useful, particularly when compared to Google’s own Android equivalents powered by Gemini Intelligence. Now Bar and Now Brief, two heavily promoted features in Samsung’s marketing, are wildly disappointing.

At I/O this past May, Google announced Spark, an agent that goes out and performs actions on your behalf. And while it’s since been somewhat eclipsed by more capable mobile options like Meta’s Muse, it’s obvious that Google is working on a follow-up that will perform functions both on the phone and inside apps.

That’s exactly what Samsung is rumored to be working on with Bixby Action, a spiritual successor to its namesake that, when it debuted, prioritized on-device tasks like turning on Wi-Fi over web-based actions Google Assistant did better.

According to leaks from SamMobile, Galaxy AI Premium will offer agentic upgrades for phone users to “create commands triggers and system routines for daily tasks” or “automate routine daily tasks using Galaxy AI.” Gen Video will reportedly “convert static photos into dynamic video clips,” while a new Daily Briefing likely does what it says on the tin.

Will Samsung users see enough value in this hodgepodge of features paywalled only because they use a surfeit of compute? And what models will power them on the backend?

Right now, Google is in the enviable position of being able to offer its AI at three distinct layers: the model itself, which is invisible to the user; the OS level, which can promulgate Gemini across every Android phone that runs Google services; and the hardware level, where Pixel lets it specialize and optimize.

Samsung, not particularly known for its software prowess, will likely need to step gingerly around Google’s AI shadow while also justifying the cost to shore up its much-needed services revenue. Based on this leaked roadmap, though, I’m not confident it will work out.

Instead, Samsung may want to acquire one of the many up-and-coming AI agent startups whose valuations are rising more quickly than their user bases. While Muse and OpenAI’s Dots have taken up most of the recent conversation, I’ve been using Hark, Town, and Instinct pretty regularly for a month or so and could see them being brought in-house by Samsung and offered either as standalone products or customized for Galaxy devices. As Google is doing with its own AI plans, Samsung could provide Galaxy users a year of free service, after which they would be expected to pay.

Samsung doesn’t have a great history of building software, but many of its acquisitions, from SmartThings to Viv Labs (which became “next-gen” Bixby) to LoopPay (which became Samsung Pay) to Whisk (which became Samsung Food), have proven successful. Given the current urgent state of mobile AI, I’d forgo the hodgepodge and bring in some outside expertise.

Jensen Huang’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy is the only thing tech media can talk about right now, likely because it has implications far beyond just Nvidia’s stock price. The Nvidia CEO, who is one of the world’s richest and most powerful tech executives, is believed to have been targeted by Chinese intelligence to potentially influence American trade policy around AI.

Alex Kantrowitz’s Big Technology piece is a useful starting point for why this matters at a global scale:

Beyond the affair, the real issue is that Huang’s love interest, ex-Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang, is someone the United States government believes passed information to the Chinese government while building data centers with Nvidia’s help in Asia. There’s simply no getting away from this detail. This isn’t just an affair, it’s a corporate and national security issue.

Whether Huang can survive the impending investigation by Nvidia’s board remains to be seen, but the CEO has been a stalwart in the tech space for 33 years, and there is no clear successor to his position should he be forced to resign.

Damien reviewed DroidDeck, which I wrote about last week as part of a broader look at Android’s gaming renaissance.

Over the last few days, things have become even more preposterous: the Ocarina of Time remake, which was released on Thursday, has already been recompiled to run natively on Android (though it runs better on PC), largely with the help of frontier AI models.

All of these AI-powered shenanigans come after Anthropic said it would stop making it possible to recreate copies of games, claiming that “recreating someone else’s specific game using Claude to infringe IP is against our usage policy.” Good thing there are no other models capable of doing the same thing.

I think when you zoom out and look at production, AI really has the potential to be the biggest creative unlock in modern history. The last time we saw anything close to this was really the, you know, advent of CGI and digital composites in the early ’90s. That technology gave birth to amazing stories like Jurassic Park and Terminator 2, and it’s hard to imagine that AI is not going to have a more significant impact than that. And I personally believe that’s going to be an incredible unlock on creativity.

—David Ellison, Skydance CEO, when asked about how AI could impact the movie-making process

Thanks for reading, and have a great day. I’ll talk to you on Thursday.

#10294. Technical Aspects of AI-Generated Video Assets for Web Design

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI-generated videos are becoming an important part of modern web design. Designers can now create animated backgrounds, product demonstrations, hero section videos, and visual storytelling elements without relying entirely on traditional video production. However, generating a visually appealing video is only one part of the process. To use AI-generated videos effectively on a website, developers and designers must also understand video formats, visual quality, file optimization, and website performance.

These technical factors directly influence how videos appear across devices, how quickly pages load, and how smoothly visitors interact with a website.

Video format selection is one of the first technical decisions when preparing AI-generated assets for the web. Different formats offer different levels of compression, compatibility, and visual quality.

For most websites, MP4 with H.264 remains a dependable default. WebM or AV1 can be offered as additional formats where appropriate.

Developers should also consider browser compatibility before selecting a format. Providing multiple sources allows browsers to choose a supported option.

AI-generated videos can sometimes contain visual inconsistencies, unnatural movements, distorted objects, or changes in lighting between frames. These issues become more noticeable when videos are displayed in large website sections.

Resolution is an important factor in maintaining visual quality. Common resolutions include:

However, higher resolution does not automatically mean better website performance or user experience. A 4K video may look impressive, but loading it unnecessarily can increase bandwidth consumption.

Frame rate also affects the viewing experience. Many website videos work well at 24 or 30 frames per second. Higher frame rates may be useful for fast-moving scenes, but they also increase the amount of visual data that needs to be processed.

Designers should review AI-generated footage before publishing it. Checking transitions, motion consistency, sharpness, and color accuracy helps prevent distracting visual problems.

Large video files can significantly increase website loading times. This is especially important for websites with video backgrounds, animated landing pages, and multiple visual sections.

Video optimization involves reducing file size without making the footage noticeably worse.

Some practical optimization techniques include:

Compression: Video compression reduces unnecessary data while preserving important visual details. Developers can use encoding tools such as FFmpeg to adjust bitrate, resolution, and codec settings.

Bitrate control: Bitrate determines how much data is used to represent each second of video. A higher bitrate generally preserves more detail but increases file size. Selecting an appropriate bitrate depends on the video's resolution, movement, and intended display size.

Trimming unnecessary footage: Website videos rarely need to be several minutes long. Removing unused sections helps reduce file size and keeps visual content focused.

Removing unnecessary audio: Background videos that do not require sound should be exported without an audio track. This reduces unnecessary data and supports a cleaner user experience.

Responsive video delivery: Websites should serve appropriately sized assets for different screen sizes. A smaller video may be sufficient for mobile users, while desktop visitors can receive a higher-resolution version.

These techniques help developers balance visual quality with efficient content delivery.

Even a well-optimized video can affect website performance if it is implemented incorrectly. Developers should consider how videos are loaded, displayed, and processed by browsers.

For example, automatically loading several large videos when a page opens can consume significant bandwidth. Instead, developers can use lazy loading strategies for videos that appear further down the page.

For background videos, the HTML video element offers several useful attributes:

Developers should also avoid autoplaying large videos unnecessarily. A static image can often provide a better experience on mobile devices or slower connections.

Performance testing tools such as Google Lighthouse and Chrome DevTools can help identify whether video assets are affecting loading speed, bandwidth usage, or rendering performance.

Technical optimization should not come at the expense of accessibility. Background videos should not distract users or make website content difficult to understand.

Developers should respect the prefers-reduced-motion media query, which allows users who prefer reduced animation to receive a less motion-intensive experience.

A suitable static background image can be displayed as an alternative.

Mobile compatibility is equally important. Smaller screens, limited data plans, and varying network conditions can make heavy video assets less practical. Testing videos on different devices and connection speeds helps identify potential problems before publication.

AI-generated video assets offer exciting possibilities for modern web design, but their successful implementation requires more than creative output. Choosing suitable video formats, maintaining consistent visual quality, compressing files, and considering website performance are all essential parts of the development process.

By combining thoughtful video production with responsive delivery, accessibility considerations, and performance testing, designers and developers can create engaging websites without compromising loading speed or usability. As AI video technology continues to evolve, understanding these technical fundamentals will remain valuable for building efficient and visually appealing web experiences.

#10293. Step by Step guide: Build a Voice Agent That Never Goes Silent using ADK

A guide to streaming tools, non-blocking function calls, and response scheduling in the Agent Development Kit (ADK) Gemini Live API Toolkit.

In text chat, a 13-second database query or RAG pipeline shows a loading spinner. In a live voice conversation, 13 seconds of blocking tool execution produces 13 seconds of dead air, causing users to assume the call dropped and hang up.

You cannot always make backend queries sub-second, so the fix must be architectural: keep the agent conversational while slow tools run in the background. Rather than forcing you to wire up custom client-side WebSocket event loops to manage asynchronous tool state, the open-source ADK and gemini-3.8-live let you implement non-blocking voice tools natively in Python. In our benchmark sales-report workflow on gemini-3.8-live, switching from a blocking tool to a streaming tool cut the time to the agent's first words from 14.4 seconds to 1.2 seconds.

This guide covers how to use streaming tools, non-blocking FunctionTool definitions, and response_scheduling to build responsive, natural voice agents.

Watch the 1-minute ADK Live AI Concierge demo video to hear scheduled responses in action:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Implementing non-blocking tool calls in ADK provides several immediate user experience benefits:

In standard agent development, a tool is defined as an async def function that completes its work and returns a single value:

When passed directly to an Agent in ADK, this function creates a blocking tool call. During a Gemini Live API bidirectional voice session (run_live), the execution loop awaits the tool's return before generating further model turns.

If query_sales_database takes 13 seconds, the agent produces 13 seconds of complete silence. Prompting instructions such as "tell the user you are working on it" cannot resolve this, because the model's turn is blocked waiting for the function response payload.

To keep the conversation fluid, you can choose between two main non-blocking patterns depending on the desired user experience.

When an operation consists of meaningful stages (such as connecting to a database, aggregating records, and formatting results), a streaming tool lets the agent narrate progress as it happens.

In ADK, turning a standard tool into a streaming tool requires changing only return to yield. By defining the function as an AsyncGenerator, ADK automatically marks the tool declaration as NON_BLOCKING and returns an immediate status acknowledgement to the model.

Continuous progress commentary is not always appropriate. If a user asks for a report and immediately pivots to another topic, an agent that continually interrupts with status updates can feel intrusive.

When the wait has no story to narrate, run the task quietly in the background.

An effective way to get an immediate verbal acknowledgment without ongoing progress chatter is an async generator that yields only once, upon completion:

Because this is an async generator, ADK issues an immediate pending response. The agent promptly acknowledges the request ("I'm pulling that report together for you now"), remains silent during computation, and presents the figures once ready or when the user finishes speaking.

If you prefer to keep standard coroutines (async def ... -> dict), wrap the function in a FunctionTool and configure response_scheduling:

Understanding the Trade-Off: When using response_scheduling with a return-once coroutine, the model receives no payload until the entire function completes. As a result, the model typically does not utter an initial verbal acknowledgment unless the user continues speaking. This pattern is ideal when the user immediately continues with other questions and you want the result woven into the dialogue at the next natural pause.

The response_scheduling attribute controls when the model reacts to an incoming tool response. WHEN_IDLE never cuts into a turn in progress: if the model is still speaking or the user is still talking, the result waits until that turn ends, and the model responds at the next natural pause. ADK supports three scheduling behaviors:

In benchmark sessions with gemini-3.8-live:

Execution shape (streaming vs. single-return) and delivery timing (response_scheduling) are independent and can be composed. Setting response_scheduling on a streaming tool stamps every partial update with that behavior.

Furthermore, in ADK 2.8 and newer, streaming tools can dynamically specify scheduling per update by yielding types.FunctionResponse:

The rule of thumb: If the wait has a story, stream it. If the wait is just a wait, run it in the background and let the result land when idle.

Non-blocking tools do not reduce backend execution time: a 13-second query still takes 13 seconds. What changes is the conversational presence of your agent.

A web application that takes ten seconds to load is perceived as slow; a conversational voice agent that goes silent for ten seconds is perceived as broken. By pairing ADK's streaming tools and flexible response scheduling with the real-time capabilities of gemini-3.8-live, you can build voice assistants that remain attentive, natural, and present throughout complex operations.

Try it yourself: the interactive demo runs the same 13-second query through every tool pattern and response_scheduling mode on gemini-3.8-live, so you can hear the difference.

#10288. Best AI Humanizer Tools Tested and Compared: 7 Options for Web Content

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The best AI humanizer for web content is the one that changes how a draft reads without changing what it says. If you need a specific tone, Humanize Max gives you four styles and three lengths in one run. If you already work in QuillBot or Grammarly, their humanizers fit the account you have. GPTinf, Humanize AI and Undetectable AI put an AI check next to the rewrite, and Ahrefs gives you quick variants of a short passage.

To compare them on equal terms, we ran the same 103-word AI-written release note through each tool in November 2026. It contains a product name, two image formats, a settings path and the word "must", so it shows whether a tool keeps the details a reader acts on. We also checked every feature, limit and price on the vendor's own pages on the same day.

A humanizer earns its place only if its output needs fewer fixes than the draft you started with, and these six checks show whether a tool will do that for your content.

Humanize Max is a single-purpose humanizer: you paste an AI draft, upload a file or load the built-in sample, choose a style and a length, and get a rewritten version in a few seconds. The four styles are Academic, Casual, Creative and Formal, and the three lengths are Shorten, Standard and Expand, so you can turn a stiff feature description into casual blog copy and trim it in the same run.

The result highlights every phrase it changed, which makes it quick to check whether a product name or a settings path came through. If the rewrite doesn't land, Run it again produces a different version of the whole text. At the time of writing, it takes up to 2,000 words per run and works without an account.

Pricing: Plans and limits are still being finalized, so check the site for current terms.

Best for: Rewriting AI drafts of blog posts, product pages and emails into a specific tone.

QuillBot's AI Humanizer sits next to its paraphraser, grammar checker and AI detector, which makes it a natural pick if you already use QuillBot for rewording. Basic mode is free for up to 125 words per run, while Advanced mode, which rewrites more deeply, comes with Premium (the pricing page also lists three Advanced uses on the free plan). In our test, it asked us to create a free account before it showed the first result.

Premium removes the word limit and adds Freeze words, which keeps the terms you choose unchanged, along with tone insights, a history of past runs and a Chrome extension that humanizes text inside ChatGPT and Gemini.

Pricing: Basic mode is free. Premium is listed at $8.33 a month, billed annually.

Best for: Quick fixes to a paragraph or two for writers who already use QuillBot.

GPTinf is a writing toolkit built around its humanizer, with an AI detector, plagiarism checker, grammar checker, paraphraser and readability checker on the same site. The humanizer has two modes, Standard and Academic, and takes pasted text or a PDF, Word or plain-text file. File-to-File mode returns the humanized document in its original format, so a long brief keeps its layout.

Custom Style lets you paste 150 to 1,200 words of your own writing, and later runs are shaped to match it, with no account needed. After each run, an AI-likelihood estimate appears under the output, so you can see whether a draft needs another pass. Treat that number as a probability, not proof of who wrote the text.

Pricing: A free plan is available. Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $24.99 and Unlimited at $59.99.

Best for: Freelancers and content teams that want to rewrite and check drafts without switching tools.

Grammarly's AI Humanizer comes in two layers. The tool on its web page is free, works without an account and offers basic humanization of pasted text or an uploaded document. A free Grammarly account adds the Humanizer agent, with four preset styles, a custom voice built from a writing sample, and support for English, Spanish, French, German, Portuguese and Italian.

Grammarly says the humanizer focuses on clarity and readability rather than on AI detectors, which matches how most web teams use these tools. If you need to show how a piece was written, Grammarly Authorship shows which parts came from AI, which came from online sources and which you wrote yourself. The basic tool's word counter allows up to 1,500 words per run.

Pricing: The basic humanizer is free. Grammarly Pro is listed at $12 on the plans page, which doesn't state the billing period.

Best for: Teams that already write in Grammarly and want one voice across documents.

Humanize AI is built for people who want to tune the output. Without an account you get the Free tone, a Rewrite level slider with three stops (Quality, Balanced and Max) and Custom style, which learns your voice from 150 to 1,200 words of samples. A free account opens eight more modes, including Formal, Informal, Academic, Expand and Shorten, and on desktop a Writing level menu sets how plain or dense the result reads.

View changes highlights every edit, and File-to-File accepts PDF, DOCX, TXT and MD documents and returns them in the same format. Paid plans also include a public API that supports the same styles and rewrite levels as the web app, so a developer can add a humanizing step to a CMS or publishing pipeline.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (10,000 words), with Standard at $19.99 and Unlimited at $59.99. Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Teams that need precise tone control or want to humanize text through an API.

Undetectable AI keeps its humanizer simple: one text box, one button and no style or length settings. Its free allowance works differently from the others, with 1,000 words in total before you need an account and another 1,000 once you create a free one.

Under the output, a Check for AI button runs a detection check on the result, and the same site has a separate AI detector and plagiarism checker. Its FAQ says pasted text isn't published or shared but doesn't say whether it's stored, so keep confidential details out of the drafts you run through it.

Pricing: Paid plans start at $9.99 a month for Lite (5,000 words), with Pro at $25 (25,000 words) and Unlimited at $59 (1,000,000 words). Yearly billing lowers the monthly price.

Best for: Trying a humanizer on a full-length draft before you commit to an account.

Ahrefs, best known for its SEO platform, offers a free AI Text Humanizer among its writing tools. You paste up to 2,048 characters, choose one, three or five variants, and pick the version that reads best. It ran without an account in our test.

Variants are the useful part. For a hero headline, a meta description or a product blurb, comparing three rewrites side by side is faster than prompting a chatbot three times, and you can export all results as a text file. The character limit, which fits a few short paragraphs, rules it out for full articles.

Best for: Short marketing copy where you want several options to choose from.

The best AI humanizer tool for a documentation team isn't the one a growth marketer needs, so match the tool to the content in front of you.

There isn't one winner, because these tools solve different problems. The quickest way to decide is to run a paragraph from your own work, ideally one with a product name, a number and an instruction, through two or three of the tools above, and keep the one whose output you'd publish with the fewest fixes.

Google's guidance on using generative AI content is about value rather than the tool: mass-producing pages that add nothing for readers can count as scaled content abuse. A humanizer changes phrasing, not usefulness, so the same rules apply. Google also recommends fact-checking AI-generated titles, meta descriptions and image alt text before you publish.

The best humanizer for your team is the one whose controls match your content: tone presets for blog posts, variants for short copy, change highlighting for docs and a custom voice for marketing. Run one of your own paragraphs through two or three tools from this list, then read every highlighted change, especially names, numbers and words like "must", before anything goes live.

#10282. Why CTF Competitions May Be More Important Than Ever

Cybersecurity In-Depth: Feature articles on security strategy, latest trends, and people to know.

As artificial intelligence squeezes early-career learning opportunities, capture-the-flag contests could be one way to help pick up the slack.

Artificial intelligence (AI) may be gobbling up entry-level jobs and learning opportunities for security practitioners, but it can't erase the value of hands-on experience with enterprise systems and security tools.

No matter how much agentic action cybersecurity teams embed into their security operations center (SOC) and the rest of their programs, smart people have to backstop that work. And the only way these "humans-in-the-loop" can do that capably is if they build up the kind of judgment that's best learned from the school of hard knocks.

The problem? With SOC tier 1 analysts becoming an endangered species and bug bounty programs on the ropes from a glut of AI submissions, the chances are dwindling for security newbies to rack up real-world experience.

There's no great fix to this career pipeline problem just yet, but some of the answers might be found in one of the industry's age-old mechanisms for both camaraderie and skill-building: the trusty capture-the-flag (CTF) competition.

Related:NIST: Solo Shouldn't Mean Less Secure

"CTFs give passionate talent the opportunity to grow and learn in ways that they might not have at their day jobs," says Jason Walker, a security engineer by day at software-as-a-service firm Five9. An avid CTF competitor, he won the Sentinels League competition sponsored and run by SentinelOne, beating two other finalists from a field of thousands of competitors worldwide. Besides street cred, Walker picked up a $5,000 prize for his efforts and a $10,000 donation in his name to his charity of choice, Trans Lifeline.

The livestreamed event offered up the spectacle and fun of an e-sports competition, but it may hold deeper clues as to how the industry can keep refreshing its talent pool in the coming years. As Walker puts it, CTFs are the ultimate way to expose both new and experienced cyber professionals to a wider range of problems and technologies.

"CTFs have helped me be a lot more versatile," he says. "If your company is running well and your program is mature, you aren't going to necessarily be firefighting new things every week. You're not going to be exposed to every new technique and threat. Doing CTF events and other hands-on training is a really important way to make sure you can stay up to date."

CTF competitions come in many shapes and sizes, but they fundamentally challenge players to find hidden pieces of data called "flags" buried inside intentionally vulnerable systems. Most fall into one of two formats: Jeopardy-style events, the kind Sentinels League runs, hand out standalone puzzles across categories such as Web exploitation, forensics, and cryptography, with points awarded per solve. Attack-defense events ask each team to defend its own vulnerable network while trying to break into everyone else's.

Related:Incidents Move Too Fast for Pen & Paper

The attack-defense format took shape at DEF CON's CTF, which started in 1996 and is still the event most associated with it. It has sprung forth a wellspring of CTF formats that range from grassroots industry events to big vendor-sponsored competitions like Sentinels League.

Sentinels League grew out of ThreatOps, a set of internal games SentinelOne's solutions engineers have run since 2020 to teach customers how to hunt through telemetry for hidden flags. This year's championship built on that with four games — Endpoint, Cloud, SIEM, and AI — spread across 12 weeks of qualifiers between June and October

The opening player pool of 2,236 entrants came from more than 100 countries, competing for a total prize pool of $100,000. Smaller prizes were dispensed across the qualifier and regional events, with the bigger prizes kept on reserve for the final, which was held in conjunction with SentinelOne's annual OneCon event.

Ryan O'Grady, director of SE operations at SentinelOne, says the firm invests the money in the prizes and the considerable logistics of running such a big event as a way to give back to the community, to build up expertise and exposure to its platform, and as a recruiting tool.

Related:Behind the Scenes of How Operation Blackout Went Down

Whether motivated for the prizes or the training, the appetite for the event keeps growing. This year's drew enough interest that the company had to get a bigger ballroom to accommodate spectators.

Sentinels League is just one example of what a CTF looks like amid a much broader spectrum of competitions available. On CTFtime, the volunteer-run archive that tracks the hobbyist side of the scene, last year's calendar alone listed more than 200 individually organized events, with active teams from countries spanning every populated continent.

DEF CON CTF, often called the World Series of hacking, pulled 673 teams through its 2026 qualifiers before finals in Las Vegas this August. The National Cyber League, a nonprofit circuit O'Grady competes in, drew more than 7,500 students from 670 schools and universities this past spring. Kaspersky continues to run its own wildly popular SAS CTF out of Bali.

Other more academic events, such as New York University's CSAW and Carnegie Mellon's picoCTF, run versions aimed at students. Larger consulting firms, such as KPMG, use the format to scout corporate recruits.

In spite of the obvious demand for CTFs evident in the number of events held worldwide and the thousands of participants willing to sink their personal time into these pursuits, many security pundits worry about what AI is going to do to the purpose and purity of these competitions.

CTF organizers are thinking deeply about what this means for their rule books and how they enforce them. RITSEC's 2026 rules ban using AI to autonomously solve challenges outright, calling it against the spirit of the competition. Meantime, CSAW leaned into AI, this year creating an entire track built around using large language models (LLMs) to solve past CTF problems.

DEF CON CTF's 2026 qualifier split the difference. It barred fully autonomous AI teams but let human competitors use AI tools as long as a person stayed meaningfully in charge, requiring every team to label itself No AI, Low AI, or Human-Led AI on the public scoreboard.

A recent study of hundreds of Hack The Box machines released between 2017 and 2025 found solve times dropped roughly 16% a year, with the steepest declines after 2022. The hardest tier of challenges went from a 15-hour median solve down to about five, according to penetration tester Jacob Krell of Suzu Labs, who did the analysis.

He's not the only one worried. An academic study earlier this year from IBM Research and the College of William & Mary pitted 41 participants in a live, on-site CTF against four autonomous AI agents. It found that those agents placed second overall, ahead of most of the human teams competing.

Findings like these are enough to get firms like OtterSec, a blockchain security firm, to make noise about how to "save" CTFs. This summer the firm launched a $100,000 "Save CTFs Fund" to bankroll new formats that stay meaningful as AI keeps improving.

SentinelOne's O'Grady doesn't buy the death-of-CTFs talk. In his view, AI just forces organizers to redesign CTFs so they still force a human to think laterally, AI assistance or not.

AI may not be killing creative CTFs anytime soon, but it is definitely making cybersecurity's career ladder wobble. In the process it may actually be making CTFs even more crucial.

SANS Institute polled nearly 1,000 security leaders and found unclear career progression to be one of the top hiring challenges, cited by 32%, more than triple the rate last year. Only one-quarter of organizations say they have a clearly defined career path, and entry-level jobs that used to train the next generation of defenders are diminishing. SOC and security analyst roles lead the AI-driven cuts, down 32%. Threat intelligence analysts are next, at 26%, with incident responders close behind, at 22%.

Bug bounty programs are buckling under AI's pressure. At the start of the year, maintainers of the open source project Curl shut down their program after a massive influx of junk AI submissions. HackerOne paused its Internet Bug Bounty program in April after a wave of low-quality, AI-assisted reports flooded in. And this month Google Bug Hunters also paused its open source bounty program due to similar problems.

These are all blows for cyber career progression, as bug bounty problems were another great place for security professionals to flex their skills publicly and for newcomers to gain a foothold into the industry.

As long as CTF organizers can keep their competitions relevant, these events will remain a valuable puzzle piece in the skills training picture for the industry, alongside other important hands-on challenges like cyber ranges.

"With LLMs basically sweeping the table on bug bounties, it's become even harder than it already was for people to get out there, show off their skills, and get their name out there," O'Grady says. "AI has only increased the importance of CTF events like this."

An award-winning freelance reporter with nearly two decades of experience covering B2B cybersecurity, Ericka Chickowski has tackled every angle of security and risk for Dark Reading since 2009. Her coverage has spanned the full range of cyber topics important to every kind of cybersecurity stakeholder, including cybersecurity practitioners, CISOs, security-minded business managers, and security-aware board directors. She specializes in going deep on topics, breaking down how the technology trends affect real people, and how risk impacts the bottom line.

Some special areas of focus include: adversarial AI; AI risk and resilience; AI and agentic governance; application security (AppSec); governance, risk, and compliance (GRC); cloud security; network security; software supply chain risk; cyberinsurance; DevSecOps; enterprise risk management; and CISO career development.

In addition to Dark Reading, Ericka has written for many different IT and business publications over the years. Her byline has appeared in dozens of trade and consumer magazines, including Baseline, Consumer Digest, Channel Insider, CIO Insight, CSOOnline.com, DevOps.com, eWeek, Entrepreneur, Forbes, InformationWeek, SC Magazine, and SecurityBoulevard. She regularly contributes independent stories about AppSec and supply chain risk to the ReversingLabs Blog. She was one of the founding contributing editors for DevOps.com and has deep expertise in digital transformation and development best practices. This helps inform her stories on software risks and business resilience.

Ericka is also a prolific executive ghost writer, with experience helping cybersecurity and IT leaders bring their ideas to life. She's helped them craft important position pieces for respected publications like the Financial Times and The Wall Street Journal.

Prior to beginning her freelance career, Ericka served in a number of editorial staff roles, including West Coast Bureau Chief for SC Magazine. Before that position, she was editor-in-chief for a cluster of regional technology and lifestyle magazines in the Seattle area, as well as a beat reporter for a regional business newspaper. She holds a bachelor's degree in English from the University of Washington. Her work is well-regarded and has won multiple awards from the Society of Professional Journalists.
