#10249. How Text-to-Image AI Generators Work, and 5 Tools That Deliver Good Results

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Type “a lighthouse on a cliff at dusk, oil painting, warm light” into a text box, wait ten seconds, and a picture that never existed appears. It feels like magic, but it is a well-understood pipeline of language models, neural networks and a great deal of math. Knowing how that pipeline works makes you a better prompter, helps you diagnose bad results, and makes it easier to choose the right tool for a job.

This guide explains how text-to-image generators turn words into pixels, why they fail in predictable ways, and how to write prompts that work with the model instead of against it. It then compares five tools that consistently produce good results, starting with Pollo AI, and finishes with a practical workflow, a troubleshooting table and an FAQ. Plans, credits and model versions change often, so confirm current details on each vendor’s site before you commit to a subscription.

Early image generators were mostly GANs, or generative adversarial networks. A GAN pits two networks against each other: a generator that produces images and a discriminator that tries to spot fakes. GANs could produce sharp faces and textures, but they were hard to train, often collapsed into repeating the same few outputs, and were poor at following free-form text.

A second line of research used autoregressive models, which generate an image piece by piece the way a language model generates words. These handled text prompts better but were slow at high resolution.

The shift came with diffusion models. Researchers showed that a network trained to reverse a gradual noising process could generate images with excellent quality and diversity, and that it was far more stable to train than a GAN. Combine that with text conditioning and a compressed image representation, and you get the systems behind most of today’s tools. Newer models increasingly swap the traditional convolutional network for transformers, but the core idea of iterative denoising is still the foundation.

Under the hood, mainstream generators combine three ideas: a way to understand text, a way to represent images compactly, and a way to generate them gradually. The sections below follow a prompt from keystroke to finished picture.

Neural networks cannot read words, so your prompt is first split into tokens, small chunks of text such as whole words or word fragments. A text encoder then converts those tokens into an embedding, a grid of numbers that captures meaning and context. Many systems use encoders trained jointly on images and their captions, so that the text “a red bicycle” and pictures of red bicycles end up close together in the same mathematical space. Some newer systems add a large language model to understand longer, more detailed instructions.

This is why wording matters. The encoder only sees what you wrote, and it has a limited capacity. A short, specific prompt produces a clear signal. A long, rambling one dilutes each instruction.

Instead of drawing on a blank canvas, the model begins with random static. The pattern of that static is controlled by a seed number. The same prompt with a different seed gives a different image, because it starts from different noise. If a tool lets you fix the seed, you can make results repeatable and change just one variable at a time.

During training, engineers take millions of real images and add noise to them in small steps until only static remains. The network is then trained to predict the noise that was added at each step, which teaches it to reverse the process. At generation time, the model applies that skill to pure noise, removing a little at each step. Because it has learned what real images look like, the “noise” it removes gradually reveals something that looks plausible.

Your text embedding is fed into the network through a mechanism called cross-attention. At each step, parts of the evolving image can “look at” relevant parts of the prompt and adjust accordingly. A region that will become a lighthouse attends to the word “lighthouse”. A region that will become the sky attends to “dusk”.

A setting called classifier-free guidance, often shown as guidance scale or prompt strength, controls how strongly each step is pulled toward the prompt. The model computes one prediction with the prompt and one without it, then exaggerates the difference. A low value gives the model freedom and often more natural, varied results. A high value follows the words more literally but can produce oversaturated, over-sharpened or distorted images.

Running diffusion directly on full-resolution pixels would be very expensive. The idea behind Stable Diffusion, known as latent diffusion, is to compress images into a much smaller latent representation using an autoencoder. Denoising happens in that compact space, and a decoder turns the final latent back into a full image. This is a major reason high-quality generation now runs in seconds on a cloud GPU instead of minutes.

The number of denoising steps and the scheduler, which decides how much noise to remove at each step, shape the speed and look of the result. More steps usually add detail up to a point, then give diminishing returns. Many tools hide these settings, but the trade-off is always the same: fewer steps are faster and cheaper, more steps can look more refined.

Most pipelines finish with an upscaler or refinement pass that sharpens detail and fixes small artifacts. Many tools also offer inpainting, which regenerates only a selected region, outpainting, which extends the canvas, and variations, which restart from a related image.

A generator can only produce what it has learned to represent. Models are trained on very large collections of images paired with captions, and the quality, diversity and licensing of that data affect both the output and the legal picture. Models trained on more photographs tend to produce convincing photorealism. Models tuned on illustration and concept art lean toward painterly looks.

This also explains a few behaviors. Common subjects look great because the model has seen thousands of examples. Rare or very specific subjects, such as an unusual machine part or a niche cultural item, often look generic or wrong. Biases in the data can show up as stereotypes, for example in how “a doctor” or “a CEO” is depicted, so it helps to describe the people and setting you actually want.

Understanding the process explains the familiar failures:

Pollo AI’s text to image AI is a browser-based generator built around a simple loop: write a prompt, pick a style, generate, and download. Its page highlights HD output and a wide range of looks, from photorealistic scenes to cute cartoons, anime, pencil sketches, isometric art, abstract compositions and surreal visuals. Files can be downloaded as WebP, PNG or JPEG, a practical spread for web work: WebP keeps pages light, PNG preserves clean edges and transparency-friendly graphics, and JPEG works almost everywhere.

Pollo is also part of a wider platform that covers AI video, so a still image you like can become the starting point for motion without switching ecosystems. That matters for teams producing social content, because one workflow can cover both the image and the short clip built from it.

Things to check: the page does not list specific model names or credit amounts, so review the current plan details before relying on it for high-volume work. Commercial use requires a paid plan.

Best for: quick, versatile image generation across many styles, especially if you also plan to make video.

Midjourney has built its reputation on strong aesthetics. Images tend to arrive with polished composition, dramatic lighting and a distinctive painterly feel, which is why it is popular for concept art, mood boards, album-style visuals and editorial illustration. It offers style references and personalization, so you can steer results toward a consistent look across a project. It also supports editing and variation tools for refining a chosen image.

The trade-offs are practical. It is a paid service, and its prompts reward experimentation over literal instruction following, so you sometimes get something beautiful that is not exactly what you described.

Best for: artistic, high-impact visuals where mood matters more than exact specification.

Adobe Firefly focuses on professional workflows and commercial confidence. Adobe describes Firefly’s own models as trained on licensed content such as Adobe Stock and public-domain material, and it attaches Content Credentials metadata that records AI involvement. It connects with tools many teams already use, including Photoshop features like generative fill and generative expand, which suits designers who edit as much as they generate.

Firefly also provides controls such as style and composition references. Its results can feel cleaner and more commercial than Midjourney’s, which is an advantage for marketing assets and a limitation if you want a wild artistic look.

Best for: marketing and design teams that need editing tools and clearer licensing.

Ideogram is known for rendering legible text inside images, a task where many generators struggle. That makes it a strong choice for posters, social graphics, logo concepts, packaging mockups and product visuals that need a headline or short phrase to read correctly. It also offers style controls and a community gallery that doubles as a prompt library.

Even so, long paragraphs of on-image text can still go wrong. Keep the copy to a few words, put it in quotation marks in the prompt, and describe the lettering style.

Best for: graphics that combine imagery with short, readable text.

Leonardo AI is built for creators who want more control. It offers multiple models, fine-tuning options, image-to-image guidance and a canvas for editing, with a particular following among game artists and designers who need consistent characters and assets. Features such as reference-based generation help keep a character recognizable across many scenes.

The wide set of options is powerful but means a steeper learning curve than a one-box generator, and credit systems can take time to understand.

Best for: consistent characters, game and concept assets, and users who like tuning settings.

A reliable prompt has five parts. Put the most important part first, because it often carries the most weight.

How do text-to-image generators work? They convert your prompt into a numerical representation, then use a diffusion model to remove noise from a random starting image step by step, guided by that representation, until a matching picture appears.

Which text-to-image tool is best? It depends on the job. Pollo AI suits fast, varied generation, Midjourney suits artistic work, Adobe Firefly suits commercial design, Ideogram suits images with text and Leonardo AI suits tuned, consistent assets.

Why does the same prompt give different results? Each run starts from a different random seed. Fixing the seed, where a tool allows it, makes results more repeatable.

Can I use AI-generated images commercially? Often yes, but the rules differ by tool and plan. Pollo, for example, notes that commercial use requires a paid plan. Always check the current terms.

How do I get readable text in an AI image? Use a tool strong at text, keep the wording to a few words, and add the text in a design editor if accuracy is critical.

What file format should I download? WebP for lightweight web pages, PNG for graphics and sharp edges, JPEG for broad compatibility.

Can I turn a generated image into a video? Many platforms support it. Pollo AI, for example, also covers AI video, so you can move from a still to motion in one place.

Text-to-image generators work by turning language into numbers and using those numbers to guide a model that denoises random static into a picture. Once you understand that, prompts stop being guesswork: you give the model a clear subject, style and lighting, you iterate one change at a time, and you fix small flaws locally. Start with a versatile option such as Pollo AI, then add Midjourney, Firefly, Ideogram or Leonardo when a project needs their particular strengths.

#10244. Anthropic-Themed Phishing Emails Bypass Top Security Tools

Obvious phishing emails with numerous red flags are somehow getting past some of the industry-leading secure email gateway (SEG) products.

Attackers are managing to sneak low-grade phishing emails through brand name email security products, as evidenced by a recent Anthropic impersonation campaign.

The campaign, described in a new blog post by Cofense, won't impress anybody. Since September, an unidentified threat actor has been sending out emails around the world, pretending to grant early access to Anthropic's cutting-edge artificial intelligence (AI) technology. Their goal: stealing Google and Microsoft account credentials.

To avoid being relegated to spam folders, the attacker has been using novice tactics: shortening their malicious URLs, adding CAPTCHAs to their phishing flow, and leveraging legitimate video conferencing tools. The only remarkable thing about the campaign, really, is that it actually seems to be working.

Even as far as phishing campaigns go, this one's pretty lazy.

Related:CISA Announces Enhanced Reporting Form for Cyber Incidents

Attackers are appealing to targets with VIP invites to a "private preview" of Anthropic's supposed "new social media AI tool." Emails emphasize that this special early look is "private, invitation-only."

Right off the bat, there are ample reasons to be suspicious. The email titles have a grammatical error. The email sender's name includes a typo — "Austin from nthropic" — and their domain belongs to GoTo Webinar, not Anthropic. Visually, the messages are barebones, possessing no visual elements that even attempt to impersonate Anthropic branding.

If for some reason someone actually clicks on the fake registration link — a link that doesn't point to an Anthropic or even a conferencing app domain — they'll end up on a fake Zoom meeting page. If, further, the victim doesn't catch that a GoTo Webinar invite led to a Zoom call, they might choose to sign into Zoom via Google or Microsoft Single-Sign On (SSO). If they choose Google, they won't even be prompted for a password, thus making half of this malicious campaign completely useless, even to the attacker running it.

Inconsistent brand impersonations and missing or broken data theft flows are endemic to AI-generated campaigns; Cofense couldn't confirm if AI was used here. "However, due to the multiple variations of Zoom pages, it's likely generative AI was used," Cofense researcher Jacob Malimban says.

He points to one bit of additional evidence: Beginning Oct. 28, the threat actor began spoofing Anthropic's website instead of Zoom's. The similar but slightly off format of the Anthropic spoofing page, he says, also suggests AI generation.

Related:Operação de influência russa tem como alvo generalizado a América Latina

The overall package is so maladroit that one can only wonder how such an email bypassed any spam filter in the first place. In fact, Cofense found these emails getting through market-leading email security products like the Proofpoint Secure Email Gateway (SEG) and Microsoft Defender for Office 365. Cofense pointed to a few little details that might help explain why.

First, the emails are sent from a legitimate email address, customercare@gotowebinar[.]com — a shared sender address that any GoTo Webinar user might use to distribute webinar information. The legitimacy and ubiquity of this shared address shields the sender from scrutiny.

Second, the malicious registration link in the emails was run through a URL shortener, Zapier, eliminating a security product's ability to interpret red flags in the domain itself.

Lastly, the phishing flow incorporates two separate CAPTCHAs. The first — a Cloudflare CAPTCHA, presented before the fake Zoom or Anthropic page — is meant to stop an automated scanner in its tracks.

The second CAPTCHA — placed between the fake Zoom or Anthropic page and the fake SSO page — is a malicious twist on the form, designed to block VPN services, Tor browsers, and other tools defenders might use. "Some phish kits used that specific type of CAPTCHA before. It appears to have been actively developed by threat actors to detect the tools of both automated and human activity, as well as see if the IP address was proxied," Malimban explains.

Related:Philippine Migrant Worker Data Likely Leaked in 69GB Government Breach

None of these tactics will knock anyone's socks off but, in the gestalt, they seem to do the trick.

"The reality of modern cybersecurity is that Secure Email Gateways (SEGs) will always have blind spots," he says.

Barney adds that organizations should assume phishing emails will end up in employees' email inboxes, and therefore security teams need to build defenses that focus on identity.

"If an employee is tricked by a fake Anthropic lure and their Microsoft or Google credentials are compromised, strict least-privilege access policies, phishing-resistant multifactor authentication and privileged access management are what prevent the attacker from moving laterally, elevating privileges and accessing sensitive data," he advises. Lastly, he adds, "Utilizing a centralized password manager that validates domain URLs before autofilling credentials provides a critical last line of defense against the lookalike domains used in this attack, neutralizing the threat at the point of interaction."

In the spirit of Cybersecurity Awareness Month 2026, and its emphasis on avoiding and reporting phishing scams, Malimban adds, "We hope that employees are trained using Secure Behavior Management to recognize and report phishing. To defend against suspicious account access, organizations should have robust account monitoring, like with CISA's free tool (Logging Made Easy). All in all, having multiple controls and defense in depth is necessary to defend against modern attacks."

Then he transitioned to journalism at Threatpost, where he covered cybersecurity news and trends. #10241. Building Reliable Product Emails Into Your Application

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A password reset email is part of your login flow. An invoice notification is part of your billing experience. Yet development teams often treat these messages as background tasks: render a template, call a sending API, and move on. That approach leaves an important part of the product experience untested until a user cannot complete the action they started.

Testing the application interface alone leaves a gap. A successful request to an email provider does not establish that the recipient can find the message, understand it, or use its links. Product emails need explicit success criteria, useful diagnostic records, and recovery options when something fails.

A reliable workflow combines application tests, controlled mailbox checks, and pre-send diagnostics for authentication and inbox placement. A comparison of available tools can help you identify which checks your existing setup covers and where additional testing would be useful. The goal is to investigate specific gaps rather than add another service without a clear purpose.

Start with the action the recipient needs to complete. A weekly activity summary can tolerate a delay that would make a sign-in code useless. An invoice needs accurate amounts and a clear payment destination. A security alert needs enough context for someone to recognize the event without exposing sensitive information.

For each message, document its trigger, expected timing, required data, and expiration behavior. Decide what the application should display if submission fails or the user requests another copy. These decisions give developers and testers concrete acceptance criteria.

For a password reset, test the complete journey: requesting the message, receiving it in a controlled mailbox, following the link, and setting a new password. Also check what happens when the link expires or has already been used.

Template tests should cover the data your application actually encounters. Try long names, missing optional fields, non-Latin characters, and large order totals. Escape user-provided values appropriately, and make sure conditional sections disappear cleanly when their data is absent.

Inspect both HTML and plain-text versions. Instructions should remain understandable when images are blocked, while descriptive links and readable text should make the next action clear. Check that every URL points to the correct environment, especially when staging and production share template code.

A development sandbox lets you inspect generated messages without contacting customers. Use it to check headers, attachments, and content. Keep a separate rendering check for the email clients your audience uses, because a sandbox preview cannot reproduce every client’s behavior.

A background queue can prevent a slow sending API from delaying a web request. It also creates another failure boundary: a worker may time out after the provider has accepted the email but before your application records the result.

If the job retries blindly, the recipient may receive two messages. Assign a stable identifier to each logical notification and record the provider’s message identifier when available. Where supported, reuse an idempotency key for retries of the same send operation, following the provider’s documented retention window.

Distinguish retries from intentional resends. A user asking for a new reset link is a different event from a worker repeating an uncertain request. Define which link remains valid when another is issued, and test that behavior explicitly.

Retries also need limits. Temporary service failures may justify another attempt with backoff. A permanently invalid address needs a correction or suppression workflow, rather than repeated sending.

Provider events describe different stages of the sending process. An acceptance response means the provider accepted the request. A delivery event generally means the receiving mail server accepted the message; it does not prove inbox placement or that a person read it.

Capture the events your provider exposes, including bounces, delays, and complaints. Associate them with the original notification so support staff can investigate a particular report. Confirm each event’s documented meaning instead of treating similar labels across providers as interchangeable.

Webhook handlers should verify incoming requests using the provider’s documented mechanism and tolerate duplicate notifications. Avoid assuming events always arrive in chronological order. Record enough history to explain what happened without allowing a late event to overwrite a more informative state.

Before releasing a significant template or infrastructure change, send controlled messages through the production sending configuration to test accounts at commonly used mailbox providers. Use synthetic data, and check placement, rendering, link behavior, and how long the message takes to arrive.

Inspect authentication results in the received headers. Published DNS records are only part of the configuration; the actual message must use the expected signing domain and sending infrastructure. If you change providers or domains, repeat these checks rather than assuming the previous results still apply.

Treat test mailbox results as samples. They can reveal problems, but they cannot guarantee identical placement for every recipient. Mailbox settings, filtering decisions, and recipient history can produce different outcomes.

Provide a resend option with sensible rate limits and explain when users should expect the message. Where appropriate, let signed-in users verify or correct their address. For public account-recovery forms, avoid responses that reveal whether a particular address has an account.

Give support staff a way to distinguish a notification that was never generated from a failed submission or a message accepted by the receiving server. Protect that diagnostic access, limit retention, and avoid logging reset tokens, private message bodies, or unnecessary personal information.

Reliable product email depends on the whole journey, from the triggering event to the recipient’s next action. Testing each stage and making failures observable helps teams resolve problems before users repeatedly click resend or abandon the task.

#10235. Why Transactional Emails Fail After Deployment: A Practical Debugging Guide for Developers

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Transactional emails are easy to overlook during application development. A registration form works, the database stores the user's information, and the backend successfully triggers an email. Everything appears to be functioning as expected.

Then a user reports that their password-reset message never arrived.

The problem might not be in the email template or the sending function. It could involve DNS configuration, authentication failures, message formatting, queue processing, recipient-server policies, or an incorrectly configured production environment.

For developers building SaaS applications, ecommerce platforms, and web services, reliable email delivery is an important part of the user experience. An account-verification message that never arrives can prevent someone from accessing an application, while a delayed security notification can create unnecessary confusion.

Understanding how to investigate these problems requires looking beyond the email-sending function. This guide explores a practical debugging workflow, the technical layers involved, and the tools developers can use to identify common email delivery failures.

One of the first mistakes developers make when troubleshooting email is assuming that a successful API response means the message has reached the recipient.

When an application submits an email through an SMTP server or an email service provider's API, the provider may accept the request and return a successful response. However, the receiving mail server still needs to process the message.

Depending on its configuration and policies, the receiving server might:

Accept the message and place it in the inbox.

Accept the message but classify it as spam.

Reject the message because of authentication or policy issues.

Accept the message but place it in another folder.

A successful request therefore confirms only that a particular stage of the process completed.

Developers should distinguish between three important states:

Submission: The application successfully handed the message to its sending provider.

Acceptance: The receiving mail server accepted responsibility for the message.

Inbox placement: The message became visible in the recipient's expected inbox.

These distinctions make troubleshooting considerably easier. Instead of immediately rewriting an email template, developers can identify which stage is actually failing.

Before investigating external email infrastructure, examine the application's own implementation.

A common example is a password-reset workflow. The application receives a request, generates a temporary token, creates a reset URL, and sends an email containing that URL.

Several problems can occur before the message even reaches an email provider.

The application generates an expired token.

The reset URL points to a staging environment.

An asynchronous task fails before processing the message.

The email service credentials are incorrect.

An API request times out without being retried.

The application records success before confirming the provider's response.

Start by logging the important stages of the process.

For example, a Node.js application might use structured logging:

This example assumes that the email provider returns a message identifier. Adapt the response fields to the actual SDK being used.

Notice that the logs identify the recipient through an internal ID rather than exposing their complete email address.

Avoid logging passwords, authentication tokens, reset URLs, or other sensitive information.

The objective is to establish a traceable record of what happened without introducing additional security risks.

Email authentication is another important area to investigate when messages are rejected or consistently classified as spam.

Three commonly used mechanisms help receiving servers evaluate whether a message is authorized to represent a particular domain.

Sender Policy Framework (SPF) allows a domain owner to specify which servers are authorized to send messages using that domain.

This information is published through DNS.

A simplified SPF record might look like this:

The actual record must reflect the sending services authorized by the domain owner.

Forgetting to authorize a new email provider.

Publishing multiple SPF records for the same domain.

Leaving outdated sending services in the record.

Remember that SPF evaluates the sending infrastructure against the envelope sender domain. It does not automatically guarantee that the visible From address is aligned.

DomainKeys Identified Mail (DKIM) adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages.

The receiving server can retrieve the corresponding public key through DNS and verify that the signature is valid.

A mismatch between the configured selector and published record.

Developers integrating multiple email providers should verify that each provider has the correct signing configuration.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance (DMARC) builds on SPF and DKIM.

It allows domain owners to define how receiving servers should handle messages that fail authentication and alignment requirements.

A basic monitoring-oriented DMARC record might look like this:

This is an illustrative configuration. The reporting address must be valid, and the domain should have an appropriate monitoring and policy-enforcement plan.

Developers can use MXToolbox to inspect DNS records and investigate common authentication problems.

For additional technical background, SitePoint's guide to HTML email development explains why email environments require different implementation considerations from ordinary web pages.

An email template that looks perfect in a browser can behave differently when rendered by Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, or another email client.

This happens because email clients do not implement HTML and CSS in exactly the same way.

A layout may break because of unsupported CSS properties, image restrictions, unexpected font rendering, or differences in how tables are handled.

For this reason, email testing should be part of the development process rather than something performed only after users report a problem.

HTML validation: Check the markup for malformed elements, missing attributes, and unnecessary code.

Rendering tests: Preview the email across desktop and mobile email clients.

Link verification: Confirm that buttons, links, and personalized URLs point to the correct destinations.

Image testing: Check whether the message remains understandable when images are blocked.

Content inspection: Review subject lines, sender information, and the plain-text alternative.

Deliverability checks: Examine authentication, content-related warnings, and potential inbox placement problems.

Developers can also incorporate pre-send diagnostics into their release workflow to identify potential deliverability problems before a message is sent to a larger audience.

The important point is to treat these checks as one part of quality assurance, not as a guarantee that every message will reach the primary inbox.

For more information about email rendering differences, SitePoint's article on essential HTML email coding tips provides useful examples of client-specific behavior.

Testing transactional emails with real customer addresses during development can create unnecessary problems.

A better approach is to use a dedicated testing environment that captures outgoing messages without delivering them to actual recipients.

For example, Mailpit provides a local SMTP server and web interface for inspecting messages during development.

The final output generated by the application.

Another option is Mailtrap, which provides email testing capabilities for development workflows.

Consider a registration feature that sends a welcome message whenever a new user creates an account.

Instead of sending hundreds of test messages to real addresses, developers can configure the development environment to capture them in a testing inbox.

This makes it possible to repeatedly test different templates without affecting actual customers or generating unnecessary email traffic.

A separate testing environment also reduces the risk of accidentally sending development notifications to production users.

Applications that send large numbers of emails often use background workers instead of processing every message during the original HTTP request.

This approach helps keep the application responsive, but it introduces another potential source of failures.

Imagine an ecommerce application that sends order confirmations after checkout.

The application creates an order successfully and places an email task in a queue. However, the background worker becomes unavailable before processing the task.

The customer receives an order confirmation much later than expected, or perhaps not at all.

To investigate these problems, developers should monitor:

Retries are particularly important, but they need to be implemented carefully.

A temporary network failure may justify another attempt. A permanent rejection caused by an invalid recipient address usually does not.

Exponential backoff can help prevent repeated requests from overwhelming an unavailable service.

In a production system, add random jitter to avoid having multiple workers retry simultaneously.

Also consider idempotency. If a worker processes the same task twice, the application should avoid sending duplicate transactional messages whenever possible.

A reliable queue architecture should distinguish between temporary failures and permanent failures rather than retrying every error indefinitely.

Testing before deployment is important, but it cannot reveal every issue that may occur in production.

Real email traffic introduces changing conditions, including recipient-server behavior, sender reputation, invalid addresses, and differences between email providers.

Developers should therefore establish ongoing monitoring.

For Gmail-related monitoring, Google Postmaster Tools provides information about domain reputation, spam rates, and authentication.

It is particularly useful for teams sending enough email to generate meaningful reporting data.

Another useful resource is Can I Email, which documents HTML and CSS feature support across email clients.

These tools address different problems. Monitoring sender reputation will not fix a broken password-reset URL, just as validating HTML will not resolve a DNS authentication failure.

The best approach is to monitor each layer independently.

A repeatable checklist helps developers investigate problems without overlooking important details.

Before releasing a new email feature, review the following:

Confirm that the application successfully submits the message.

Verify that provider credentials and environment variables are correct.

Inspect SPF, DKIM, and DMARC configuration.

Validate the HTML and plain-text versions.

Preview the message on desktop and mobile email clients.

Verify that development and production configurations are separated.

Check delivery events and provider error reports.

Monitor bounce and complaint rates after deployment.

Not every application needs an expensive testing platform or a complicated monitoring stack.

A small project might only require a local email testing environment, basic DNS checks, and structured application logs.

A larger SaaS platform may benefit from automated integration tests, provider webhooks, queue monitoring, authentication reports, and dedicated deliverability testing.

The right setup depends on the application's sending volume, complexity, and the importance of its email workflows.

Reliable email delivery requires more than calling an SMTP function or integrating an email API.

Developers need to understand the complete process, from application logic and message construction to authentication, queue processing, recipient-server acceptance, and inbox placement.

The most effective troubleshooting strategy is to isolate these layers instead of treating every missing message as a template problem.

Start with application logs, inspect provider responses, validate authentication records, test email rendering, and monitor production behavior.

By incorporating these checks into development and deployment workflows, teams can reduce avoidable failures and build applications that communicate more reliably with their users.

#10232. Bringing Visual Graph Debugging to Custom Edge Compilers with Model Explorer

Model Explorer goes beyond high-level model graphs. See how Arm plugs in custom compiler IRs, verifies NPU delegation side-by-side, and maps cycle-level profiling traces directly onto your execution graph.

Machine learning models run locally on a wide range of hardware, from low-power edge devices to gaming graphics and physical AI systems. Running models efficiently on these targets requires compilation steps such as quantization, graph lowering, operator fusion, and hardware delegation.

Arm used Google AI Edge Model Explorer to give developers visibility into these pipelines. With Model Explorer's hierarchical graph visualization and plugin architecture, Arm built several open-source adapters that make edge model transformations easier to inspect.

When deploying a model to edge hardware, a framework change or operator constraint can alter the exported execution graph. Engineers often need to answer three questions:

Answering these questions by reading raw flatbuffers or text logs takes time, especially for large language models (LLMs) and neural networks. Arm needed a visual tool to inspect Arm-specific intermediate representations and hardware profiling traces.

To address these deployment challenges, Arm developed custom adapters for Google AI Edge Model Explorer to support several model formats (i.e., TOSA, VGF, and PTE), establishing a comprehensive visual workflow for on-device optimization. Using these extensions, engineering teams can validate exported pipelines, verify accelerator delegation, and pinpoint cycle-level performance bottlenecks directly on the model graph:

Arm engineers used Model Explorer to validate a PyTorch-trained Neural Super Sampling (NSS) model exported through the ExecuTorch Arm backend as a VGF file for Vulkan ML Extensions. While inspecting the VGF graph, the team found unintended TRANSPOSE operators introduced by a change in the ExecuTorch Arm backend.

Model Explorer enables users to inspect individual nodes of the model graph, such as the rescale operator depicted below, which displays the operator's attributes and SPIR-V tensor connections. This functionality assisted the Arm team in identifying and resolving the regression without requiring manual analysis of raw log files.

To optimize vision models for accelerators like the Arm Ethos-U85 NPU, Arm engineers used Model Explorer's split-pane view to compare CPU and NPU-delegated graphs side-by-side for a Ultralytics YOLO model.

The split-pane view showed the structural difference: the CPU graph displays each individual layer, while the NPU graph groups the network into a single EthosUBackend block between quantize and dequantize nodes. For partially supported models in LiteRT or ExecuTorch, this view helps identify un-delegated operators and split subgraphs.

To find latency bottlenecks on the Ethos-U NPU, Arm ran their model on their Fixed Virtual Platform (FVP) simulator and used Model Explorer's node data plugin to overlay cycle-accurate execution traces onto the graph. Color-coded heatmaps showed that a single TRANSPOSE node was the main latency bottleneck.

You can use Google AI Edge Model Explorer along with Arm's open-source adapters today to inspect and optimize your own on-device models locally via PyPI or GitHub.

#10226. What Actually Happens When a Kafka Region Fails?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most Kafka deployments in the industry plan to survive the loss of a single Kafka broker or an availability zone. But that's not enough for critical Tier-0 infrastructure where downtime or data loss of even a few seconds could have a massive business impact.

In such scenarios, the failure model should consider not just the loss of a broker or a zone, but the loss of an entire region. The business is expected to operate without interruption even when a whole region goes down, and it should be possible to also roll the cluster safely while operating in that state, to apply any break-glass changes.

But what actually happens inside Kafka when an entire region goes down? What happens to the replicas, producer writes, and KRaft quorum? And what happens when that region comes back?

Let's walk through these scenarios using a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka architecture.

For a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster, we need three different regions with isolated failure domains and a minimum of two brokers in each region.

Brokers in each region should be configured with rack awareness to indicate their failure domains as follows:

For Tier-0 workloads, each topic's partitions should be configured to have a replication factor (RF) of 6. Kafka's rack-aware replica assignment will spread the replicas across the three regions. If the automatic replica assignment doesn't allocate two replicas per region, we have to explicitly reassign replicas immediately after topic creation to meet our availability guarantees.

This way, if one region goes down entirely, we still have four replicas surviving and the cluster would still continue to operate.

But just running brokers in multiple regions is not the solution here. In fact, that's the easy part. The hard question is, what guarantee remains when one of those regions disappears, and does it solve the business needs?

The two brokers in that region become unavailable, leaving four surviving replicas across Regions B and C.

For any partitions whose leaders were in Region A, Kafka needs to elect new leaders from the surviving in-sync replicas.

But having four surviving replicas alone does not determine whether Kafka will continue accepting writes.

The next step is to define what is considered a successful write in this Kafka setup. For this, we need two configs:

min.insync.replicas determines the minimum number of replicas that should be present in the in-sync replica set (ISR) of a partition for Kafka to accept writes when producers use acks=all.

acks=all is a producer-side config that ensures that the producer will get an acknowledgment only when the write has been successfully replicated to the required in-sync replicas.

These two settings work together to satisfy the durability guarantees we want.

When all three regions are healthy, ISR would include all six replicas. Let's say one region goes down, then the ISR would drop down to four.

Since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

There may be temporary interruptions while Kafka detects the failure, elects new leaders, and clients reconnect to them. But once the surviving leaders are available, the cluster can continue accepting writes as long as the ISR requirements are met.

The goal isn't availability at any cost, but rather the system should be available as long as the durability guarantees that we designed for can be maintained.

Now let's make the scenario more interesting.

Region A is still down. We have four replicas surviving across Regions B and C.

What if we need to roll the cluster one broker at a time, to apply any break-glass changes?

During that time, the ISR can go down from four to three. But since minISR is three, writes will still continue to work.

Let's look at how the cluster behaves under different failure scenarios.

Kafka will stop accepting writes only when the surviving ISR drops below three.

This is an important distinction for Tier-0 workloads.

It's not sufficient to design a cluster that survives a region failure. We also need to consider whether the cluster can continue operating safely while already in that degraded state.

So far, we focused on the data plane. Now switching gears, the KRaft controllers have a completely different availability model.

They form the metadata quorum, and a majority is needed to continue operation.

For our setup, five controllers could be distributed as 2 + 2 + 1 across the three regions, requiring a majority of three to maintain quorum.

If either of the regions containing two controllers goes down, three controllers remain. If the region containing one controller goes down, four remain.

So, the controller quorum survives the loss of any one region.

But, unlike brokers, we cannot necessarily roll the controllers at that point because taking one controller down could cause the quorum to drop below three.

With a static quorum, one way to solve this is to use more controllers, and a 3 + 3 + 3 distribution would provide this. Losing one region would leave six controllers with room to still roll the controllers, as we only need five to maintain quorum.

On the flip side, an increase in quorum size also increases the coordination overhead. The official Kafka documentation typically recommends three or five controllers, which tolerate one or two concurrent failures respectively.

Alternatively, with dynamic KRaft quorum (KIP-853), introduced in Kafka 3.9, we can add or remove controllers from the voting quorum without restarting the cluster.

For example, with our 2 + 2 + 1 setup, if a region containing two controllers goes down, the remaining three controllers can still maintain quorum. We can then remove the failed controllers from the voting membership, one at a time, reducing the quorum size to three. This allows us to roll one of the surviving controllers while still maintaining the new majority of two.

However, dynamic quorum reconfiguration is not automatic. The surviving controllers must be able to maintain quorum and successfully commit the membership changes before we can proceed with the controller restart.

You need to consider whether you want the same availability guarantee for the controllers as that of the data plane, and whether dynamic quorum reconfiguration during a regional outage is acceptable for your Tier-0 workloads.

So far, we assumed that a region either works or goes down completely.

But the WAN itself becomes a failure mode.

In this setup, a partly working WAN between two regions can be more dangerous than a link that's completely down.

Increased latency, packet loss, or intermittent connectivity issues between regions can cause replication to lag, ultimately resulting in replicas falling out of ISR.

The three regions might be independently healthy, but if the connectivity between them is not, then the availability of the cluster might be impacted.

Going back to the CAP theorem, to tolerate a network partition, we must choose between consistency and availability.

So, if a partition's ISR falls below the configured min.insync.replicas of three due to a network partition, Kafka stops accepting writes with acks=all rather than weakening those guarantees.

Similarly, if the KRaft controller quorum is lost, then no metadata changes, like leader elections, can happen in the cluster, even though existing data-plane traffic may continue for some time.

The availability guarantees we discussed also come with their own costs.

The first cost is client latency. With cross-region replication and acks=all, the network latency between these regions directly affects producer latency. Consumers can also pay the WAN latency cost when consuming from partition leaders located in a different region.

The second cost is replication overhead. With RF=6, there is additional storage, network traffic, and replication-related coordination cost in the brokers compared to a smaller number of replicas.

So, in an architecture like this, monitoring the health of the network paths between these regions is as critical as monitoring the Kafka brokers and controllers.

Metrics like round-trip latency (RTT) between regions, packet loss, TCP retransmissions, connection errors/timeouts, and network throughput/bandwidth utilization are a good start.

When one region goes down, all the partition leaders in that region would move to the two other surviving regions.

This implies that those two regions would have to handle more producer and consumer traffic, network throughput, and disk I/O.

Even if the cluster is designed to satisfy its ISR and controller quorum requirements, if it's not sized to handle these failures when one region goes down, the surviving regions would be overloaded, resulting in higher latencies, timeouts, and operational instability.

So, for Tier-0 infrastructure, capacity planning should always be done for failure states and not stop at normal operations.

Now let's say Region A comes back online.

The brokers in the recovered region might have to catch up on a significant amount of data before they become fully in sync again.

This can result in a sudden burst of cross-region network traffic and disk I/O across all regions.

Region recovery needs to be controlled so that it doesn't overwhelm the healthy part of the cluster.

This can be achieved by throttling the replica catch-up, with the trade-off of longer recovery time to minimize the impact on live production traffic.

Replication throttling needs a deeper discussion of its own, so we won't go into details here.

The important point is that bringing a failed region back online is not simply a matter of restarting its brokers. We need to make sure that the recovery process itself doesn't impact the business.

Before putting a Tier-0 multi-region Kafka cluster into production, validate these scenarios:

There is no shortage of ways to deploy Kafka across multiple regions. The challenge here is to pick the right architecture that addresses all our durability guarantees.

For Tier-0 workloads, this means designing the architecture around failures first.

What happens when a region goes down? Can we still roll the cluster? What continues to work, and at what point do we deliberately stop accepting writes?

Lastly, can we safely bring back the region that was down without impacting the business?

That is ultimately the difference between simply running Kafka across multiple regions versus actually supporting a mission-critical Tier-0 workload.

#10219. Unifying Edge AI: How Coral NPU’s open standard brings portable, energy-viable intelligence from model to silicon

Edge AI complements the cloud to bring the best AI experiences. These rely on a continuous flow of context and intelligence across both, working as a model cascade where information is captured and processed in the right place at the right time. Ultra-low-power models on the edge can continuously sense and respond in real time, while seamlessly handing off to larger on-device or cloud models when deeper reasoning is needed.

Still, building for the edge is much harder than it should be. While cloud infrastructure has converged around a few standard software stacks, the edge is split across hundreds of proprietary chip architectures and incompatible toolchains. For ML developers, this creates a heavy fragmentation tax, where you end up rewriting kernels, re-quantizing models, and debugging vendor-specific compilers for every chip you target.

Last year, Google Research introduced Coral NPU with a simple idea: an open hardware and software standard is the only way to unify the edge and unlock developer productivity and portability. Our standards based, ML-first RISC-V architecture paired with a reliable open-source toolchain gives developers confidence that their models will compile and run, letting teams optimize a model once and deploy it across different SoC vendors, devices, and power envelopes.

Today, we are reaching three major milestones in the Coral effort:

Fixing fragmentation at the edge takes more than publishing an instruction set. Historically, many edge accelerators were designed to hit high peak-compute numbers on spec sheets, leaving compiler teams to figure out software support later. In practice, unsupported neural network layers fall back to a general-purpose CPU, which can hurt latency and drain the battery.

We designed the Coral NPU architecture and its compiler together from the start. Instead of chasing peak metrics, our focus is on energy-viable compute: helping to deliver the right performance at the right power envelope for your application.

Over the past year, we released the first three milestones of the 32-bit Coral NPU design: a C-programmable RISC-V scalar frontend for control flow (Milestone 1), an RVV v1.0 vector co-processor for SIMD operations (Milestone 2), and a tightly coupled memory subsystem with floating point support (Milestone 3). With this month's release of the Matrix Execution Unit (Milestone 4), the 32-bit architecture is complete.

Scalar and vector units are great for general control logic and signal processing, but modern ML workloads—from vision encoders to transformer attention layers—spend most of their time on 2D matrix-matrix (GEMM) and matrix-vector (GEMV) multiplication. Running 2D matrix math on a 1D vector engine forces the chip to repeatedly load weights and activations from memory, which can mean spending more energy moving data than doing math.

Our Matrix Execution Unit leverages the RISC-V VME specification (formally part of the Zvt family) to address this need and uses a dedicated outer-product multiply-accumulate (MAC) engine. Because an outer-product engine computes a full 2D tile of operations in a single cycle (256 MACs per cycle), it reuses weights and activations directly inside local registers. This brings quadratic throughput scaling to the core, configurable for up to 1 TOPS.

Having a dedicated matrix engine in a sub-10 mW envelope changes what developers can build on a small battery. Rather than stopping at simple keyword spotting or basic motion detection, devices can run continuous, multimodal workloads, including compact transformer models like Gemma, real-time voice translation on wearables, spatial audio filtering on hearables, and low-latency visual perception on robots.

To help silicon teams bring this open design straight into commercial production, the complete 32-bit RTL is available today on GitHub.

To make the hardware easy to target without writing custom assembly, we are also releasing the v1.0 foundation of our open-source MLIR/IREE compiler toolchain.

With this stack, you can bring models from JAX, PyTorch, or TensorFlow through standard representations like StableHLO and TOSA. The compiler partitions workloads between the host CPU and the Coral NPU core, handles operator fusion, tiles matrices to fit local SRAM and matrix accumulators, and progressively lowers workloads directly to Coral NPU scalar, RVV v1.0 vector, and VME matrix instructions. Alongside our open-source MPACT simulator and reference FPGA design, hardware and software teams can benchmark models and tune silicon configurations before a chip ever goes to fabrication.

Turning an open standard into commercial silicon takes collaboration across the entire semiconductor value chain. To help silicon partners bring this open design straight into commercial production, the complete 32-bit RTL is available today on GitHub, and we are partnering with VeriSilicon on the design implementation and verification (DV) to ensure turnkey readiness for silicon tape-outs. We are also working with GlobalFoundries on their 12LP+ FinFET process - accelerating core timing by 70%, and reducing standard-cell area by up to 15% across RTL iterations, with ongoing evaluation of 12LP+ ultra low-power libraries for the Matrix Execution Unit.

On the commercial silicon side, our collaboration with Synaptics shows how quickly this open standard translates into real hardware across very different power budgets without splitting the software experience.

At Synaptics Tech Day (October 20, 2026), Synaptics is launching three commercial silicon and hardware products built on Coral NPU technology and supported by the open IREE/MLIR toolchain:

Because both product families build on the same architecture and compiler foundation, ML teams can optimize a model once and deploy it to a milliwatt-class wearable (Coralboard SR) or a 1 TOPS board (Coralboard SL) using the same workflow. Developed in collaboration with hardware and System-on-Module partner Grinn, the Coralboard lineup also gives teams a direct path from devkit prototyping to commercial production hardware.

With our 32-bit architecture and v1.0 compiler stack in place, we are already working on the next phase of the roadmap, including a 64-bit Coral NPU architecture and CHERI support for security.

Whether you are designing a custom edge SoC, working on ML compilers, or deploying models to wearables and robotics, Coral NPU can help give you an open path from model to silicon. Here is where you can get started:

We would like to thank the core contributors and leadership team for this work, particularly Billy Rutledge, Ben Laurie, Derek Chow, Shaked Flur, Alex Van Damme, Bilal Khan, David Gao, Gregory Kielian, Julian Mullings-Black, Khaled Talukder, Lun Dong, Matthew Wilson, Michael Hoang, Murali Vijayaraghavan, Naveen Dodda, Roman Lewkow, Stefan Hall, Tor Jeremiassen, Yenkai Wang, Manya Dasari, Akib Uddin, Divya Pandya, Steve He, Preeti Singh, Anthony Phalen, Hans Rempel, Esmeralda Cardenas, Isha Mishra, Katy Zima, Resham Parikh, Hannah Bayer Rushing, Taylor Montgomery, Kai Yick, Srikanth Muroor, Cindy Liu, Bangfei Pan, Alec Go, Geoff Stirling, Robin Dua, Gaurav Arora, Steve Goldberg, Francesco Visin, Gus Martins, Ian Ballantyne, Kat Black, Ravin Kumar, Tris Warkentin, Avinatan Hassidim, Jay Yagnik, Katherine Chou, Shahram Izadi, and Yossi Matias.

#10217. As Russia's Cyberwar Evolves, Belarus Steps Forward

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

The "FrostyNeighbor" advanced persistent threat (APT) launched a destructive attack in Ukraine and is raising concerns about deeper alignment with Russian cyber objectives.

Researchers have spotted the first destructive cyberattack by a Belarus-aligned threat actor against a critical target in Ukraine, suggesting Belarus may be upping its support for Russian national objectives — carrying implications for much of Europe.

ESET this week published its APT Activity Report for Q2 and Q3 2026. The report highlights a range of state-sponsored activity across Europe, Asia, and the Middle East, but one of the most significant assertions involves Belarus, an Eastern European country that has been close allies with Russia in the latter's invasion of Ukraine.

Historically, Belarusian cyber operations have been tracked separately from those attributed directly to Russian intelligence services, though researchers have long observed overlap in targeting and strategic interests. The Belarusan state-affiliated group FrostyNeighbor — also tracked as UNC1151 — has been known for disinformation and cyber espionage, the latter conducted through various means such as spear phishing and malware.

Related:Russia's Hybrid Cyber-Physical War in Europe Heats Up

While the Eastern European country has also set its sights on Ukrainian and Polish targets in phishing attacks to further its own and possibly Russia's goals, ESET identified a recent escalation in these activities. Mainly, ESET's report notes that its researchers "identified the first destructive cyberattack by a Belarus-aligned threat actor that we have documented in the European theater."

Specifically, FrostyNeighbor deployed Chisel, UltraVNC, Cobalt Strike, and a VBScript wiper via Group Policy against a manufacturing company in Ukraine that ESET said "plays an important role in the country's transportation sector," which is notable because destructive operations up until this point have been explicitly Russia-aligned.

"While some of their targeting interests overlap, notably around the drone sector, our telemetry more clearly points to Belarus-aligned groups acting as cyberproxies or force multipliers for Russia," the report read. "We observed increased and more assertive activity from Belarus-aligned threat actors, with their operations increasingly reflecting Russia’s intelligence priorities in Ukraine in some areas."

Damien Schaeffer, senior malware researcher at ESET, says that the strongest evidence for an increasing operational closeness with Russia is the number of attacks and the victimology that appears closer to and aligned with Russia's strategic priorities than Belarus's own, as well as a sophistication behind these attacks, "which seems relatively high to Belarus's potential interests alone." He says ESET first observed this trend of increasing aggressiveness at the beginning of 2024.

This comes as North Korea expands its own military cooperation with Russia in the latter's war on Ukraine, which ESET added could mean cyber activity will continue to align as well.

Russia's war on Ukraine stretches far beyond Ukraine, especially in the realm of cyber operations. Russia has grown bolder in cyberattacks particularly against European countries that support Ukraine militarily or politically, are on NATO's eastern flank, and support critical logistics hubs for aid flowing into Ukraine.

This, to varying degrees, has included Poland, Estonia, Latvia, Lithuania, Sweden, Czech Republic, and Germany, among others. In other words, if Russian allies are growing bolder in their cyberactivities explicitly supporting Russia, that carries consequences for all of Europe.

Alexander Leslie, a national security expert at Recorded Future, tells Dark Reading that its evidence shows FrostyNeighbor as an actor executing sustained operations and evolving its techniques, though not necessarily an overall increase in operational scale nor shared operational command.

Recorded Future's recommendation is to explicitly track Belarus-linked clusters and correlate their infrastructure, tools, and targeting with Russian threat campaigns "while preserving attribution distinctions."

"Threat models should cover credential theft, persistent access, and the use of compromised accounts to reach additional people. Intelligence collection should include malicious login infrastructure, email exploitation, personal-account targeting, and abuse of trusted contacts, which are the access routes documented in these campaigns," Leslie says. "Defenders do not need proof of Russian tasking to prioritize an actor already targeting them."

While researchers differ on whether Belarusian activity represents deeper operational integration with Russia, they appear to agree that defenders should not dismiss Belarus-linked actors as secondary threats. As Leslie explains, CERT Polska in June 2026 identified UNC1151 as one of the most active APT groups it monitors, "observing new phishing domains almost daily in the preceding weeks."

Organizations in sectors tied to government, transportation, and defense that show support for Ukraine may increasingly encounter campaigns whose objectives align closely with Moscow's broader strategic interests.

He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. #10215. How to Build an Accessible Gold Value Calculator in Vanilla JavaScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Ask anyone selling an old chain or a broken ring what they want to know, and it's one thing: what is this worth? A small calculator can answer that in a second, but most of the ones you find online get the details wrong. They ignore troy ounces, treat 14K as 58.5% one day and 58.3% the next, or show a "value" that no buyer will ever pay.

In this tutorial, we'll build a gold value calculator that gets those details right. It runs on plain HTML and JavaScript, updates as the user types, works with a screen reader, keeps its state in a shareable URL and pulls a live spot price through a small Node.js endpoint. The calculation lives in one pure function, so we can test it in a few lines.

Every gold calculator comes down to three inputs: how much the item weighs, how much of that weight is actually gold, and what pure gold is trading for today.

Purity is measured in karats, out of 24. A 14K ring is 14 parts gold and 10 parts other metals, so it's 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3% gold. You'll also see 14K stamped as "585", which is the hallmark many manufacturers use for the same alloy. For a calculator, the karat fraction is the safer default:

The second detail most tutorials miss is the unit. Gold spot prices are quoted in USD per troy ounce, which is 31.1035 grams, not the 28.35-gram ounce on a kitchen scale. Jewelers also use pennyweight (dwt), which is 1.5552 grams. If your calculator multiplies grams by a per-ounce price without converting, every result is wrong by a factor of 31.

Put together, the formula looks like this:

For example, with gold at $2,400 per troy ounce, 10 grams of 14K gold contains 5.83 grams of pure gold, which is worth $449.85 at melt.

That $449.85 is the value of the gold content alone. Refiners and buyers pay a percentage of it, because refining, assaying and handling cost money. Depending on the buyer, the item and the market, the payout can sit well below melt value.

That gap is why good production tools separate the two numbers. A dedicated scrap jewelry gold calculator, like the one The Alloy Market offers, lets sellers estimate what their gold is worth from its weight and karat before they send anything in. Our calculator does the same job with a payout field: leave it at 100% to see melt value, or enter a buyer's rate to see a realistic offer.

Start with real form controls and a visible

A few choices here matter more than they look:

A little CSS gives touch-friendly controls. Keeping inputs at 16px (1rem) also stops iOS Safari from zooming in when a field gets focus.

The biggest problem with most calculator snippets is that the math is buried inside an event handler, mixed with getElementById calls. You can't test it, and you can't reuse it on a server. Put the calculation and validation in their own module instead:

Both functions take plain values and return plain values. They don't know a DOM exists, which means the same file can run in the browser, in Node.js and in your test runner.

Notice that we don't round inside the calculation. Rounding happens once, at display time. Rounding in the middle of a chain of multiplications is a classic way to end up a few cents off.

Now connect the form to the module. Listening for the input event on the form itself catches changes from every field, including the dropdowns, so there's no need to wire up each control separately.

With the markup, CSS and script in place, here's the finished calculator showing our 10 g, 14K example with an 80% buyer payout:

Asking users to type the spot price works, but prefilling it is friendlier. Don't call a metals price API straight from the browser, though. Your API key would be visible to anyone who opens DevTools, and every visitor would spend one of your API calls.

Instead, put a small endpoint in front of your price provider and cache the result. This example uses Express and the fetch built into Node.js 18 and later:

Three things keep this endpoint dependable:

On the page, the loadSpot() function from Step 3 fills the field and shows the time of the price. The field stays editable, so a seller who has a quote from a specific buyer can type that instead.

A seller comparing offers might want to send their numbers to a family member, or come back to them tomorrow. Because every input has a name, turning the form into a query string is one line with URLSearchParams.

The syncUrl() function in Step 3 runs on every change and uses history.replaceState instead of pushState, so typing doesn't fill the back button with dozens of entries. On load, restoreFromUrl() puts the values back. A link like this opens with the form already filled in:

This is also the progressive enhancement story. If JavaScript fails to load, the form still submits as a GET request. Your server can read the same parameters, import the same gold.js and render the result in HTML.

Because goldValue() is a pure function, testing it needs no browser and no mocks. Node.js ships with a built-in test runner, so there's nothing to install:

Run it with node --test. (Add "type": "module" to your package.json so Node treats the files as ES modules.) The troy ounce test is the one worth keeping forever: if anyone "simplifies" the conversion later, it fails immediately.

A quick checklist for putting this calculator on a real store:

A gold calculator looks like a ten-line script, but the details are what make it useful: converting troy ounces properly, separating melt value from payout, keeping the math testable and making the result accessible and shareable. The same pattern of a pure calculation module, a thin DOM layer and a cached server endpoint works for any calculator where the price depends on several inputs, from shipping estimates to currency conversion.

Mathematically, 14K is 14 ÷ 24 = 58.3%. Many manufacturers stamp 14K jewelry "585" and alloy it to that standard, so both numbers are common. Pick one, state it in the dropdown, and use it consistently.

Let the user choose. Most people weigh jewelry in grams, but spot prices are quoted per troy ounce, so the calculator should accept either and convert internally.

Yes. Drop Step 4 and let users type the spot price, or have a scheduled job write the current price into a static JSON file. Everything else runs entirely in the browser.
